Start here

How to use this pack

Read a topic, then do its labs in a real terminal until the commands come without thinking.

The idea

You already understand the concepts. What's missing is reps: typing the commands yourself, seeing the real errors, and fixing them. Every topic here has a short guide and practice labs on your laptop that grade you the way the quiz checker does. Reading alone won't fix this. Doing will.

The loop for every topic

  1. Read the topic's mental model and command tables (10–15 min). Don't memorise yet.
  2. Do the labs in order: Drill → Quiz-style → Twist. Try first, without looking.
  3. Stuck for 5 minutes? Look at the guide's tables (not the solution). Still stuck? Open SOLUTION.md.
  4. Redo it. Run ./setup.sh again and solve the lab from memory. The second clean run is what counts.
  5. Tick it in the Practice labs list. Your ticks are saved in this browser.

The Lab Practice Tool (quiz-style screen)

This is the easiest way to practise: a browser page laid out like your quiz. You get the task, a file panel to open, create and edit files, a real terminal, and a Check button that shows ✓/✗ for each check. Start it from a terminal:

$ cd Practice-Labs         # the folder you cloned (wherever it lives)
$ ./start-lab-tool.sh
# opens http://127.0.0.1:8765 in your browser. Leave this terminal open; Ctrl+C stops the tool.
  1. Pick a lab on the left. Its work/ folder is built automatically the first time.
  2. Read the task. Edit files in the Files panel, like the quiz: click a file to open it, + New file creates one (e.g. migrations/03_add_x.sql), Ctrl+S saves. Type only commands (git, docker, flyway, pytest…) in the terminal.
  3. Press ✓ Check. Passed labs get a ✓ in the list.
  4. Press ↺ Reset lab to start that lab over, then solve it again from memory.
Save before running git

The terminal only sees what's saved to disk. After editing a file in the panel, press Ctrl+S before git add, exactly like the quiz's file panel. When a command changes a file (e.g. git merge writes conflict markers), the panel reloads it automatically.

Running a lab from your own terminal

You can also do every lab in a normal terminal, editing files in any editor. Every lab is a folder with four files:

TASK.md

The situation and the task, worded like the quiz. Read it first.

setup.sh

Builds a fresh work/ folder with the scenario. Run it again any time to start over.

check.sh

Grades your work/ folder with ✓ / ✗ per check, like the quiz's "What was checked".

SOLUTION.md

The exact commands with a why for each, and common wrong attempts. Open it only after trying.
$ cd labs/01-shell/01-files-and-folders   # from the Practice-Labs folder
$ cat TASK.md            # read the task
$ ./setup.sh             # builds work/ fresh
$ cd work                # you solve the task in here
#   ... your commands ...
$ ../check.sh            # grade yourself (works from work/ or from the lab folder)
$ cd .. && ./setup.sh    # start over and do it again from memory
Re-run setup from the lab folder, not from inside work/

setup.sh deletes and rebuilds work/. If your terminal is inside work/ when that happens, it's left in a deleted folder and commands act strangely. Always do cd .. first, then ./setup.sh, then cd work again.

Docker labs leave their container running

After a Docker lab passes, its container keeps holding its port (8080, 8081…). Before starting the next Docker lab, go back to the finished lab and run ./setup.sh to clear it, or run docker rm -f <name>. Docker must be running for Topic 4 and Topic 6.

One-time setup (do this first)

# 1. Make plain "git pull" merge when branches have diverged (the quiz assumes it does).
#    Without this, git 2.43 stops with "Need to specify how to reconcile divergent branches".
$ git config --global pull.rebase false

# 2. Optional: use nano instead of vim when git opens an editor
$ git config --global core.editor nano

# 3. Topic 6 in your own terminal only (the Lab Practice Tool does this for you).
#    Run from the Practice-Labs folder, once per terminal:
$ export PATH="$PWD/labs/bin:$PATH"
#    ...or permanently (also from the Practice-Labs folder; $PWD is saved as the full path):
$ echo "export PATH=\"$PWD/labs/bin:\$PATH\"" >> ~/.bashrc
Why hashes don't match the quiz

A commit's hash depends on its time and author, so every setup.sh run produces new hashes. Read them from git log --oneline or git reflog. Never copy them from the task text.

Terminal survival kit

Key / commandWhat it does
TabAutocompletes file names and commands. Press it constantly: it saves typing and prevents typos in paths.
↑ / ↓Brings back earlier commands so you can fix and rerun them.
Ctrl+CStops whatever is running and gives you the prompt back.
qQuits a pager. git log, git diff and man open one; you'll see a : or (END) at the bottom.
clear or Ctrl+LClears the screen.
pwdPrints where you are. Check this whenever a path "doesn't exist".
cmd --helpShows a command's options. The quiz allows this, so use it.
cat filePrints a file so you can read it in the terminal. To change a file, use the Files panel instead.

Stuck inside an editor? (the merge-commit trap)

Commands like git commit (with no -m) or git merge can open a text editor inside the terminal, so you can type a message. If you don't know how to leave it, you're stuck. Learn both editors' exit keys:

vim (screen with ~ lines)

Save and quit: press Esc, type :wq, press Enter.
Quit without saving: Esc :q! Enter.

nano (shortcuts listed at the bottom)

Save: Ctrl+O then Enter.
Exit: Ctrl+X.
Avoid the editor completely

Give the message on the command line: git commit -m "message", or for a merge git commit --no-edit / git merge branch --no-edit.

Your 2-day plan

Day 1

Topic 1 Shell (all labs) → Topic 2 Git basics → Topic 3 Git remotes & recovery.
Evening: redo every Quiz-style lab from memory.

Day 2

Topic 4 Docker → Topic 5 Testing → Topic 6 Migrations.
Evening: do every Twist, then read the Quiz-day traps page.
Why the Twists matter

Your professor won't repeat the same questions. The twists use the same commands in combinations you haven't seen. If you can solve those, you can handle a new quiz question.

Topic 1

Shell basics: files, grep, pipes and redirects

Move around, create/move/copy/delete files, and filter log files into report files with grep, | , > and >> without guessing.

Mental model

You are always standing in one folder (pwd). Every path you type is either relative to that folder or starts from /. Commands are small tools that read lines of text and write lines of text to the screen. | plugs one tool's output into the next tool's input. > and >> send the final output into a file instead of the screen. The shell sets up all the plumbing (opens files, expands *) before any command runs, which explains most of the surprises below.

Where am I? Moving around

The quiz terminal starts in the task's folder. Before anything else, find out where you are and what is there. A path without a leading / is relative: it starts from the current folder.

CommandWhat it does / why
pwdprint working directory: the folder you are standing in
ls / ls -lalist files. -l long format (size, date, d = folder), -a also hidden files (names starting with .)
ls -R logslist a folder and everything below it: great for checking your final state
cd logsgo into logs
cd ..go up one folder. .. = parent, . = this folder
cd ~ or cdgo to your home folder
cd -go back to the folder you were in before
cmd --helpshort manual for a command (the quiz allows this). Pipe it: grep --help | less
$ pwd
/home/student/task
$ ls -la
drwxr-xr-x 2 student student 4096 Sep 28 09:00 logs
# the leading d means "directory" (folder)
$ cd logs && pwd
/home/student/task/logs
$ cd ..
Keyboard tips that save marks

Tab completes file names: type logs/acc + Tab. If it doesn't complete, the file isn't there (typo or wrong folder). Up-arrow brings back your last command, so you can fix it or add | ... instead of retyping. Ctrl+C stops a command that hangs (for example grep waiting for input because you forgot the file name). q quits less and --help | less pages.

Looking inside files

CommandWhat it does / why
cat fprint the whole file. cat a b prints a then b (concatenate)
less fscroll a long file (arrows, Space, /word to search, q to quit)
head -n 3 ffirst 3 lines (default 10)
tail -n 3 flast 3 lines: newest entries of a log
wc -l fcount lines. Prints 8 f (number and name)
wc -l < fprints just 8: with < the shell feeds the data in, so wc never sees the name
Tip

Always cat the input files first. Most traps (a 503ms time, a DEBUG line that mentions ERROR, a user=71) are visible in two seconds.

Create, copy, move, delete

CommandWhat it does / why
mkdir reportsmake a folder. Fails if the parent folder is missing
mkdir -p logs/archive/2026-10-p = make every missing parent too, and no error if it already exists
touch a.txtcreate an empty file (or just update its time if it exists)
cp a.txt b.txtcopy. Order is always SOURCE then DEST. Original stays
cp -r src/ backup/-r (recursive) is needed to copy a folder
mv old.txt new.txtDEST is not a folder -> rename
mv a.log logs/archive/DEST is an existing folder -> move into it, name kept
mv a.log b.log logs/archive/several sources: the last argument must be a folder
rm a.txt / rm -r folderdelete. Folders need -r. There is no recycle bin
ls incoming/*2026-10-24** = any text. The shell swaps it for matching names before the command runs
mv logs/access.log.1 logs/archive/
  1. move (or rename)
  2. source: the file to move
  3. destination; the trailing / says "this must be a folder", so a typo gives an error instead of a silent rename
Trap: mv into a folder that doesn't exist

mv logs/access.log.1 logs/archive when logs/archive is missing does not error. It renames the log to a file called archive. Always mkdir first, and write the destination with a trailing /: then you get Not a directory instead of a silent mistake.

Trap: rm -r is forever

Read the line before Enter. rm -r tmp / (stray space) tries to delete the whole disk. Use ls with the same pattern first to see what would be hit.

echo, quotes, >, >>, < and |

Every command writes to the screen ("standard output"). The shell can redirect that stream:

SymbolMeaning
cmd > fwrite output to file f. Creates f, or wipes it first if it exists
cmd >> fadd output to the end of f (creates it if missing). Keeps what was there
cmd < ffeed file f as the input
cmd1 | cmd2pipe: cmd1's output becomes cmd2's input. No file in between
echo "text"just prints its arguments + a newline. Useful to save a value a command worked out, e.g. echo $? > code.txt (see exit codes below)
$ head -n 2 visitors.txt > some.txt    # first 2 lines into a new file
$ tail -n 1 visitors.txt >> some.txt   # >> adds the last line at the end
$ cat some.txt
maya
liam
ben
$ tail -n 1 visitors.txt > some.txt    # > starts from empty again
$ cat some.txt
ben
Terminal or editor?

Rule of thumb: content produced by a command (grep, sort, wc, ls ...) is saved with > / >> in the terminal. Text you type yourself (a note, a config line, a new file) goes in the editor: in the Files panel open the file, or click + New file and type a path like notes/todo.txt (folders are created), then save with Ctrl+S. The quiz portal works the same way.

Quoting. The shell splits your line on spaces and treats | > < * $ as special. Put patterns in single quotes ('method=(GET|DELETE)') so the shell passes them to grep untouched. Double quotes also protect spaces and | but still expand $var.

Why "redirect into a missing folder" fails

> can create a file, never a folder. The shell opens reports/orders-500.txt before grep starts; if reports doesn't exist you get bash: reports/orders-500.txt: No such file or directory and grep never runs. Fix: mkdir reports (or mkdir -p) first.

Trap: never redirect into the file you are reading

sort visitors.txt > visitors.txt or grep -v x app.log > app.log leaves the file empty: the shell wipes the > target before the command reads it. Write to a new file. (In a "don't change the log" task this loses all marks.)

grep: keep only matching lines

grep PATTERN FILE... prints every line of the file(s) that contains PATTERN. It never changes the file. With no file name it reads its input (the pipe, or the keyboard, which looks like a hang: Ctrl+C).

grep -h 'status=500' logs/access.log.1 logs/access.log > reports/x.txt
  1. the filter command
  2. option: hide file names
  3. the pattern, quoted, as exact as possible
  4. files, read in the order you list them
  5. save the result instead of printing it
OptionEffect / when you need it
-iignore case: ERROR, error, Error all match
-vinvert: keep lines that do not match ("everything except health checks")
-cprint only the count of matching lines
-nput the line number in front: 3:2026-...
-hno filename: prefix when searching several files
-Hthe opposite: always show the file name
-wwhole word only: -w 'user=7' skips user=71
-Eextended regex: | (or), ( ), {4,}, + work
-Ffixed string: dots and stars are normal characters (grep -F '1.2.3')
-rsearch every file in a folder and below: grep -r 'TODO' src/
-oprint only the matched part, not the whole line
-lprint only the names of files that match
$ grep 'status=500' logs/access.log.1 logs/access.log
logs/access.log.1:2026-09-27T10:02:17 service=orders method=POST status=500 3021ms
logs/access.log.1:2026-09-27T10:05:40 service=users method=GET status=500 88ms
logs/access.log:2026-09-28T09:03:29 service=orders method=POST status=500 2987ms
logs/access.log:2026-09-28T09:06:48 service=orders method=GET status=500 3104ms
# two or more files -> grep adds "file:" in front. -h removes it.
$ grep -c 'status=500' logs/access.log
2
Trap: the pattern is a substring

grep 500 also matches 3500ms and user=500. grep error misses ERROR. Match the whole field: 'status=500', 'level=ERROR', -i when case varies, -w for whole words.

AND, OR and regex basics

AND = two greps in a pipe. The first reads the file; the second has no file, so it filters what comes down the pipe. OR = one grep -E 'a|b'.

$ grep 'service=orders' logs/access.log | grep 'status=500'
2026-09-28T09:03:29 service=orders method=POST status=500 2987ms
2026-09-28T09:06:48 service=orders method=GET status=500 3104ms
$ grep -E 'level=(WARN|ERROR)' logs/app.log      # WARN or ERROR
Trap: the Quiz 1 hint grep P1 file1 | grep P2 file2 > file3

If the second grep is given a file, it reads that file and ignores the pipe completely. So grep service=orders access.log.1 | grep status=500 access.log just prints today's status=500 lines (including other services) and yesterday is lost. In an AND chain only the first grep gets file names. To combine two files, either list both in the first grep (grep -h P1 f1 f2 | grep P2) or run one chain per file with > then >>.

Regex pieceMeansExample
.any one character'status=5..' = any 5xx
.*anything (zero or more chars)'service=orders.*status=500' = AND in one grep, if the order is fixed
^start of line'^2026-09-27' = lines from that day
$end of line'ms$'
[0-9]one digit'status=5[0-9][0-9]'
{4,} (needs -E)4 or more of the previous thing-E '[0-9]{4,}ms' = 1000ms or more
a|b (needs -E)a or b-E 'GET|DELETE'
Trap: forgetting -E or the quotes

grep 'GET|DELETE' (no -E) finds nothing: in basic grep | is a plain character. grep -E GET|DELETE f (no quotes): the shell makes a pipe, prints DELETE: command not found, and grep waits for keyboard input. Ctrl+C.

sort, uniq, cut, find

CommandWhat it does / why
sort fsort lines A-Z. Timestamps like 2026-09-27T10:02 sort into time order
sort -n / sort -rnsort as numbers (so 10 > 9) / biggest first
sort -usort and drop duplicates
uniqdrop repeated lines, but only neighbours, so sort first
uniq -ccount each group: 3 service=orders
cut -d' ' -f2split each line on spaces, keep field 2 (default delimiter is TAB)
find . -name '*.log'find files by name in this folder and below (quote the pattern)
$ grep 'status=5' logs/access.log | cut -d' ' -f2 | sort | uniq -c
      3 service=orders
      2 service=payments
      1 service=users
# build it one | at a time: up-arrow, add the next stage, Enter, look

Quiz 1 Q18 step by step (order matters)

Task: put every service=orders + status=500 line from yesterday's logs/access.log.1 then today's logs/access.log into reports/orders-500.txt, then move access.log.1 into a new logs/archive.

$ mkdir reports                                  # 1. > can't create folders
$ grep 'service=orders' logs/access.log.1 | grep 'status=500' > reports/orders-500.txt    # 2. yesterday, >
$ grep 'service=orders' logs/access.log | grep 'status=500' >> reports/orders-500.txt    # 3. today, >>
$ cat reports/orders-500.txt
2026-09-27T10:02:17 service=orders method=POST status=500 3021ms
2026-09-28T09:03:29 service=orders method=POST status=500 2987ms
2026-09-28T09:06:48 service=orders method=GET status=500 3104ms
$ mkdir logs/archive                             # 4. mv won't create it
$ mv logs/access.log.1 logs/archive/              # 5. grep FIRST, move LAST
$ ls -R logs
Why this order

Grep before mv: once the log is moved, logs/access.log.1 no longer exists and grep says No such file or directory. > for the first write (start clean), >> for the second (keep yesterday on top). One-liner alternative: grep -h 'service=orders' logs/access.log.1 logs/access.log | grep 'status=500' > reports/orders-500.txt.

Common errors and what they mean

bash: reports/orders-500.txt: No such file or directory

Means: a folder in the path doesn't exist (here reports). Same for grep: logs/x.log: No such file or directory: wrong name, wrong folder, or you already moved it.

Fix: ls, pwd, use Tab completion; mkdir -p the folder.

rm: cannot remove 'tmp': Is a directory
grep: logs: Is a directory

Means: you gave a folder to a command that wants a file.

Fix: rm -r tmp; for grep name the files (logs/*.log) or use grep -r.

mv: cannot move 'a.log' to 'logs/archive/': Not a directory

Means: the destination folder doesn't exist (the trailing / protected you from a silent rename).

Fix: mkdir -p logs/archive then repeat (up-arrow).

mkdir: cannot create directory 'project/src/utils': No such file or directory

Means: parent folders are missing.

Fix: mkdir -p project/src/utils.

bash: ./run.sh: Permission denied

Means: the file isn't executable, or you can't write to that place.

Fix: bash run.sh or chmod +x run.sh; write inside your own folders.

bash: DELETE: command not found
bash: gerp: command not found

Means: a typo, or an unquoted | split your pattern into a second "command".

Fix: check spelling; put patterns in single quotes.

(nothing happens, no prompt)

Means: the command is waiting for input: usually grep/cat/sort with no file name and no pipe.

Fix: Ctrl+C, then add the file name.

Worked scenarios

Drill Last 2 lines of a log into a file

logs/app.log exists. Create tail.txt with only its last 2 lines.

Show answer
$ tail -n 2 logs/app.log > tail.txt

tail -n N = last N lines; > saves them.

Drill Count today's failed requests

Write only the number of status=500 lines in logs/access.log into count.txt.

Show answer
$ grep -c 'status=500' logs/access.log > count.txt
# also fine: grep 'status=500' logs/access.log | wc -l > count.txt

Don't use wc -l file: it adds the file name.

Quiz-style Everything except health checks, from two logs, then archive

logs/access.log.1 (yesterday) and logs/access.log (today). Create reports/no-health.txt with every line that is not service=health, yesterday first. Then move access.log.1 into a new logs/old/.

Show answer
$ mkdir -p reports logs/old
$ grep -hv 'service=health' logs/access.log.1 logs/access.log > reports/no-health.txt
$ mv logs/access.log.1 logs/old/

-v inverts, -h drops file names (options can be joined: -hv). Files in the order listed = yesterday first. Grep before mv.

Quiz-style POST or PUT failures of one service

From logs/access.log, save every service=users request whose method is POST or PUT and whose status is any 5xx into reports/users-writes-5xx.txt.

Show answer
$ mkdir -p reports
$ grep 'service=users' logs/access.log | grep -E 'method=(POST|PUT)' | grep 'status=5' > reports/users-writes-5xx.txt

AND = more pipes; OR = -E 'a|b'; status=5 matches 500-599 but not a 500ms time.

Quiz-style Back up a folder and add a note

Copy the whole config folder to config-backup, then add the line backup done to the end of CHANGELOG.txt without losing its lines.

Show answer
$ cp -r config config-backup
# Files panel: open CHANGELOG.txt, add a new last line, save with Ctrl+S:
#   backup done
$ tail -n 2 CHANGELOG.txt     # check: the old last line, then "backup done"

Folders need cp -r (else -r not specified; omitting directory). The note is text you type, so it goes in the editor; keep the old lines and only add one at the end.

Twist Which users hit the API most?

logs/access.log lines contain user=NAME somewhere. Create reports/top-users.txt: the 3 users with the most requests, biggest first, as COUNT user=NAME.

Show answer
$ grep -o 'user=[a-z0-9]*' logs/access.log | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -n 3 > reports/top-users.txt

-o prints just the matching part (works even if the field position varies, unlike cut). sort -> uniq -c counts -> sort -rn biggest first -> head keeps 3.

Twist Clean up temp files everywhere

A build folder has .tmp files hidden in many sub-folders. List their paths, sorted, into tmp-list.txt, then delete them (only them).

Show answer
$ find build -name '*.tmp' | sort > tmp-list.txt
$ cat tmp-list.txt                 # look before deleting
$ find build -name '*.tmp' -delete

Quote '*.tmp' so find (not the shell) does the matching in every sub-folder. rm build/*.tmp would only reach the top level.

Quiz-style Validate AI code: does the function exist?

A generated pull request calls send_invoice(...). In the repo folder src (many sub-folders), save every line that mentions send_invoice as path:line:text, sorted, into reports/invoice.txt. Then save just the number of files under src that contain TODO into reports/todo-files.txt.

Show answer
$ mkdir -p reports
$ grep -rn 'send_invoice' src | sort > reports/invoice.txt
$ grep -rl 'TODO' src | wc -l > reports/todo-files.txt
$ grep -r 'def send_invoice' src        # no output = it is never defined: reject the PR

-r walks every sub-folder, -n adds line numbers, -l prints each matching file once (so wc -l counts files, not lines). sort because -r's order isn't guaranteed. Trap: grep -rc TODO src prints one count per file (src/a.py:2...), not one number.

Twist Keep only the errors of a build, log a failure

./build.sh prints progress on stdout and problems on stderr, and exits non-zero when it fails. Run it so that the screen stays quiet, the progress is thrown away, and only the problems go into build-errors.txt. In the same command line, append <today> build failed to build.log only if the build failed (date from date +%F).

Show answer
$ chmod +x build.sh                 # only if you get "Permission denied"
$ ./build.sh > /dev/null 2> build-errors.txt || echo "$(date +%F) build failed" >> build.log
$ echo $?                          # exit code of the LAST command on that line

> is stream 1 (stdout), 2> is stream 2 (stderr), /dev/null throws output away. a || b runs b only when a failed (exit code not 0); a && b only when it succeeded. $(date +%F) is replaced by e.g. 2026-10-28, but only inside double quotes. Both streams into one file: > all.txt 2>&1 (2>&1 after the file).

Twist Sum and maximum from a CSV with a header

payments.csv starts with the header id,amount,currency, then lines like 17,250,GBP. Save the total amount of the GBP payments into gbp-total.txt, and the whole line of the single biggest payment (any currency) into biggest.txt.

Show answer
$ awk -F, '$3 == "GBP" { s += $2 } END { print s }' payments.csv > gbp-total.txt
$ tail -n +2 payments.csv | sort -t, -k2 -rn | head -n 1 > biggest.txt

awk -F, splits on commas, $3 == "GBP" compares the whole field (a grep for GBP could also hit an id or note), END prints once at the end. tail -n +2 skips the header; sort -t, -k2 -rn = split on commas, sort by field 2, as numbers, biggest first. Without awk: grep ',GBP$' payments.csv | cut -d, -f2 | paste -sd+ | bc.

Quiz-style Count, record and delete month-old logs

Under srv (many sub-folders) find every .log file not modified for more than 30 days. Save their paths, sorted, into old.txt and see how many there are on screen, in one command. Then delete exactly those files.

Show answer
$ find srv -type f -name '*.log' -mtime +30 | sort | tee old.txt | wc -l
3
$ cat old.txt                                         # read the list before deleting
$ find srv -type f -name '*.log' -mtime +30 -delete

tee FILE writes its input to the file and passes it on down the pipe (a T-junction), so you save the list and count it at once. -mtime +30 = older than 30 days (-30 = newer), -size +1M works the same way for size. -type f skips folders named like logs. Moving instead of deleting: -exec mv {} archive/ \;.

Practice labs

Cheat sheet

  • pwd / ls -la / ls -R dir where am I, what's here, final-state check
  • cd dir cd .. cd - cd ~ move around
  • cat f head -n 3 f tail -n 3 f less f (q quits) look inside
  • mkdir -p a/b/c make folders incl. parents
  • cp src dest / cp -r dir dir2 copy (source first)
  • mv old new rename; mv f dir/ move into existing folder
  • rm f / rm -r dir delete for good
  • cmd > f overwrite/create; cmd >> f append; neither creates folders
  • a | b a's output into b
  • grep 'pat' f; -i case, -v invert, -c count, -n line no., -w word
  • grep -h 'pat' f1 f2 several files, in that order, no name prefix
  • grep 'A' f | grep 'B' AND (second grep gets NO file)
  • grep -E 'A|B' f OR (quote it)
  • ^ start, $ end, . any char, .* anything, [0-9]{4,} 4+ digits (-E)
  • sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -n 1 count and rank
  • cut -d' ' -f2 field 2 of space-separated lines
  • wc -l < f just the line count
  • find . -name '*.log' find files by name
  • find d -type f -name '*.log' -size +100k -mtime +14 files only, bigger than 100 KiB, older than 14 days; add -exec mv {} archive/ \; to act
  • grep -rn 'x' dir | sort search a whole tree; -l file names only
  • cmd > out 2> err split streams; cmd > all 2>&1 both (2>&1 last)
  • echo $? last exit code; a && b || c b on success, c on failure; "$(cmd)" insert output
  • Order: mkdir -> grep (> then >>) -> cat to check -> mv last
Topic 2

Git basics: local work, branches, merging

Move changes between the working directory, staging area and local repo on purpose, make branches, merge them, and fix a conflict without panicking.

Mental model

Your work lives in four places: the Working Directory (files on disk), the Staging Area (what the next commit will contain), the Local Repository (saved commits) and the Remote (the team's copy). git add copies working dir to staging, git commit copies staging to the local repo, git push sends it to the remote. Every commit is a snapshot plus a pointer to its parent. A branch is only a label that points at one commit and moves forward when you commit. git status compares the first three places, never the remote.

git status: read every section

Run git status before and after every command. Each heading tells you where a file is.

$ git status
On branch main                                  # the branch HEAD points at
Changes to be committed:                        # STAGING differs from LAST COMMIT
  (use "git restore --staged <file>..." to unstage)
        modified:   config.py
        new file:   routes/webhooks.py
Changes not staged for commit:                  # WORKING DIR differs from STAGING
  (use "git add <file>..." to update what will be committed)
  (use "git restore <file>..." to discard changes in working directory)
        modified:   config.py
        deleted:    old.txt
Untracked files:                                # git has never seen these
        debug.log
You seeMeans
nothing to commit, working tree cleanworking dir = staging = last commit. Says nothing about the remote.
same file under "to be committed" AND "not staged"you added it, then edited it again. The commit would take the staged version only.
You have unmerged paths. / both modified:a merge is in progress with a conflict. Fix the file, git add it, then commit.
All conflicts fixed but you are still merging.everything is resolved; run git commit to finish.
Your branch is ahead of 'origin/main' by 1 commitremote topic: you have commits not pushed yet.
Tip

git status -s is the short form: two columns, left = staging, right = working dir. M staged, M unstaged, MM both, ?? untracked, UU conflict.

add and commit: working dir to staging to repo

A commit takes exactly what is staged, not everything you touched. That is why you choose with git add.

CommandWhat it does
git add file.pystage one file (new or modified) as it is right now
git add .stage everything in this folder and below: new, modified, deleted
git add -Astage everything in the whole repo, including deletions
git commit -m "Msg"save the staging area as a new commit on the current branch
git commit -am "Msg"stage all modified/deleted tracked files, then commit. Skips untracked files!
git commit (no -m)opens an editor for the message (see the editor trap below)
git commit -a -m "Add webhook route"
  1. save staging as a snapshot
  2. -a = first stage every tracked file that changed (new files are NOT included)
  3. -m = the message, so no editor opens
Trap

You create routes/webhooks.py and run git commit -am "...". The new file is not in the commit: -a only knows files git already tracks. Run git add routes/webhooks.py first.

Commit hygiene

One commit = one change. Subject in the imperative, like a command: "Add webhook route", "Fix session timeout", not "updates" or "fixed stuff". Quiz checks compare the message exactly, so copy capitals and spaces.

nothing added to commit but untracked files present (use "git add" to track)

Means: the staging area is empty. Maybe you edited but did not save, or did not git add.

Fix: save the file, git add it, check with git status.

diff vs diff --staged

Two different comparisons. Remember which two locations each one looks at.

CommandCompares
git diffworking dir vs staging = what git add would still add
git diff --staged (same as --cached)staging vs last commit = what git commit would save
git diff HEADworking dir vs last commit (both together)
git diff main featuredifference between two branch tips
# last commit: TIMEOUT = 30, staged: 60, on disk: 6000
$ git diff --staged
-TIMEOUT = 30
+TIMEOUT = 60
$ git diff
-TIMEOUT = 60
+TIMEOUT = 6000

Lines with - are the old side, + the new side. Press q to leave the pager.

restore: undo before you commit

CommandCopies
git restore filestaging → working dirthrow away UNSTAGED edits (gone for good)
git restore --staged filelast commit → stagingunstage; your edit stays on disk
git restore --source=HEAD --staged --worktree filelast commit → bothback to the committed version completely
Trap (Quiz 1 Q12)

Staged 60, typo 6000 on disk, want to keep 60: git restore config.py. Not --staged (that unstages 60 and keeps the typo), not git reset --hard (loses 60 too), not git add (stages the typo).

log --oneline --graph --all --decorate: read the graph

git log --oneline --graph --all --decorate
  1. show history
  2. one line per commit: short hash + subject
  3. draw the lines between commits
  4. every branch, not only the current one
  5. show labels: (HEAD -> main), (feature)
$ git log --oneline --graph --all --decorate
*   9e1f0a2 (HEAD -> main) Merge branch 'feature'   # merge commit: 2 lines go into it
|\
| * f03224a (feature) Use friendlier greeting     # on feature only
* | 4a13d04 Welcome returning users               # on main only
|/
* 0fdc766 Add greet                               # common ancestor (base)
  • Newest is on top. Each * is a commit. Lines go down to the parent.
  • HEAD -> main = you are on main. A label in brackets without HEAD is another branch.
  • |/ is where a branch split off. |\ under a commit means it has two parents (a merge).
  • Commits "on main" = everything reachable going down from main, following both parents of a merge. Above: main has 4, feature has 2.
Counting
git rev-list --count mainnumber of commits reachable from main (what quiz checks use)
git log --oneline main | wc -lsame number: one line per commit, wc -l counts lines
git log --oneline main..featurecommits on feature that main does not have
git show feature:greet.pyprint the file as it is at the tip of feature, without switching
git log -1 --format=%sjust the subject of the last commit

branch, switch, checkout -b

A branch is a pointer to one commit. Creating one is instant; no files are copied. Switching rewrites your working dir to match that branch.

CommandWhat it does
git branchlist local branches; * marks the current one
git branch fixcreate fix here, but STAY on the current branch
git switch fixmove to an existing branch
git switch -c fixcreate + switch (modern)
git checkout -b fixcreate + switch (old form, same result)
git switch -c fix maincreate from main even if you are somewhere else
git branch --show-currentprint only the current branch name
git branch -d fixdelete a merged branch (only if the task says so)
Trap

git branch fix-greeting then edit and commit: the commit lands on main, because git branch does not switch. Use switch -c, and check git status line 1 ("On branch ...") before committing.

error: Your local changes to the following files would be overwritten by checkout:
        notes.txt

Means: you have uncommitted edits to a file that differs on the other branch.

Fix: commit them, or git stash, switch, and later git stash pop.

fatal: invalid reference: fix

Means: git switch fix but the branch does not exist. Fix: git switch -c fix.

merge: fast-forward or merge commit?

git merge X brings branch X into the branch you are on. So: switch to the receiving branch first (usually main), then name the other one.

Graph beforeResult
main has no new commits since feature branched (main is below feature on one line)Fast-forward: main label just slides up to feature. No new commit, no editor, can't conflict.
both branches have new commits (the graph forks |/)Merge commit: a new commit with two parents. Adds 1 to the count. Opens the editor unless you pass --no-edit or -m.
both forks changed the same lines of a fileConflict: git stops halfway and asks you.
$ git merge feature
Updating 3f9a1c2..8b41d07
Fast-forward                                  # no new commit
$ git merge feature --no-edit
Merge made by the 'ort' strategy.             # new merge commit, 2 parents
Predict the count

After a merge commit, main's count = all commits on main + feature's commits that main lacked + 1. Quiz 1 Q17: base + main's 1 + feature's 1 + merge = 4. Check with git rev-list --parents -n1 main: a merge commit prints 3 hashes (itself + 2 parents).

Already up to date.

Means: the named branch is already inside the current one. Usually you are on the wrong branch (e.g. on feature merging main, or on main merging main). Check git status.

merge conflicts: markers, resolve, finish

Git compares both sides with their common ancestor. If only one side changed a region, it takes that side. If both changed the same region, it has two valid edits and no rule to choose, so it writes both into the file and stops.

$ git merge feature
CONFLICT (content): Merge conflict in greet.py
Automatic merge failed; fix conflicts and then commit the result.
$ cat greet.py
def greet(name):
<<<<<<< HEAD
    return f"Hello, {name}! Welcome back."      # OURS: the branch you are on (main)
=======
    return f"Hi there, {name}."                 # THEIRS: the branch being merged in
>>>>>>> feature

The steps, always the same:

  1. Open the file in the Files panel (it shows the markers). Delete the 3 marker lines and write the final text the task asks for. Save with Ctrl+S.
  2. Check no markers are left: grep -n '<<<<<<<\|=======\|>>>>>>>' greet.py prints nothing.
  3. git add greet.py: "add" here means mark as resolved.
  4. git commit --no-edit (keeps git's "Merge branch 'feature'" message) or git commit -m "...".
CommandUse
git merge --abortgive up: back to exactly before git merge
git checkout --ours f / --theirs ftake one side's whole file (then git add f)
git diff --checkwarns about leftover conflict markers
git status"Unmerged paths" lists what is still unresolved
error: Committing is not possible because you have unmerged files.
hint: Fix them up in the work tree, and then use 'git add/rm <file>'

Means: at least one conflicted file was not git added. Fix: git status, resolve and add each "both modified" file.

error: Merging is not possible because you have unmerged files.
fatal: You have not concluded your merge (MERGE_HEAD exists).

Means: you ran git merge / pull again while the old merge is unfinished. Fix: finish it (add + commit) or git merge --abort.

Trap

Git does NOT stop you from committing a file that still contains =======. If you git add with markers inside, they are committed and the content check fails. Look at the file before adding.

Tip

Files that only one side changed, or a new file from the other branch, are already staged during the conflict ("Changes to be committed: new file"). Leave them alone.

The editor trap: commit without -m

You change files in the Files panel editor. But git commit without -m, and a merge that makes a merge commit, open a different text editor, inside the terminal, for the commit message. On this machine the default is vim (no core.editor is set). The message is already filled in; you only need to save and quit.

EditorHow to recognise itSave + quit
vimscreen full of ~ lines, no menupress Esc, type :wq, Enter
nanomenu at the bottom: ^O Write Out ^X ExitCtrl+O, Enter, Ctrl+X
$ git config --global core.editor nano     # make git use nano from now on
$ git commit --no-edit                      # during a merge: no editor at all
$ git merge feature --no-edit               # merge commit with default message, no editor
Trap

In vim, typing letters before pressing i runs commands, not text. If the screen looks wrong, press Esc a few times and type :wq. Lines starting with # in the message are comments and are dropped. To abort the commit from vim use :cq; the merge then stays in progress and you finish later with git commit --no-edit.

git mv, git rm, rm --cached, .gitignore

CommandWhat it does
git mv a.py b.pyrename on disk + stage the rename
git rm old.txtdelete on disk + stage the deletion
git rm --cached .envstop tracking, keep the file on disk
git rm -r --cached folder/same for a folder
.gitignore (make it with + New file, one pattern per line, e.g. *.log)future git add skips matching untracked files

Plain mv/rm also work, but then git sees a deletion and a new untracked file, and you must git add -A to stage both.

Trap

.gitignore only affects untracked files. If .env was already committed, adding it to .gitignore does nothing; you need git rm --cached .env as well. Patterns: *.log any .log file, node_modules/ a folder, .env one file. The file name must be exactly .gitignore (with the dot, no .txt).

stash: a drawer for unfinished work

CommandWhat it does
git stashsave tracked uncommitted changes (staged + unstaged) and clean the working tree
git stash -ualso stash untracked files
git stash listsee what is in the drawer
git stash popre-apply the latest stash and remove it from the list
git stash applyre-apply but keep it in the list (then git stash drop)
error: Your local changes to the following files would be overwritten by merge:
        notes.txt
Please commit your changes or stash them before you merge.

Means: the merge needs to change a file you have edited but not committed. Fix: git stash, merge, git stash pop.

Undo a commit (the short version)

SituationCommand
made one commit too many, keep its changesgit reset --soft HEAD~1 (commit gone, changes stay staged)
committed on main, should be on a new branchgit branch newname then git reset --hard HEAD~N then git switch newname (clean tree only)
wrong commit message on the last commitgit commit --amend -m "Right message"

HEAD~1 = the parent of the current commit, HEAD~2 = two back. The full story of reset modes, reflog, detached HEAD, rebase and remotes (push / fetch / pull) is in the git history topic.

Drill Which commits go where?

You edited a.py, b.py and created c.py. You run git add a.py then git commit -am "Fix a". Which files are in the commit?

Show answer

a.py and b.py. -a stages every tracked modified file (a.py, b.py). c.py is untracked, so it stays out. If the task wanted one change per commit, run git commit -m without -a.

Drill Predict the merge 
* 7c1 (feature) Add tests
* 5b2 Add sub()
* 3a9 (HEAD -> main) Add add()

You run git merge feature. New commit or not? How many commits on main afterwards?

Show answer

Fast-forward: main is directly below feature. main just moves to 7c1. No new commit, main has 3 commits, no editor opens.

Quiz-style Quiz 1 Q16: fix-greeting branch

On main with one commit "Add greet" (return "Hello " + name). Create and switch to fix-greeting, change the line to return f"Hello, {name}!", commit "Use f-string in greeting", stay on fix-greeting.

Show answer
$ git switch -c fix-greeting
# Files panel: open greet.py, make line 2  return f"Hello, {name}!"  then Ctrl+S
$ git diff             # check: only the return line
$ git add greet.py
$ git commit -m "Use f-string in greeting"
$ git status           # On branch fix-greeting, clean

Create the branch FIRST, so the commit lands on it. Checked: branch exists, current branch, file content at the branch, tip message.

Quiz-style Quiz 1 Q17: resolve the conflict, main has 4 commits

main and feature both changed greet.py's return line after "Add greet". Merge feature into main, make the line return f"Hi there, {name}! Welcome back.", finish the merge with git's own message.

Show answer
$ git merge feature                 # CONFLICT in greet.py
# Files panel: open greet.py, remove the 3 marker lines and both old lines,
#   write  return f"Hi there, {name}! Welcome back."  then Ctrl+S
$ git add greet.py                  # mark resolved
$ git commit --no-edit              # or: git commit, then :wq in vim
$ git rev-list --count main
4

4 = Add greet + Welcome returning users + Use friendlier greeting + the merge commit. Don't delete feature, make no other commits.

Quiz-style Quiz 1 Q12: keep the staged 60, drop the 6000

Committed TIMEOUT = 30, staged 60, working dir has a typo 6000. Throw away only the typo.

Show answer
$ git restore config.py

Copies the staged version (60) over the working file. Staging is untouched, so 60 stays staged.

Quiz-style Merge blocked by your own edit

You have an unfinished, uncommitted edit in notes.txt. git merge feature says "Your local changes ... would be overwritten by merge". Merge now, keep your edit uncommitted afterwards.

Show answer
$ git stash
$ git merge feature --no-edit
$ git stash pop
$ git status        # modified: notes.txt (unstaged), stash list empty
Twist Two commits on the wrong branch

You made "Add login form" and "Validate login fields" on main. They belong on a new branch login; main must go back to "Add greet". Nothing pushed.

Show answer
$ git branch login          # label the work first
$ git reset --hard HEAD~2   # move main back 2 (clean tree only)
$ git switch login

Branches are labels: you are only moving labels, the commits stay. Doing the reset before git branch leaves the commits without a label (recover with git branch login ORIG_HEAD).

Twist Conflict in two files, keep the other side's whole file

Merge reports conflicts in greet.py and config.py. The task: config.py = feature's version, greet.py = a combination you type.

Show answer
$ git checkout --theirs config.py   # theirs = branch being merged in
# Files panel: open greet.py, hand-combine the line, remove the markers, Ctrl+S
$ git add config.py greet.py
$ git status                        # All conflicts fixed but you are still merging.
$ git commit --no-edit
Quiz-style Typo in the last commit, a forgotten file, and a private edit staged

Your last commit is "Add setings loader" (typo) and is missing settings.json (untracked). todo.txt has a private note that is staged. Nothing pushed. Fix the last commit (message "Add settings loader", include settings.json) without adding a commit, and keep the todo.txt edit out of it but still on disk.

Show answer
$ git restore --staged todo.txt          # FIRST: unstage, edit stays on disk
$ git add settings.json
$ git commit --amend -m "Add settings loader"
$ git status                             # only: modified todo.txt (not staged)

--amend rebuilds the last commit from whatever is staged NOW, so unstage todo.txt before amending. Without -m, amend opens the editor. git restore todo.txt (no --staged) would be wrong: it copies the staged private version over your file.

Quiz-style Copy one fix from feature to main, then tag it 
* a87 (feature) Document power()
* f84 Fix divide by zero
* e41 Add power()
| * 57c (HEAD -> main) Explain how to run
|/
* 4c1 (tag: v1.0.0) Add calc

Get only "Fix divide by zero" onto main (no merge, power() must not come), then add an annotated tag v1.0.1 with message "Hotfix: divide by zero". How many commits does main have afterwards?

Show answer
$ git log feature --oneline | grep divide
f84 Fix divide by zero
$ git cherry-pick f84
$ git tag -a v1.0.1 -m "Hotfix: divide by zero"
$ git cat-file -t v1.0.1                  # tag  (annotated = its own object)

3 commits (Add calc, Explain how to run, the copy). The copy gets a NEW hash because its parent is different. git cherry-pick feature would copy feature's tip ("Document power()"), not the fix. git tag v1.0.1 without -a is a lightweight tag.

Twist Someone left the wrong merge half-done

git status says "You have unmerged paths", both modified: app.py. A teammate started git merge experiment, but experiment must never reach main. You need to merge feature, where both sides added a different last line to requirements.txt and both lines must stay.

Show answer
$ git merge --abort                       # back to main's tip, no merge in progress
$ git merge feature                       # CONFLICT in requirements.txt
# Files panel: open requirements.txt, delete only the 3 marker lines, keep both lines, Ctrl+S
$ git add requirements.txt
$ git commit --no-edit

Running git merge feature first fails ("Merging is not possible because you have unmerged files"). Fixing app.py and committing would finish the experiment merge. "Keep both" means removing <<<<<<<, ======= and >>>>>>> and keeping the text from both halves (HEAD's half first).

Twist Hotfix an old tagged release + read the objects

main is 2 commits past the annotated tag v1.0. Make a fix commit on top of v1.0 only, keep it on a branch v1-hotfix, and return to main. Then answer: what is the type of v1.0? Which tree does your new commit point to? Two files in main's tree have the same content. How do you show that git stores them once?

Show answer
$ git switch --detach v1.0                # or git checkout v1.0 -> detached HEAD
# Files panel: open config.py, make it  RETRIES = 3 , Ctrl+S
$ git commit -am "Fix retries for 1.0"
$ git switch -c v1-hotfix                 # give the detached commit a name NOW
$ git switch main
$ git cat-file -t v1.0                    # tag
$ git cat-file -p v1-hotfix               # first line: tree <hash>
$ git cat-file -p main^{tree}             # same blob hash on two lines = one stored object

If you switch back to main before creating the branch, no branch points to the commit (git prints its hash so you can run git branch v1-hotfix <hash>). Blobs are named by a hash of their content, so files with the same bytes share one blob. The names are stored in the tree.

Practice labs

Cheat sheet

  • git status where is each file? run it after every step
  • git add f / git add . / git add -A working dir → staging
  • git commit -m "Imperative msg" staging → local repo
  • git commit -am "msg" add tracked changes + commit (never untracked files)
  • git diff unstaged; git diff --staged staged
  • git restore f drop unstaged edits; git restore --staged f unstage
  • git log --oneline --graph --all --decorate see all branches
  • git rev-list --count main count commits on main
  • git show branch:file file content at a branch
  • git switch -c name (= git checkout -b name) create + switch
  • git branch name create only; git branch list
  • git merge feature bring feature INTO the current branch
  • git merge feature --no-edit merge commit without the editor
  • conflict: open the file in the Files panel, remove markers, Ctrl+S → git add f → git commit --no-edit
  • git merge --abort undo a merge in progress
  • vim: Esc :wq Enter; nano: Ctrl+O Enter Ctrl+X; git config --global core.editor nano
  • git rm --cached f untrack but keep; git mv a b rename
  • git stash / git stash pop park and restore unfinished work
  • git reset --soft HEAD~1 undo the last commit, keep changes staged
  • git commit --amend -m "msg" replace the last commit with what is staged now (unpushed only)
  • git cherry-pick <hash> copy one commit onto the current branch (new hash)
  • git tag -a v1.0.1 -m "msg" annotated tag on HEAD (no -m = editor)
  • git cat-file -t X / -p X type / content of an object; X^{tree} = a commit's tree
Topic 3

Git remotes, history and recovery

Push, fetch and pull with confidence, fix "rejected" and "diverged", find lost commits with the reflog, and rewrite or undo history (rebase, cherry-pick, reset, revert) without losing anyone's work.

Mental model

Four locations: working directory → staging area → local repository → remote repository. git status only compares the first three, plus your last downloaded copy of the remote.

A commit is a snapshot + the hash of its parent. Its id is a hash of all of that, so changing anything (even just the parent) makes a new commit with a new id.

A branch is a pointer: a tiny file holding one hash. origin/main is also a pointer, your local note of "where main was on origin last time I talked to it". HEAD says which branch (or bare commit) you're on.

Almost every command here just moves pointers or adds commits. Commits that nothing points to aren't gone yet: the reflog still remembers them.

Remotes: origin is just a name

A remote is just another repository (GitHub hosts the same format you have locally). origin is not special: it's the nickname git gives the first remote (on git clone), or the one you add yourself. The remote only knows what you've pushed.

In the labs, the "GitHub" is a bare repository remote.git in the same folder: a repository with no working directory (just what's normally inside .git). You can't edit files in it; you only push to it and fetch from it. Make one with git init --bare remote.git.

git remote add origin remote.git
  1. register a new remote
  2. the nickname you'll type later
  3. its URL or path (on GitHub: https://github.com/you/repo.git)
CommandWhat it does
git remote -vlist remotes with their URLs (fetch + push lines)
git remote add origin URLadd a remote called origin
git remote set-url origin URLfix a wrong URL
git remote remove originforget a remote (deletes nothing on the server)
git clone URL [folder]copy a repo; origin is set up automatically
git branch -a / git branch -rshow local + remote-tracking branches (remotes/origin/main)
git -C remote.git log --oneline mainrun git inside another folder (here: see what the remote has). -C = "act as if started in"
$ git remote -v
origin	remote.git (fetch)
origin	remote.git (push)
error: remote origin already exists.

Means: you ran git remote add origin twice.

Fix: git remote set-url origin NEWURL if the URL was wrong; otherwise nothing to do.

push: move the remote's pointer to match yours

git push sends the commits the remote is missing, then asks it to move its branch pointer to your commit. The remote only agrees if that is a fast-forward: its current commit must already be in your history (you're just ahead). Otherwise someone's commits would be dropped, so it refuses.

git push -u origin main
  1. upload commits + move the remote's branch
  2. --set-upstream: remember origin/main as main's partner, so later plain git push / git pull work and status shows ahead/behind
  3. which remote
  4. which local branch (it becomes main on the remote)
$ git push -u origin main
To remote.git
 * [new branch]      main -> main
branch 'main' set up to track 'origin/main'.
$ git push                       # later: no arguments needed
   f141ba5..246fe06  main -> main   # old..new remote position
Tip

git push origin main pushes only main, whatever branch you are on. Plain git push pushes the branch you're on to its upstream. If a task says "don't push branch X", use the explicit form and never git push --all.

 ! [rejected]        main -> main (fetch first)
hint: Updates were rejected because the remote contains work that you do not
hint: have locally. ...

Means: someone pushed commits you don't have, and you haven't even fetched them yet.

Fix: integrate first (git pull --no-rebase or git pull --rebase), then push again.

 ! [rejected]        main -> main (non-fast-forward)
hint: Updates were rejected because the tip of your current branch is behind
hint: its remote counterpart.

Means: same problem, but you've fetched already: origin/main has commits your main lacks (behind or diverged).

Fix: git merge origin/main (or git rebase origin/main), then push. Never --force on a shared branch.

fatal: The current branch main has no upstream branch.

Means: plain git push doesn't know where to push.

Fix: git push -u origin main once. (fatal: No configured push destination = there's no remote at all: git remote add first.)

fetch vs pull: look vs look-and-merge

CommandWhat changes
git fetchdownloads new commits into the local repository and moves origin/main. Your main, staging area and files are untouched.
git pullgit fetch + git merge origin/main into your current branch: main moves and your files on disk change.
git pull --no-rebasepull that merges (makes a merge commit if diverged)
git pull --rebasefetch + replay your unpushed commits on top of origin/main (linear history)
git log --oneline main..origin/maincommits origin has that you don't (read A..B as "in B, not in A")
git log --oneline origin/main..mainyour commits not pushed yet
$ git fetch
From remote
   6016bbe..a567bf9  main       -> origin/main     # origin/main moved, nothing else
$ git status
Your branch is behind 'origin/main' by 2 commits, and can be fast-forwarded.
$ git pull
Updating 6016bbe..a567bf9
Fast-forward                                     # main just slid forward, no merge commit
 config.py | 1 +
Trap

git status never talks to the network. "Your branch is up to date with 'origin/main'" means "up to date with what I downloaded last time". Run git fetch first if you want the truth.

hint: You have divergent branches and need to specify how to reconcile them.
...
fatal: Need to specify how to reconcile divergent branches.

Means: newer git (2.27+) won't guess merge vs rebase when both sides have new commits. Nothing was changed (the fetch part did happen).

Fix: git pull --no-rebase (merge, as in quiz 1 Q11) or git pull --rebase. To set it once: git config pull.rebase false.

Ahead, behind, diverged: what to do

git status saysPictureDo
Your branch is ahead of 'origin/main' by 1 commit.you have commits origin lacksgit push
Your branch is behind 'origin/main' by 2 commits, and can be fast-forwarded.origin has commits you lackgit pull (or git merge origin/main)
Your branch and 'origin/main' have diverged, and have 1 and 1 different commits eachboth sides have something newgit pull --no-rebase then git push; or git fetch + git merge origin/main + git push; or git pull --rebase + git push
$ git log --graph --oneline --all --decorate
* 0c4b258 (HEAD -> main) Log each request
| * 1894d77 (origin/main) Add README
|/
* aec22f7 Add app
$ git pull --no-rebase --no-edit
Merge made by the 'ort' strategy.
$ git push
   5064391..bfa5af5  main -> main
Why not the "quick fixes"

git push --force makes origin's main = your main, so the teammate's commit vanishes from origin. git reset --hard origin/main moves your main back to origin's and overwrites your files, so your commit is thrown away. Only merging (or rebasing) keeps both. That's the whole of quiz 1 Q11.

Detached HEAD: on a commit, not a branch

Normally HEAD points at a branch (cat .git/HEAD → ref: refs/heads/main) and committing moves that branch. git checkout <hash> (or git switch --detach <hash>) makes HEAD hold the hash itself. You can look around and even commit, but the new commits are pointed to only by HEAD. Switch away and nothing points at them anymore.

$ git checkout 21e158d
Note: switching to '21e158d'.

You are in 'detached HEAD' state. You can look around, make experimental
changes and commit them, and you can discard any commits you make in this
state without impacting any branches by switching back to a branch.
...
$ git commit -m "Try x"
[detached HEAD eb596e0] Try x
$ git switch main
Warning: you are leaving 1 commit behind, not connected to
any of your branches:

  eb596e0 Try x

If you want to keep it by creating a new branch, this may be a good time
to do so with:

 git branch <new-branch-name> eb596e0
Tip

Still detached and want to keep the work? git switch -c new-name right there. Already left? Use the reflog (next section). git status shows HEAD detached at 21e158d while you're detached.

reflog: find lost commits, put a branch on them

git reflog is a local diary of every place HEAD has been: each commit, checkout, merge, rebase, reset. Newest first. git log follows parent links from branches, so it can't see commits no branch reaches; the reflog can (entries are kept ~90 days).

$ git reflog
0f9523b HEAD@{0}: checkout: moving from bc59506... to main
bc59506 HEAD@{1}: commit: Add rate limit config        # the lost commit
e8bf956 HEAD@{2}: checkout: moving from main to e8bf956  # went detached here
0f9523b HEAD@{3}: commit: Add README
e8bf956 HEAD@{4}: commit (initial): Add request handler
$ git reflog | grep "rate limit"
bc59506 HEAD@{1}: commit: Add rate limit config
bc59506 HEAD@{1}: commit: Add rate limit config
  1. the commit HEAD was on (short hash: use this)
  2. "where HEAD was 1 move ago"; also usable as a name: git branch x HEAD@{1}
  3. what moved HEAD + the commit message
CommandWhat it does
git branch rate-limit bc59506create a branch pointing at that commit. You stay where you are.
git switch -c rate-limit bc59506create it AND switch to it (git checkout -b name hash is the old form)
git branch -f name hashmove an existing branch to a commit
git branch -D namedelete a branch even if unmerged (prints Deleted branch x (was 6c4f777).; that hash is your way back)
git log --oneline --allcommits reachable from any branch/tag (still not the lost ones)
Trap

The branch must exist with that name, pointing at that commit. git merge bc59506 or git cherry-pick bc59506 skips the branch (quiz check "rate-limit is the commit ..." fails), and cherry-pick even makes a copy with a new hash, so the original isn't "merged".

rebase: replay your commits on a new base

git rebase main (while on feature) takes the commits that are on feature but not on main, and re-creates them one by one on top of main. Then it moves feature to the last new copy. Each copy has a new parent, so each gets a new hash. main doesn't move. The old commits stay in the repository (and the reflog) until garbage collection.

# before                              # after: git switch feature ; git rebase main
* d032d80 (feature) Strip whitespace     * 728ce94 (HEAD -> feature) Strip whitespace in notes
* 1dbf664 Add notes                      * 484ae4b Add notes
| * 0f708d1 (main) Update changelog      * 0f708d1 (main) Update changelog
|/                                       * 47b8a42 Add README
* 47b8a42 Add README
git switch feature ; git rebase main
  1. be on the branch you want to MOVE
  2. replay my commits...
  3. ...on top of this (the new base). Can be origin/main too

Conflicts during a rebase. Git stops at the commit that conflicts. Fix it like a merge conflict, then continue:

$ git pull --rebase
CONFLICT (content): Merge conflict in routes.txt
error: could not apply 68f0b35... Add search endpoint
$ cat routes.txt
GET /orders
<<<<<<< HEAD                 # during rebase HEAD = the base you replay ONTO (the teammate's side)
GET /health
=======
GET /search
>>>>>>> 68f0b35 (Add search endpoint)   # your commit being replayed
# Files panel: open routes.txt, keep what's needed, delete the 3 marker lines, Ctrl+S
$ git add routes.txt
$ git rebase --continue          # opens a terminal editor with the message: vim :wq Enter
Successfully rebased and updated refs/heads/main.
CommandWhat it does
git rebase --continueafter git add-ing the fixed files: make this commit and go on
git rebase --abortpanic button: back to exactly before the rebase
git rebase --skipdrop the commit that conflicts (rarely what you want)
git merge --abortsame panic button for a conflicted merge
Way to combineHistory afterwardsUse when
git merge feature (on main)original commits kept + a merge commit with 2 parents (or fast-forward if main had nothing new)always safe, also on shared branches
git rebase main (on feature)linear; your commits get new hashes; no merge commitonly on commits nobody else has (unpushed, or your own branch)
git merge --squash feature + git commitall of feature's changes as ONE new commit on main; feature's commits aren't in main's historytidy main for a messy feature (GitHub's "Squash and merge")
Trap

Direction matters. git rebase main moves the branch you're on. On main, git rebase feature would move main instead. Check with git branch first. And don't rebase commits that are already pushed and pulled by others: their copies would no longer match.

cherry-pick: copy one commit here

git cherry-pick <hash> takes the change that commit made (its diff against its parent) and applies it as a new commit on your current branch. New parent → new hash. The original commit and its branch stay as they are. Typical use: a hotfix made on one branch that main needs right now.

$ git switch main
$ git log --oneline --all | grep "Fix timeout"
9a1b2c3 Fix timeout
$ git cherry-pick 9a1b2c3
[main 5d6e7f8] Fix timeout                     # same message, different hash

Conflicts work the same: fix, git add, git cherry-pick --continue (or --abort).

reset (soft / mixed / hard) and revert

git reset <commit> moves your current branch pointer to that commit. The mode decides which of the 3 local locations are also reset to match it:

CommandLocal repo (branch)Staging areaWorking directory
git reset --soft HEAD~1moves backkept (changes staged)kept
git reset HEAD~1 (= --mixed)moves backreset (changes unstaged)kept
git reset --hard HEAD~1moves backresetreset: edits are lost

HEAD~1 = parent of HEAD, HEAD~2 = grandparent. The "removed" commits are not deleted: git reflog + git reset --hard <hash> brings them back. Uncommitted edits wiped by --hard are truly gone.

$ git reset --soft HEAD~1          # "uncommit", keep everything staged
$ git restore --staged debug.log   # unstage the file that shouldn't be in it
$ git commit -m "Add discount"      # recommit, fixed

git revert <hash> is the opposite approach: it adds a new commit that undoes that commit's change. History only grows, so it's the right tool for commits that are already pushed.

$ git revert --no-edit 0866799
[main 632cb80] Revert "Disable auth check"
$ git push                          # fast-forward, teammates just pull
Why

Reset on a pushed commit moves your main behind origin's: the push is rejected, and forcing it rewrites history everyone else already has. Rule: not pushed → reset is fine. Pushed → revert.

Trap

git revert HEAD undoes the latest commit, which may not be the bad one. Look up the hash with git log --oneline | grep. Without --no-edit, revert opens an editor for the message.

Internals you can poke from the terminal

Everything in git is an object named by the hash of its content (blob = file content, tree = directory listing, commit = tree + parent + author + message, tag). Branches and HEAD are plain text files.

CommandWhat it shows
cat .git/HEADref: refs/heads/main (on a branch) or a bare hash (detached)
cat .git/refs/heads/mainthe 40-char hash main points at. That's the whole branch.
git rev-parse main / HEAD / HEAD~1 / origin/mainturn any name into its full hash (works even when refs are "packed")
git rev-parse --abbrev-ref HEADcurrent branch name
git cat-file -t HASHtype of object: commit / tree / blob / tag
git cat-file -p HASHpretty-print it (commit: tree + parent lines; tree: file list; blob: content)
echo 'test content' | git hash-object --stdinthe hash git would give that content (same content → same hash, anywhere)
git rev-list --count mainnumber of commits reachable from main (what "main has 4 commits" checks)
git merge-base --is-ancestor A Bexit 0 if A is in B's history ("A is merged into B")
$ cat .git/HEAD
ref: refs/heads/main
$ cat .git/refs/heads/main
c7636ff5a2559b91b1b5f789625a30bd334ea922
$ git cat-file -t HEAD
commit
$ git cat-file -p HEAD
tree f4b354863caa9cea99b95422c9dab70465757d87
parent 21e158d954167dbdf7556ed32baccd74875ee538
author S <s@x> 1793032842 +0530
committer S <s@x> 1793032842 +0530

Add b
$ git cat-file -p 'HEAD^{tree}'
100644 blob 78981922613b2afb6025042ff6bd878ac1994e85	a.txt
100644 blob 61780798228d17af2d34fce4cfbdf35556832472	b.txt
$ echo 'test content' | git hash-object --stdin
d670460b4b4aece5915caf5c68d12f560a9fe3e4
Tip

The parent line is why rebase and cherry-pick change hashes: same change, different parent → different commit content → different hash. Run git cat-file -p on a merge commit: it has two parent lines.

Secrets in history

Deleting a file makes a new snapshot without it. Every older snapshot still has it, in every clone. So a committed .env is leaked even after "Remove env file".

CommandWhat it does
git log --all --oneline -- .envevery commit (any branch) that touched .env. -- = "what follows are paths"
git log --all --full-history -- .envthe lecture's proof: no output = never committed anywhere
git log --all --oneline --diff-filter=A -- .envonly the commit that Added it (D = deleted, M = modified)
git show HASHmessage + full diff of that commit
git show HASH:.envthe file as it was in that commit (folder untouched)
git log -p -S "DB_PASSWORD" --allcommits where that string was added/removed
git check-ignore -v .envwhich .gitignore line ignores it (no output = not ignored)
$ git log --all --oneline -- .env
cfc90e8 Remove env file
677777a Add config loader
$ git show 677777a:.env
DB_PASSWORD=Summer2024!
# Files panel: + New file -> .gitignore, type the line  .env  then Ctrl+S
$ git add .gitignore && git commit -m "Ignore .env"
Why rotate

Once pushed, assume it's copied (forks, clones, scanners find it in minutes). The fix that works is rotate: change the password where it's used so the leaked one is dead. Rewriting history afterwards is clean-up, not a fix. Prevent it with .gitignore + a committed .env.example. Note: .gitignore doesn't affect files git already tracks (untrack with git rm --cached .env).

Drill Push a brand-new repo

You have commits on main and an empty repository at remote.git. No remote is set. Connect it as origin and push so that later a plain git push works.

Show answer
$ git remote add origin remote.git
$ git push -u origin main

-u stores origin/main as main's upstream; from then on git push/git pull need no arguments.

Drill Which locations changed?

A teammate pushed 2 commits. You run git fetch. Which of the four locations changed? Then you run git pull. Which now?

Show answer

After fetch: only the local repository (new objects + origin/main moved). main, staging and working directory are untouched; status says "behind by 2". After pull: the merge moves main and updates the working directory (and staging matches it).

Quiz-style Lost detached commit (quiz 1 Q19)

main: "Add request handler" (pushed), "Add README" (not pushed). Earlier you committed "Add rate limit config" on a detached HEAD at the first commit and switched back. Make branch rate-limit at that commit, merge it into main, push main. Don't push rate-limit. Expected: main has 4 commits.

Show answer
$ git reflog | grep "rate limit"
bc59506 HEAD@{1}: commit: Add rate limit config
$ git branch rate-limit bc59506
$ git merge --no-edit rate-limit
$ git push origin main

4 = request handler, README, rate limit config, merge commit. Without --no-edit an editor opens: save and close.

Quiz-style Push rejected, diverged (quiz 1 Q11)

status: "have diverged, and have 1 and 1 different commits each". Which get your commit onto origin and keep the teammate's? (a) git push --force (b) git pull then git push (c) git fetch, git merge origin/main, git push (d) git reset --hard origin/main, git push.

Show answer

(b) and (c). Force erases the teammate's commit on origin; reset --hard throws yours away. On a real terminal (b) may need git pull --no-rebase because of the "divergent branches" message.

Quiz-style After a rebase (quiz 2 Q1)

main: README → "Update changelog". feature: README → "Add notes" → "Strip whitespace in notes". You run git switch feature, git rebase main. True or false: (a) the two feature commits get new ids (b) feature's tip contains CHANGELOG.md (c) main now points at feature's tip (d) the old commits are deleted immediately.

Show answer

(a) true: new parent → new hash. (b) true: they're replayed on top of "Update changelog". (c) false: rebase moves only the branch you're on. (d) false: they stay (reflog) until garbage collection.

Quiz-style Bad commit already pushed

"Disable auth check" was pushed 2 commits ago; teammates pulled. Undo just that change and push, keeping the later commit.

Show answer
$ git log --oneline | grep "Disable auth"
0866799 Disable auth check
$ git revert --no-edit 0866799
$ git push

Revert adds a new commit, so it's a normal fast-forward push. reset --hard HEAD~2 would also delete the later commit and need a force push.

Twist Unseen: you committed on the wrong branch

You made commit "Fix typo" on main (not pushed), but it belongs on docs (which exists). Get it onto docs and remove it from main.

Show answer
$ git log --oneline -1          # note the hash, e.g. 4f5e6d7
$ git switch docs
$ git cherry-pick 4f5e6d7       # copy it onto docs (new hash)
$ git switch main
$ git reset --hard HEAD~1       # OK: not pushed, and the work now lives on docs

Cherry-pick copies; reset removes it from main. If it had been pushed, use git revert on main instead.

Twist Unseen: undo a "reset --hard" mistake

You ran git reset --hard HEAD~2 by accident and two commits vanished from git log. Get main back to where it was.

Show answer
$ git reflog
1a2b3c4 HEAD@{0}: reset: moving to HEAD~2
9f8e7d6 HEAD@{1}: commit: Add totals      # where main was before
$ git reset --hard 9f8e7d6            # or: git reset --hard HEAD@{1}

Commits are never deleted by reset; only the pointer moved. The reflog shows the old position. (Uncommitted edits lost by the first --hard can't be recovered this way.)

Quiz-style Hard: read ahead/behind, then the two-dot logs

After git fetch, status says: "Your branch and 'origin/main' have diverged, and have 2 and 1 different commits each". (a) Which command lists the commits you would be pushing? (b) Which lists the commits you are missing? (c) How many lines does each print? (d) Team rule: linear history. Which two commands get your work onto origin?

Show answer
$ git log --oneline origin/main..main   # (a) yours, not on origin: 2 lines
$ git log --oneline main..origin/main   # (b) theirs, not in main: 1 line
$ git rebase origin/main                 # (d) or: git pull --rebase
$ git push

A..B = "reachable from B, not from A". The first number in "2 and 1" is always your side. After the rebase status says "ahead by 2", and the push is a fast-forward.

Quiz-style Hard: undo a whole merged feature that's pushed

main: "Add app" → "Add README" → Merge branch 'discount' (brought discount.py + a change to app.py) → "Add logging config". All pushed, teammates pulled. Take the whole discount feature out with one new commit. What happens with a plain git revert <merge>, and what's the right command? What does the wrong parent number do?

Show answer
$ git revert 2ceabd1
error: commit 2ceabd1... is a merge but no -m option was given.
$ git revert -m 1 --no-edit 2ceabd1    # parent 1 = main side = keep it
$ git push

A merge has two parents; -m 1 says "main line is parent 1, undo what parent 2 (the feature) brought in". -m 2 does the opposite: keeps discount and removes "Add README". Reverting the two feature commits one by one also works but makes 2 commits. To bring the feature back later: revert the revert (merging the branch again does nothing).

Twist Unseen: the rebase stops on a conflict

You run git rebase origin/main with 3 local commits. It prints CONFLICT (content): Merge conflict in routes.txt and "Could not apply b2c3d4e... Add search". (a) Which branch are you on now? (b) What are your two ways forward? (c) Inside the conflict markers, which side is HEAD?

Show answer
$ git status
interactive rebase in progress; onto 9f8e7d6
You are currently rebasing branch 'main' on '9f8e7d6'.
# Files panel: open routes.txt, remove <<<<<<< ======= >>>>>>>, keep both lines, Ctrl+S
$ git add routes.txt
$ git rebase --continue           # a terminal editor opens for the message: vim :wq Enter
# or give up completely:
$ git rebase --abort              # main back exactly as before the rebase

(a) No branch: HEAD is detached while the rebase replays commits; main moves only at the end. (b) fix + git add + --continue (repeat for each commit that conflicts), or --abort. (c) During a rebase HEAD = the commit you are replaying onto (origin's side); the bottom part is your commit. That's the reverse of a merge. Don't run git commit in the middle; use --continue.

Twist Unseen: you rebased a branch you had already pushed

Your own branch feature (only you use it) was pushed yesterday. Today you ran git rebase main on it. Now git push says ! [rejected] feature -> feature (non-fast-forward) and status says "have diverged, 3 and 2 different commits". git pull would merge the old copies back in. What do you run, and why is it safer than --force?

Show answer
$ git push --force-with-lease

Rebase gave the commits new hashes, so origin's feature (old hashes) is not in your history: any normal push is rejected. You really do want to replace it. --force-with-lease replaces it only if origin's feature is still where your origin/feature says. If someone pushed to it since your last fetch, it refuses instead of erasing their work. --force erases blindly. Never do this on main or any branch others build on: use revert or merge there.

Practice labs

Cheat sheet

  • git remote -v / git remote add origin URL list / add a remote
  • git push -u origin main first push + set upstream; afterwards just git push
  • git fetch moves only origin/main; git pull = fetch + merge (files change)
  • git pull --no-rebase / git pull --rebase merge or rebase when diverged
  • git log --oneline --graph --all --decorate see every branch and where origin/main is
  • git log --oneline main..origin/main what you're missing (swap = what you haven't pushed)
  • Rejected push → integrate (pull/merge or rebase), then push. Never --force / reset --hard origin/main
  • git reflog | grep "msg" find lost commits (detached, deleted branch, after reset)
  • git branch name HASH branch at a commit (stay put); git switch -c name HASH (and switch)
  • git merge --no-edit X merge without opening the editor
  • git switch feature; git rebase main replay feature on main (new hashes, main stays)
  • git add f; git rebase --continue / git rebase --abort during a rebase conflict
  • git cherry-pick HASH copy one commit onto the current branch
  • git reset --soft|--mixed|--hard HEAD~1 move branch back; keep staged / keep files / lose all
  • git revert --no-edit HASH undo a pushed commit with a new commit
  • git revert -m 1 --no-edit MERGE undo a whole pushed merge (keep parent 1 = main side)
  • git fetch --prune / git push origin --delete B drop stale origin/* names / delete B on origin
  • git switch B (origin/B exists) / git branch -u origin/B create tracking branch / set upstream later
  • git push --force-with-lease only for your own rebased branch; refuses if someone pushed meanwhile
  • git cat-file -t/-p HASH, cat .git/HEAD, git rev-parse main look inside
  • git log --all --oneline -- .env, git show HASH:.env find a leaked secret → rotate it
  • git rev-list --count main, git merge-base --is-ancestor A B check your own work like the grader
Topic 4

Docker: build, run, publish, persist

Build an image from a Dockerfile, run it with the right ports and volumes, and debug it when curl gets nothing.

Mental model

Image = an immutable, tagged stack of read-only layers plus a default command (a recipe that is already cooked). Container = a running instance of an image: ordinary Linux processes, plus their own namespaces, cgroup limits and a thin writable layer on top. Volume = Docker-managed storage with its own lifecycle: it outlives any container. Namespaces = what a process can see (own PIDs, own filesystem, own network). cgroups = how much it can use (CPU, memory). All containers share the host's kernel. Every command in this topic is one of: make an image (build), make a container (run), look at it (ps, logs, exec, inspect), or remove things (stop, rm, image rm, volume rm).

docker build: the trailing dot

In Quiz 2 Q14, docker build -t counter:1.0 (no dot) scored 0. The dot is not decoration. It is the build context: the folder Docker packs up and sends to the builder. Every COPY in the Dockerfile copies from the context. No context, no build. Docker also looks for the file called Dockerfile inside the context unless you say otherwise with -f.

docker build -t counter:1.0 .
  1. the command: turn a Dockerfile into an image
  2. -t name:tag = the image's name and version. Name must be lowercase. No tag means :latest.
  3. . = build context = "this folder" (the one you are standing in). Required. It is the one argument the error message talks about.
docker build -t hello-cli:dev -f Dockerfile.dev .
  1. build
  2. tag
  3. -f = which Dockerfile to read (default: ./Dockerfile)
  4. the context is still needed: -f picks the recipe, . gives the ingredients
CommandWhat it does
docker build -t app:1.0 .build from ./Dockerfile, context = current folder
docker build -t app:dev -f Dockerfile.dev .build from another Dockerfile
docker build -t app:1.0 ./backendcontext = the backend folder (its Dockerfile is used)
docker image ls (or docker images)list images; docker image ls app shows only that repository
docker image rm app:1.0 (or docker rmi)delete an image tag. Fails if a container (even stopped) uses it.
docker history app:1.0the layers of an image, newest first (shows the build steps)
docker pull node:24-alpinedownload an image from a registry (build and run do this automatically)
$ docker build -t counter:1.0
ERROR: docker: 'docker buildx build' requires 1 argument

Usage:  docker buildx build [OPTIONS] PATH | URL | -
# PATH is the missing build context. Add the dot:
$ docker build -t counter:1.0 .
#5 [2/5] WORKDIR /app
#6 [3/5] COPY package.json package-lock.json ./
...
#10 naming to docker.io/library/counter:1.0 done
$ docker image ls counter
