Docker restart policy = one host. Orchestrator (Kubernetes, Swarm) = many hosts, desired state. Managed platform = the provider runs the control plane. A restart policy brings a crashed process back. It can't move the app if the VM dies. Config and secrets live in the environment (--env-file), never in the image. Liveness (/healthz) must not touch the DB; readiness (/readyz) does. To scale out, app servers must be stateless: state goes to a shared store (Postgres), not process memory. Inside one deployable (modular monolith), modules talk only through public APIs, and a CI check enforces it.

docker run like the demo: restart, loopback port, env file

This is the one command the demo uses to put hello-api on the VM (Step 3 + Step 5). Every flag answers a hosting question: background, fixed name, who restarts it, who can reach it, where its config comes from.

docker run -d --name hello-api --restart unless-stopped -p 127.0.0.1:8000:8000 --env-file /etc/hello-api.env ghcr.io/acme/hello-api:1.0
  1. start a container in the background (detached), give the terminal back
  2. a fixed name, used by docker logs/rm/inspect hello-api
  3. restart after a crash and after a Docker/host restart, but not after you stopped it
  4. HOST_IP:HOST_PORT:CONTAINER_PORT. 127.0.0.1 = only this machine can connect. The reverse proxy (Caddy) on 80/443 is the public door.
  5. read every KEY=value line of a host file into the container's environment (secrets stay out of the image)
  6. the image (registry/owner/repo:tag). Always after all options.
CommandWhat it does
docker port NAMEshows the published ports: 3000/tcp -> 127.0.0.1:8301 (loopback) or 0.0.0.0:8301 (every interface = public)
docker inspect -f '{{.HostConfig.RestartPolicy.Name}}' NAMEthe restart policy (no, on-failure, always, unless-stopped)
docker inspect -f '{{json .Config.Env}}' NAMEthe container's environment variables
docker image inspect IMAGE | grep DATABASE_URLproves a secret is (not) baked into the image
docker rm -f NAME then docker run ...the only way to change ports, env or volumes: recreate
docker logs --tail 2 NAMElast 2 log lines (the demo's Step 6)
docker tag hello-api:1.0 ghcr.io/acme/hello-api:1.0add a registry name to an image; then docker push uploads it
$ docker run -d --name lab71-hello --restart unless-stopped -p 127.0.0.1:8301:3000 --env-file app.env lab71-hello:1.0
$ docker ps
... Up 3 seconds   127.0.0.1:8301->3000/tcp   lab71-hello
# with -p 8301:3000 instead you would see 0.0.0.0:8301->3000/tcp, [::]:8301->3000/tcp (public)
Trap

docker restart does not pick up a changed env file. Environment is fixed when the container is created. The demo does docker rm -f hello-api and runs it again: same image, new configuration.

Trap

--env-file keeps quotes. DB_PASSWORD="x" gives the value "x" with the quotes. Write plain KEY=value lines, no spaces around =.

docker: Error response from daemon: Conflict. The container name "/lab71-hello" is already in use

Means: an earlier attempt (maybe Exited) still holds the name.

Fix: docker rm -f lab71-hello, then run again.

Restart policies: a supervisor for one host

PolicyRestarts when...Use for
no (default)neverone-off commands, --rm tools
on-failure / on-failure:5the process exits with a code that is not 0 (max 5 tries)jobs: migrations, batch work. Retry on failure, stay finished after success.
alwaysany exit. A container you stopped by hand comes back when the Docker daemon restarts.rarely what you want
unless-stoppedany exit and after a daemon/host restart, unless you stopped itlong-running services (the demo)
docker update --restart unless-stopped lab72-api
  1. change settings of an existing container (no recreate)
  2. the new policy (also works for CPU/memory limits; NOT for ports, env, volumes)
  3. the container
$ docker exec lab72-api pkill -9 node      # fake a crash, like the demo's sudo pkill -9
$ docker ps --filter name=lab72-api
... Up 2 seconds ...                        # Docker brought it back
$ docker inspect -f '{{.RestartCount}}' lab72-api
1
$ docker stop lab72-legacy                  # --restart always, but...
$ docker ps -a --filter name=lab72-legacy
... Exited (143) ...                        # ...stays stopped: docker stop is never undone
Trap

docker stop and docker kill both count as "stopped by hand". Docker will not restart after them, whatever the policy. A crash is the process dying on its own. docker restart also doesn't raise RestartCount.

Trap

always/unless-stopped on a job that exits 0 makes it run again and again. A crash on every start gives a restart loop and no alert. Nobody is told.

Why it's not an orchestrator

One VM + unless-stopped covers: process crash ✓, host reboot ✓. It does not cover: VM lost ✗, crash loop ✗, full disk / expired cert ✗. Surviving the loss of a machine needs several hosts + something that reschedules. The four jobs of orchestration: failure detection, scheduling (find capacity), placement/startup, routing/service discovery. It runs a reconciliation loop: desired state − observed state = actions (desired 5, observed 2 → start 3 + update the load balancer).

Exit codes you'll see: 0 success, 1 app error, 137 = 128+9 (SIGKILL), 143 = 128+15 (SIGTERM, a normal docker stop).

Health checks: liveness vs readiness

CheckQuestionWho acts on a failureAction
/healthz livenessis the process alive? (no DB!)orchestratorrestart it
/readyz readinesscan it serve traffic now? (checks DB)load balancerstop routing, no restart
startuphas a slow boot finished?orchestratorhold liveness until then
HEALTHCHECK --interval=5s --timeout=3s --retries=3 CMD wget -qO- http://127.0.0.1:3000/healthz || exit 1
  1. Dockerfile instruction: Docker runs a command inside the container on a timer
  2. every 5 s, each try may take 3 s, 3 failures in a row = unhealthy
  3. the probe. Use a tool the image has: Alpine images have wget, usually not curl. Use 127.0.0.1: localhost can resolve to IPv6 ::1
  4. turn any failure into exit code 1 (0 = healthy)
$ docker ps --filter name=lab73-api
... Up 8 seconds (healthy) ...
$ docker stop lab73-db                       # DB outage
$ docker inspect -f '{{.State.Health.Status}}' lab73-api
healthy                                     # liveness doesn't depend on the DB: correct
$ docker exec lab73-api wget -S -qO- http://127.0.0.1:3000/readyz
  HTTP/1.1 503 Service Unavailable          # readiness fails: LB stops routing
$ docker inspect -f '{{json .State.Health}}' lab73-api   # last results + output of each probe
Trap

If /healthz checks the DB, a 2-minute DB outage makes every replica "dead", and the orchestrator restarts all of them: a restart storm that can't fix the database. And plain Docker only marks a container unhealthy; it does not restart it.

Tip

"/healthz OK" only proves the process is up. /hello (the DB path) can still fail. Two checks catch two different failures.

Scaling out: stateless replicas behind a load balancer

Vertical = a bigger machine: simple, but has a ceiling, needs a restart, and is still one point of failure. Horizontal = more replicas behind a load balancer: survives losing one, but every replica must be able to answer every request. So state (sessions, counters, uploads) must live outside the process: Postgres, Redis, or (in the lab) a shared named volume.

$ curl -s 127.0.0.1:8350/ ; curl -s 127.0.0.1:8350/
visits=1 served-by=3a60136cd6a9
visits=1 served-by=a71545db4393            # each replica counts in its own memory
$ docker run -d --name lab75-web-1 --network lab75-net -v lab75-data:/data lab75-web:1.1
$ docker run -d --name lab75-web-2 --network lab75-net -v lab75-data:/data lab75-web:1.1
$ docker restart lab75-lb                  # nginx resolves upstream names only at start
$ curl -s 127.0.0.1:8350/ ; curl -s 127.0.0.1:8350/
visits=3 served-by=2e9a8ec8046b
visits=4 served-by=5efcd377a970            # shared state: consistent
Trap

Replicas can't all publish the same host port (port is already allocated). Only the load balancer is published. Same reason docker compose up --scale api=3 fails when the service has a fixed ports: or container_name.

Why more instances don't always help

Each instance has its own DB pool (max_size=5), so N instances can open 5×N connections against one Postgres limit. If the trace says the time is spent waiting for a pool connection, the bottleneck is the DB pool, not the API CPU. Serverless is the extreme case: one connection per invocation against a hard server limit.

T4L1: modular monolith and enforced boundaries

A modular monolith is one deployable and one process, split into modules that own their rules and their private data and offer a public contract (functions like get_email(), or events like OrderPlaced). It has no network tax and ACID is easy. The boundary only holds if a tool enforces it in CI (Shopify's Packwerk). A comment or a team agreement doesn't count.

$ pytest -q
FF.
E   Dependency violation: billing -> users (not in users' public API shop.users.api):
E     shop/billing/invoice.py:1 imports shop.users._store
# fix: billing calls users.get_email(user_id) instead of reading USERS_TABLE
$ pytest -q
3 passed
$ git add shop/billing/invoice.py
$ git commit -m "billing: use users public API"
Trap

"It's one process, so a direct query into the other module's table is fine because it's faster" is wrong (Quiz 2A Q13). So are "silence/skip the check" and "move it to a microservice". Split into services only on measured pain (Fowler: more than 50 engineers, or very different hardware scaling).

Tip

Retries over a network need an idempotency key (a unique transaction reference). A timeout doesn't tell you the remote call failed: it may have run and only the reply was lost (partial failure).

Quiz-style Deploy hello-api like the demo

Your folder has a Dockerfile (app on port 8000) and hello.env with LOG_LEVEL and DATABASE_URL. Build hello-api:1.0. Run it in the background as hello-api, restarted after crashes and reboots but not after a manual stop, reachable only from this machine on port 8000, config from the file.

Show answer
$ docker build -t hello-api:1.0 .
$ docker run -d --name hello-api --restart unless-stopped -p 127.0.0.1:8000:8000 --env-file hello.env hello-api:1.0
$ docker inspect -f '{{.HostConfig.RestartPolicy.Name}}' hello-api
unless-stopped
$ docker port hello-api
8000/tcp -> 127.0.0.1:8000

"Not after a manual stop" = unless-stopped, not always. "Only this machine" = the 127.0.0.1: prefix. All flags before the image.

Drill Wrong policy, can't recreate

Container orders runs with no restart policy. You must give it unless-stopped without losing it (don't remove it). Then prove a crash is recovered.

Show answer
$ docker update --restart unless-stopped orders
$ docker exec orders pkill -9 node      # or whatever the app process is
$ docker inspect -f '{{.State.Status}} {{.RestartCount}}' orders
running 1

docker update changes the policy in place. Don't use docker kill/stop to test it: those are manual stops and are not restarted. (Note: if the app is PID 1 in the container, a kill from inside is ignored. The lab starts it with --init so it isn't.)

Quiz-style Which policy for the migration job?

migrate exits 1 while the DB isn't ready and 0 once it applied the migrations. It must be retried after a failure and never run again after a success. Which --restart? What happens with unless-stopped?

Show answer
$ docker run -d --name migrate --restart on-failure -v state:/state jobs:1.0
$ docker ps -a --filter name=migrate
... Exited (0) ...

on-failure restarts only on a non-zero exit. With unless-stopped (or always) Docker restarts it after the success too, forever (Restarting (0)).

Twist Healthy during a DB outage?

The Dockerfile has HEALTHCHECK CMD curl -f http://localhost:3000/healthz || exit 1 on node:24-alpine, and /healthz runs SELECT 1. The container is always unhealthy, even with the DB up. Then, after a fix, it goes unhealthy every time the DB blips. Find the three problems.

Show answer
HEALTHCHECK --interval=5s --timeout=3s --retries=3 CMD wget -qO- http://127.0.0.1:3000/healthz || exit 1

(1) Alpine has no curl: use wget (read the reason in docker inspect -f '{{json .State.Health}}'). (2) localhost may resolve to ::1 while the app listens on IPv4: use 127.0.0.1. (3) Liveness must not touch the DB: move SELECT 1 to /readyz, rebuild, recreate.

Twist Logged out at random

After scaling to 3 replicas behind a load balancer, users get logged out at random. Sessions are kept in a JavaScript Map in the server. What's wrong, and which fixes are real?

Show answer

Each replica only knows the sessions it created; the LB sends the next request elsewhere. Real fix: store sessions in a shared store (Postgres/Redis) or use stateless signed tokens, so any replica can answer. Sticky sessions only hide it: the sessions still die with a replica. Scaling vertically instead removes the symptom but keeps a single point of failure and a ceiling.

Quiz-style Boundary check fails in CI

CI prints Dependency violation: billing → user: User.find(id).email — not in user's public API. Which actions are right? (a) call the user module's public function for the email; (b) add an ignore rule for this file; (c) move billing to a microservice; (d) keep the CI check.

Show answer

(a) and (d). (b) silences the check. (c) adds the network tax without measured pain. In the lab: edit only billing/invoice.py, run pytest, then git add + one git commit.

Practice labs

Cheat sheet

  • docker run -d --name app --restart unless-stopped -p 127.0.0.1:8000:8000 --env-file app.env img:1.0 the demo's run
  • -p 127.0.0.1:H:C loopback only; -p H:C = 0.0.0.0 = public. Host port first.
  • --env-file f all KEY=value lines; -e KEY=value one variable. Options go before the image.
  • docker rm -f app + docker run ... to change env/ports/volumes (restart ≠ recreate)
  • docker update --restart unless-stopped app change the policy in place
  • no · on-failure[:N] (non-zero exit only: jobs) · always · unless-stopped (services)
  • docker stop/docker kill = manual: never restarted. Restart policy = one host, not an orchestrator.
  • docker inspect -f '{{.HostConfig.RestartPolicy.Name}} {{.RestartCount}}' app
  • docker inspect -f '{{json .Config.Env}}' app env of the container; docker image inspect img | grep SECRET must be empty
  • HEALTHCHECK --interval=5s --timeout=3s --retries=3 CMD wget -qO- http://127.0.0.1:3000/healthz || exit 1
  • docker inspect -f '{{.State.Health.Status}}' app starting / healthy / unhealthy (Docker only marks, doesn't restart)
  • liveness /healthz: no DB, fail = restart. readiness /readyz: checks DB, fail = LB stops routing.
  • docker exec app wget -S -qO- http://127.0.0.1:3000/readyz test from inside when there's no curl
  • Scale out = stateless replicas + LB + shared state (-v same-volume:/data or Postgres). Only the LB publishes a port.
  • Orchestration jobs: failure detection, scheduling, placement/startup, routing/service discovery. Reconcile: desired − observed = actions.
  • Modular monolith: one deployable; modules call public APIs only; enforce in CI (Packwerk / a test); never silence the check.
  • Exit codes: 0 ok, 1 app error, 137 SIGKILL, 143 SIGTERM.
Topic T5

Continuous safe delivery: build once, three ways back, code vs data

Tag an image by commit id, roll back by redeploying, reverting or flipping a flag, and write migrations that make rollback safe.

Mental model

The pipeline runs in a fixed order: Test, Build, Migrate, Deploy, Check health. Build the image once, tag it with the commit id, and promote that same image. When production breaks there are three ways back, fastest first: switch a flag off (seconds, no deploy), redeploy the previous image (about 1 minute, nothing is rebuilt), and revert the commit and run the pipeline again (minutes, permanent). All three bring back code. None of them brings back data: a renamed or dropped column, deleted rows or a sent email stay as they are. Code is reversible. Data is not. So migrations must only add (expand), and must never break the old code that is still running.

Build once, tag with the commit id

A tag like 2.4 is for people. The commit id says exactly which code is inside the image. Commit first, then build, so that the id names the code you built. Use one build with two -t, or one build followed by docker tag. Building twice can give a different image, which means production would get something that was never tested.

docker build --build-arg RELEASE=2.4 -t shop:2.4 -t shop:$(git rev-parse --short HEAD) .
  1. build an image from ./Dockerfile
  2. fills ARG RELEASE in the Dockerfile (an ENV RELEASE=${RELEASE} line keeps it in the image)
  3. first name: the human version
  4. second name: the shell runs git rev-parse --short HEAD first and puts its output (e.g. b7d70c4) here
  5. the build context. It is required.
CommandWhat it does
git rev-parse --short HEADprints the short commit id (7 characters)
docker tag shop:2.4 shop:b7d70c4adds a second name to the same image (no build)
docker image inspect -f '{{.Id}}' shop:2.4the full image id; both tags must print the same one
docker image ls shopboth rows show the same IMAGE ID
docker image rm shop:wrongtagremoves only that name, as long as another tag still points at the image
Trap

If you build before you commit, the "commit id" tag names the previous commit. Fix it by committing, adding the right tag with docker tag, and removing the wrong one with docker image rm.

Way back 1: redeploy the previous image

The old image still exists, and it was tested when it was built. Start a container from it with the same name, port and env. There is no test, build or migration step, which is why this takes about 1 minute. It only works if the database still fits the old code.

$ docker logs web                         # evidence: ERROR ... request: /checkout?customer=kabir&group=1
$ docker inspect -f '{{.Config.Env}}' web  # [STORE=pune PATH=... RELEASE=2.4]: copy only the -e you gave
$ docker port web                         # 3000/tcp -> 127.0.0.1:8402
$ docker rm -f web                        # frees the name AND the port
$ docker run -d --name web -p 127.0.0.1:8402:3000 -e STORE=pune shop:2.3
$ docker exec web wget -qO- http://localhost:3000/healthz     # no curl? ask from inside
{"status":"ok","version":"2.3","store":"pune"}
Conflict. The container name "/web" is already in use by container "3b1f..."

Means: the old container still exists, even if it is stopped.

Fix: run docker rm -f web first. Don't pick a new name or port, because users and the proxy know the old ones.

Trap

Don't copy RELEASE=2.4 into a -e. It comes from the image, and overriding it would make 2.3 report itself as 2.4. Don't rebuild 2.3 either: a rebuild is slower and may not give the image that was tested. Keep the 2.4 image so the developers can reproduce the bug. Quote URLs that contain &.

Way back 2: revert the commit (and ship it linearly)

git revert adds a new commit that undoes an old one. History only grows, so it is safe after a push, and the pipeline rebuilds from main. Use reset only for commits that were never pushed.

git revert --no-commit 7d02b1f
  1. undo that commit's diff
  2. stage the undo but don't commit yet, so you can choose the message
  3. the bad commit (from git log --oneline)
$ git revert --no-commit 7d02b1f
$ git commit -m "Roll back group orders"     # exact message from the task
$ git push
 ! [rejected]        main -> main (fetch first)  # a teammate pushed meanwhile
$ git pull --rebase                           # fetch + replay MY commit on top: no merge commit
$ git push
Trap

git revert -m "msg" is not "message". -m chooses a parent of a merge commit, and you get option `mainline' expects a number. A plain git revert --no-edit gives the message Revert "...", so if the task asks for a different message, use --no-commit + commit -m, or git commit --amend -m before you push. A plain git pull creates a merge commit, which gives the wrong commit count and a history that isn't linear (Quiz 3 Q9).

Tip

Q9 pattern: you have a local change and a teammate pushed. Do git add, git commit -m "exact message", git pull --rebase, then git push. If you already made a merge commit, run git reset --hard ORIG_HEAD before you push, then run pull --rebase.

Way back 3: the kill switch (feature flags)

A flag is an if whose condition lives outside the code: in a file, a database row or a flag service. If the app reads it on every request, a change applies on the next request, with no deploy and no restart. The buggy code stays in the image but is never reached.

# flags.json (edit in the Files panel, save, done: no restart)
{
  "group_order": { "rollout_percent": 0 },
  "new_menu": { "rollout_percent": 50 }
}
$ docker exec web cat /flags/flags.json       # the container sees the same (mounted) file
$ docker ps                                   # still "Up 10 minutes": same container, never restarted

In the lecture demo the flag is a database row: UPDATE flags SET enabled = false WHERE name = 'group_order';

Trap

Keep the file valid JSON: double quotes, numbers without quotes, and no trailing comma. Broken JSON breaks every request that reads it. Don't restart or re-create the container "to be safe": the task is graded on the container id and start time. If the app reads the flag only once at start-up, a change needs a restart, and then it is not a kill switch any more.

Percentage rollout: sha256 bucket, not random()

sha256("group_order:meena") first 4 bytes, big-endian % 100 < rollout_percent
  1. hash the flag name and the user, so each flag picks its own users
  2. turn the hash into a number from 0 to 4,294,967,295 (JS readUInt32BE(0), Python int.from_bytes(d[:4], "big"))
  3. the remainder gives the bucket, 0-99 (meena = 5, asha = 67)
  4. ON when the bucket is below the line. 0 = nobody (kill switch), 100 = everyone.
import hashlib

def bucket(flag, user):
    digest = hashlib.sha256(f"{flag}:{user}".encode()).digest()
    return int.from_bytes(digest[:4], "big") % 100

def is_enabled(flag, user, percent):
    return bucket(flag, user) < percent
  • Sticky: the same user always gets the same answer. With random() the button flickers on and off.
  • Monotonic: going 10 → 50 → 100 only moves the line, so nobody loses the feature.
  • Salted: with hash(user) alone, two flags at 10% hit the same 10% of users (978 of 978). With flag:user, about 1% of users get both (99).
Trap

Python's built-in hash() on strings changes on every run (it is randomised per process), so it isn't sticky. Use <, not <=: with <=, 0% still turns the flag on for bucket 0. Compare with percent, not percent/100.

Code vs data: the rename trap and expand/contract

The pipeline migrates before it deploys, so the old code is still serving while the migration runs. RENAME COLUMN items TO order_items breaks 2.3 straight away, and it stays broken after a rollback: /healthz says 2.3 is ok, but every checkout fails with column "items" does not exist. The only way out is forward, with a new migration that renames the column back.

-- V3__add_order_items.sql : EXPAND (additive only, old code unaffected)
ALTER TABLE orders ADD COLUMN order_items TEXT;
UPDATE orders SET order_items = items WHERE order_items IS NULL;   -- backfill
-- no RENAME, no DROP. Later releases: write both, read new, use only new,
-- then CONTRACT: ALTER TABLE orders DROP COLUMN items;   <- the irreversible point
Trap

Adding ADD COLUMN ... NOT NULL without a default fails on a table that has rows (SQLite: Cannot add a NOT NULL column with default value NULL), and it would break the old code's inserts. Make the new column nullable.

An applied migration was edited (Quiz 3 Q10: checksum mismatch for migration version 02): put the file back to exactly what production ran, as its own commit. Don't write a migration for the edit.

$ git log --oneline -- migrations/V2__add_group_id.sql
$ git restore --source=<production commit> migrations/V2__add_group_id.sql
$ git add migrations/V2__add_group_id.sql
$ git commit -m "Restore V2 to production version"
Drill Two tags, one image

You committed "Release 2.6". Build tiffingo:2.6 and tiffingo:<short id> with RELEASE=2.6, as one image.

Show answer
docker build --build-arg RELEASE=2.6 -t tiffingo:2.6 -t tiffingo:$(git rev-parse --short HEAD) .
docker image ls tiffingo      # same IMAGE ID twice

One build gives one image id. The commit-id tag tells you exactly which code is running.

Quiz-style Roll back, keep the config

app runs app:2.4 with -p 8080:3000 -e DB_URL=... and fails. Roll back to app:2.3 with the same name and port. Don't rebuild.

Show answer
docker inspect -f '{{.Config.Env}}' app     # copy DB_URL
docker rm -f app
docker run -d --name app -p 8080:3000 -e DB_URL=... app:2.3

Settings belong to the container, not the image, so read them before you remove it.

Quiz-style Revert with a message, teammate pushed first

Undo the pushed commit "Add coupons" with message Remove coupons. Push. History must be linear, and you must not force-push.

Show answer
git revert --no-commit <hash>
git commit -m "Remove coupons"
git push                 # rejected (fetch first)
git pull --rebase
git push

Don't use -m on revert. Don't use plain pull, because it makes a merge commit.

Quiz-style Which way back?

"Split the bill" (behind a flag) charges the wrong friend. The rest of 2.5 is fine. What is the fastest fix?

Show answer

Switch the flag off (rollout 0, or enabled = false). It takes seconds, there is no deploy, and everything else in 2.5 keeps running. Redeploying 2.4 would also remove the good parts of 2.5.

Twist Healthy but 100% failing

After you roll back to 2.3, /healthz says ok, 2.3, yet every checkout fails with column "items" does not exist. Why, and what is the fix?

Show answer

The 2.4 migration renamed the column. The rollback restored the code but not the schema, and the health check only runs SELECT 1. To fix it forward, add a new migration: ALTER TABLE orders RENAME COLUMN order_items TO items;. Next time, use expand: add order_items, backfill it, and keep items.

Practice labs

Cheat sheet

  • Test → Build → Migrate → Deploy → Check health the pipeline order; one failure stops it
  • docker build --build-arg RELEASE=2.4 -t x:2.4 -t x:$(git rev-parse --short HEAD) . build once, two tags
  • docker tag x:2.4 x:abc1234 add a name, no build
  • docker image inspect -f '{{.Id}}' x:2.4 compare image ids
  • docker inspect -f '{{.Config.Env}}' web / docker port web read settings before removing
  • docker rm -f web && docker run -d --name web -p H:C -e K=V x:2.3 redeploy the previous image
  • docker exec web wget -qO- "http://localhost:3000/healthz" test from inside, with no curl
  • git revert --no-commit <hash> + git commit -m "..." revert with your own message
  • git pull --rebase after a rejected push: linear, no merge commit
  • git restore --source=<hash> path put a file back to an old version, then commit
  • flags file / UPDATE flags SET enabled=false kill switch: seconds, no deploy, no restart
  • sha256(f"{flag}:{user}")[:4] big-endian % 100 < percent sticky percentage rollout
  • ADD COLUMN new TEXT; UPDATE t SET new = old; expand + backfill. Never RENAME or DROP while old code runs.
  • Flag off (seconds) < redeploy image (~1 min) < revert + pipeline (minutes). None of them undoes data.
Topic 9

Surviving: the database under load (RailBook Tatkal)

Count and remove N+1 queries, pick the right composite index and prove it with EXPLAIN, size connection pools with the formula, and fix the last-seat race with one conditional UPDATE, all checked with pytest.

Mental model

At 10:00 Tatkal opens and thousands of requests hit one database. Four things break, in this order of "where the time goes": round trips (too many queries: N+1), finding rows (no usable index: full SCAN), connections (each pool holds real Postgres processes; they multiply with servers; #101 is refused), and the last seat (check-then-act: two requests read 99 before either writes). Each has a measurable symptom (query count, plan line, connection total, booked > capacity) and that symptom is what a test asserts.

N+1: count the round trips

One query for a list, then one more query per row. N rows = N+1 queries. Each query may be fast; the cost is round trips x latency. Laptop: DB 1 ms away, 51 x 1 ms = 51 ms (nobody notices). Production: DB 30 ms away, 51 x 30 ms = 1,530 ms ≈ 1.5 s.

# the warning sign: a query INSIDE a loop
rows = conn.execute("SELECT id, pnr, train_id FROM bookings WHERE user_id = ?", (uid,)).fetchall()   # 1
for booking_id, pnr, train_id in rows:
    train = conn.execute("SELECT train_no FROM trains WHERE id = ?", (train_id,)).fetchone()     # + N

# the fix: ONE trip with a JOIN
conn.execute("SELECT b.pnr, t.train_no FROM bookings b JOIN trains t ON t.id = b.train_id "
             "WHERE b.user_id = ? ORDER BY b.id", (uid,))
FixQueriesNotes
JOIN1parent + child columns in one row; keep the same ORDER BY
WHERE id IN (?, ?, ...) / Postgres = ANY($1)2get the list, then all children at once
ORM eager loading1-2the ORM does one of the two above
index on the child columnstill N+1each trip faster, still 51 trips: not a fix
bigger pool / faster CPUstill N+1not a fix

Counting queries in a test (sqlite3): conn.set_trace_callback(f) makes sqlite call f(sql) once per statement it runs.

def test_at_most_two_queries():
    conn = make_db()
    queries = []
    conn.set_trace_callback(queries.append)    # record every statement
    list_bookings_with_trains(conn, 7)
    conn.set_trace_callback(None)              # stop recording
    assert len(queries) <= 2

$ pytest -s -k two_queries      # -s shows print() output, -k picks the test by name
E   assert 6 <= 2
Trap

With a JOIN, SELECT * returns columns of both tables, so row[1] is not what it was. Name the columns. And an inner JOIN drops parents with no children: use LEFT JOIN + count(b.id) (not count(*), which counts the NULL row as 1).

Indexes, the leading column, and reading the plan

No index: the database reads every row (Seq Scan in Postgres, SCAN in sqlite). An index is a sorted copy of some columns with pointers to the rows (a B-tree): a handful of steps even among crores of rows. A composite index is sorted by the first column, then by the second inside each first value: the phone-book rule.

CREATE INDEX idx_trains_station_date ON trains (from_station, journey_date);
  1. make a B-tree index (plain = blocks writes while building)
  2. its name (you will see it in the plan)
  3. which table
  4. column order matters: leading column first = the one you ALWAYS filter on
Query on (from_station, journey_date)Uses the index?
WHERE from_station = 'PUNE'yes (leading column)
WHERE from_station = 'PUNE' AND journey_date = '2026-11-14'yes (order in the WHERE does not matter)
WHERE journey_date = '2026-11-14'no (second column alone)
WHERE train_no = '12124'no (not in the index)

Read the plan. sqlite: EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN <query> (from Python: conn.execute("EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN " + sql, params), column 3 is the text). Postgres: EXPLAIN / EXPLAIN ANALYZE (really runs it, shows times).

# before (no index)
search station+date: SCAN trains
search station only: SCAN trains
# index (journey_date, from_station): the WRONG order
search station+date: SEARCH trains USING INDEX w (journey_date=? AND from_station=?)
search station only: SCAN trains                       # <- leading column is not filtered
# index (from_station, journey_date): right
search station+date: SEARCH trains USING INDEX idx_trains_station_date (from_station=? AND journey_date=?)
search station only: SEARCH trains USING INDEX idx_trains_station_date (from_station=?)
Plan textMeans
SCAN trains / Postgres Seq Scan on trainsreads the whole table
SEARCH trains USING INDEX idx (col=?) / Index Scan using idx + Index Cond: (...)jumps via the index
USING COVERING INDEX / Index Only Scanthe index alone has every needed column: even better
SCAN trains USING COVERING INDEX idxstill reads the whole index, in order: not a real lookup
SEARCH b USING AUTOMATIC COVERING INDEXsqlite builds a throwaway index on every call: you are missing an index
USE TEMP B-TREE FOR ORDER BYa sort of the result rows; fine for a few rows
Trap

Indexes are not free: every INSERT/UPDATE updates every index, and they use disk. Index the queries you run often; don't index every column. And a correct index can be correctly ignored: if the query returns a big share of the table, a Seq Scan is cheaper.

Tip

Join columns (foreign keys like bookings.train_id) are the classic missing index. Postgres indexes primary keys and UNIQUE columns for you, not foreign keys.

Why a new migration file

Add the index as migrations/02_....sql, never by editing 01_...: production already applied 01 (Flyway would refuse the changed checksum). On a live Postgres table use CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY: writes continue, but it builds slower and cannot run inside a transaction (plain CREATE INDEX blocks writes, not reads).

Connections and pool math

A Postgres connection is a separate server process with its own memory, so there is a hard cap: max_connections (default 100). A pool keeps up to pool_size connections open per app process. Every server brings its own pool, so connections multiply when you scale out. Connection 101 is refused:

FATAL:  sorry, too many clients already

Means: the total of all pools + other clients went over max_connections. Postgres does not queue it.

Fix: size pools on purpose, add a pool wait timeout, leave autoscaler headroom, or put a pooler (PgBouncer / Supavisor) in front.

# the formula: count EVERY client at the WORST case
servers_max x pool_size + other pools (workers, cron) + humans  <=  max_connections - spare

8 x 10 + 5 = 85  <= 100 - 10 = 90    OK
8 x 14 + 5 = 117 >  90                OVER  (fits at 6 servers: 6 x 14 + 5 = 89, breaks when the autoscaler adds 2)
8 x 11 + 5 = 93  >  90                OVER  (forgot the spare)
200 serverless x 5 = 1000             10x over -> a pooler, not a bigger limit
SettingWhat it does
pool_size / node-postgres maxconnections this process may hold
wait_timeout_ms / connectionTimeoutMilliswait up to this long for a free pooled connection, then fail. 0 = fail at once. Must be bounded
pooler session modea DB connection stays with one client until it disconnects
pooler transaction modelent per transaction: far more sharing, but no prepared statements / session locks
SHOW max_connections;the limit (changing it needs a restart)
SELECT count(*) FROM pg_stat_activity;connections open right now
Trap

"Scale out the app servers" makes the DB problem worse (more pools). "90% busy is fine" is wrong: queues explode near 100% (wait ~ ρ/(1−ρ): 1 at 50%, 9 at 90%, 99 at 99%).

The last seat: check-then-act and its fixes

booked = 99, capacity = 100. Two requests both read 99, both decide "seat free", both write: 2 bookings for 1 seat. Unit tests pass because they send one request at a time.

# BUG: two statements, a gap between them
booked, capacity = conn.execute("SELECT booked, capacity FROM trains WHERE id = ?", (tid,)).fetchone()
if booked >= capacity: return 409
#   <- the other request runs here
conn.execute("UPDATE trains SET booked = booked + 1 WHERE id = ?", (tid,))

# FIX 1: check AND act in one statement
cur = conn.execute("UPDATE trains SET booked = booked + 1 WHERE id = ? AND booked < capacity", (tid,))
conn.commit()
return 409 if cur.rowcount == 0 else 201       # Postgres: ... RETURNING booked; 0 rows = full
ApproachStops the oversell?Notes
Fix 1: UPDATE ... WHERE booked < capacityyessimplest; 1 row = seat, 0 rows = 409 Conflict
Fix 2: BEGIN; SELECT ... FOR UPDATE; ...; COMMIT;yeslocks that row (not the table); others wait; for multi-statement logic
Fix 3: CHECK (booked <= capacity), UNIQUE (train_id, seat_no)yes (as an error)safety net: turns an oversell into an error (500), not a friendly 409
wrap old read+write in BEGIN/COMMITnoa transaction is all-or-nothing, not mutual exclusion
retry loop / index on train_idnoirrelevant to the race
SERIALIZABLEyesPostgres aborts one transaction; you retry
optimistic locking (version column)yesWHERE id = ? AND version = ?; 0 rows = someone changed it, retry
distributed lock (Redis)-only for state outside one database

Testing a race without luck. Real threads make a flaky test. Instead, put a hook in the gap (pause()) and let the test run user B inside it. Same interleaving every run:

def test_last_seat_two_users():
    conn = make_db(capacity=100, booked=99)
    results = two_users_at_once(conn, 1)     # B runs inside A's pause()
    assert sorted(results) == [201, 409]
    assert booked(conn) == 100

$ pytest
E   assert [201, 201] == [201, 409]          # before the fix: both got the seat
Trap

WHERE booked <= capacity lets 100 become 101: it must be <. In sqlite you put a CHECK in CREATE TABLE; in Postgres: ALTER TABLE trains ADD CONSTRAINT booked_le_capacity CHECK (booked <= capacity);

Worked scenarios

Drill How long do the DB trips take?

A page lists 40 orders and fetches each order's items in a loop. The DB is 25 ms away. Roughly how long is spent on DB round trips, and what is it after a JOIN?

Show answer
before: (1 + 40) x 25 ms = 1,025 ms ≈ 1 s
after:   1 x 25 ms       =    25 ms   (2 queries with IN (...) = 50 ms)

N+1 cost is trips x latency; an index on order_items would make each trip a bit faster but keep all 41.

Quiz-style Which index for the bookings search?

Hot queries: WHERE user_id = ? AND status = ? and WHERE user_id = ?. A nightly report filters WHERE status = ?. Write the migration, and say what the plan for each query shows.

Show answer
-- migrations/03_add_bookings_user_status_index.sql
CREATE INDEX idx_bookings_user_status ON bookings (user_id, status);

user_id + status : SEARCH bookings USING INDEX idx_bookings_user_status (user_id=? AND status=?)
user_id only     : SEARCH bookings USING INDEX idx_bookings_user_status (user_id=?)
status only      : SCAN bookings      -- second column alone: fine for a nightly report

Leading column = the one every hot query filters on. A separate index for the report would cost every insert.

Quiz-style 12 servers, pool 8, plus a cron job

max_connections = 100. 12 app servers with pool 8, a cron job with pool 5. What happens at peak? With 10 spare and up to 9 servers and no cron job, what is the largest pool?

Show answer
12 x 8 + 5 = 101 > 100   -> one connection refused: FATAL: sorry, too many clients already
9 x p <= 100 - 10 = 90   -> p = 10

Count every client, at the most servers the autoscaler may run. Add a bounded wait timeout so a short spike waits instead of failing.

Quiz-style "We wrapped it in a transaction and still oversold"

The code does BEGIN; SELECT count(*) FROM bookings WHERE train_id = 1; -- if < capacity: INSERT ...; COMMIT;. 10 requests at once for the last seat: 3 succeed. Why, and give two fixes plus a safety net.

Show answer
-- why: all 10 read the same count before any of them inserted (a transaction is not a lock)
-- fix 1
UPDATE trains SET booked = booked + 1 WHERE id = 1 AND booked < capacity RETURNING booked;  -- 0 rows -> 409
-- fix 2
BEGIN; SELECT capacity FROM trains WHERE id = 1 FOR UPDATE; SELECT count(*) ...; INSERT ...; COMMIT;
-- safety net
ALTER TABLE trains ADD CONSTRAINT booked_le_capacity CHECK (booked <= capacity);
ALTER TABLE bookings ADD CONSTRAINT uniq_seat UNIQUE (train_id, seat_no);

A full train is an expected outcome: 409 Conflict, not 500.

Twist Fixed the N+1, plan still says SCAN

You rewrote the departures board as one LEFT JOIN query and added an index on trains (from_station, journey_date). The plan now reads:

SEARCH t USING INDEX idx_trains_station_date (from_station=? AND journey_date=?)
SCAN b LEFT-JOIN
USE TEMP B-TREE FOR ORDER BY

What is still wrong and what do you add?

Show answer
-- for each matching train, the join reads ALL bookings: the join column has no index
CREATE INDEX idx_bookings_train ON bookings (train_id);
-- plan: SEARCH b USING COVERING INDEX idx_bookings_train (train_id=?) LEFT-JOIN

Round trips and full reads are separate problems; check both the query count and every plan line. TEMP B-TREE is just sorting ~20 result rows.

Practice labs

Cheat sheet

  • N+1 1 list query + 1 per row; cost = queries x latency (51 x 30 ms ≈ 1.5 s)
  • JOIN / WHERE id IN (...) / = ANY($1) fix N+1 (1-2 queries); an index does not
  • conn.set_trace_callback(list.append) count sqlite statements in a test
  • LEFT JOIN ... count(b.id) keep parents with 0 children, count them as 0
  • CREATE INDEX idx ON t (always_filtered, sometimes_filtered); leading column first
  • (a, b) helps a and a + b; not b alone
  • EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN sql sqlite: SCAN = full read, SEARCH ... USING INDEX = lookup
  • EXPLAIN ANALYZE sql Postgres: Seq Scan vs Index Scan + Index Cond, real times
  • CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY no write blocking, slower, never inside BEGIN/COMMIT
  • every index slows every INSERT/UPDATE; index join (foreign key) columns
  • servers_max x pool + other clients <= max_connections - spare (default limit 100)
  • FATAL: sorry, too many clients already = over the limit; refused, not queued
  • pool wait timeout: bounded wait for a free connection (0 = fail at once)
  • pooler (PgBouncer, Supavisor): transaction mode shares more, loses prepared statements / session locks
  • UPDATE ... SET booked = booked + 1 WHERE id = ? AND booked < capacity; rowcount == 0 → 409
  • SELECT ... FOR UPDATE inside BEGIN/COMMIT locks the row; others wait
  • CHECK (booked <= capacity), UNIQUE (train_id, seat_no) = safety net, not the fix
  • BEGIN/COMMIT around check-then-insert does not stop overselling
Lecture demos

T5 demos: deploy, break, go back, flag, roll out

Run the professor's two T5 demos yourself, predict every output, and tie each moment to the slide idea it proves.

Mental model

A pipeline is a row of gates: Test → Build → Migrate → Deploy → Check health → Switch. Any gate fails, the pipeline stops and production is unchanged. Every build is an image tagged twice (version + content id), so going back = start an old image again. That is fast, but it only changes code. The database stays as the last migration left it. So a migration must keep the old code working (add, don't rename or drop). And a flag is an if whose condition lives in the database or a file: flipping it changes behaviour on the next request, with no deploy. A percentage rollout puts each user in a fixed bucket sha256(flag:user) % 100. The user is ON if their bucket is < percent.

Before you start: what you need and how to run

Everything runs in Docker. You need no Node, psql or Postgres on the host. The demos live in demos/t5/. demos/README.md has the full file list, prerequisites and troubleshooting.

NeedWhy
Docker running (no sudo)DB, proxy, app, tests and rollout.js all run in containers
Port 8080 freeCaddy, the load balancer, publishes 8080:8080. App containers are not published, only reachable through Caddy.
postgres:16-alpine, node:22-alpine, caddy:2.8.4-alpinepulled by ./preflight.sh
Internet the first timenpm ci inside the image build downloads pg. After preflight, builds come from cache.
$ cd demos/t5
$ ./preflight.sh smoke
2. Ports
  PASS  port 8080 is free
3. Download images
  PASS  postgres:16-alpine
  PASS  node:22-alpine
  PASS  caddy:2.8.4-alpine
4. Build the TiffinGo releases
  PASS  t5-pipeline (4 releases)
5. Smoke test
  PASS  t5-pipeline: deploy 2.3, deploy 2.4, roll back to 2.3
  PASS  t5-rollout: 10,000 users bucketed
All good.
CommandWhat it does
cd t5-pipeline && ./demo.shDemo 1, 12 steps, Enter between steps. ./demo.sh 7 resumes at step 7.
./traffic.sh (second terminal)fake customers, 3/second. Every 5th request is customer=kabir&group=1. kabir has no saved group.
cd t5-rollout && ./demo.shDemo 2, 9 steps, one terminal
DEMO_NOPAUSE=1 ./demo.shrun all steps without waiting for Enter
./reset-all.shremove everything both demos created (containers, network, tiffingo images, state files)

What is running once setup.sh and a deploy have finished:

your browser / curl / traffic.sh
        │  http://localhost:8080
        ▼
  t5-proxy  (Caddy)      reverse_proxy tiffingo-2-3-1793711655:3000   ← the ONE live app container
        │  docker network "tiffingo"
        ▼
  tiffingo-2-3-...  (image tiffingo:2.3, node + pg)
        │  DATABASE_URL=postgres://postgres:postgres@t5-db:5432/postgres
        ▼
  t5-db  (postgres:16-alpine)   tables: schema_migrations, orders, [flags]
Tip

Two endpoints matter. /healthz runs only SELECT 1 and answers {"status":"ok","version":"2.3"}. /checkout?customer=X&group=0|1 answers ok order #17 Rs 240 single (v2.3), or ERROR <message> (v2.4) with HTTP 500.

Demo 1, Part A (slide 14): deploy, break, redeploy the previous image

Step 1 · Start the platform and deploy v2.3

$ ./setup.sh
Network:       tiffingo
Database:      t5-db (postgres:16-alpine)
Load balancer: t5-proxy (caddy) on http://localhost:8080
Platform ready. Nothing deployed yet.
$ ./deploy.sh 2.3
Deploying TiffinGo v2.3   (live now: nothing)
[1/6] Test          unit tests in a fresh container
         $ docker run --rm node:22-alpine node --test common/pricing.test.js
         3 tests passed
[2/6] Build         one image, tagged with the version and the code's id
         $ docker build -t tiffingo:2.3 -t tiffingo:cdf64da .
         tiffingo:2.3 = tiffingo:cdf64da
[3/6] Migrate       releases/2.3/migrate.sql
         CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS orders (   ◀ new table
           ...  items      text NOT NULL, ...
[4/6] Deploy        start v2.3 beside the old version (old one still serving)
         $ docker run -d --name tiffingo-2-3-1793711655 --network tiffingo tiffingo:2.3
[5/6] Check health
         {"status":"ok","version":"2.3"}
[6/6] Switch        send traffic to v2.3
         $ caddy reload   # load balancer now sends traffic to tiffingo-2-3-1793711655
Deployed v2.3 in 10.9 s.

Notice: the order of the six stages, and the build line tiffingo:2.3 = tiffingo:cdf64da: one build, two tags. The 7-character id is a hash of the code (it stands in for the commit id).

Slide idea

"The order of the steps": Test, Build, Migrate, Deploy, Check health. "Build once, tag with the commit id (tiffingo:3f9a2c1), promote the same image."

Terminal 2: ./traffic.sh

18:44:28  ok     order #1  Rs 240  single  (v2.3)
18:44:29  ok     order #4  Rs 240  single  (v2.3)
18:44:29  ok     order #5  Rs 240  single  (v2.3)   # kabir, group=1: v2.3 has no group orders, so it's a single order

Step 2 · Which version is live?

$ curl -s http://localhost:8080/healthz
{"status":"ok","version":"2.3"}

Step 3 · The v2.4 change and its migration (diff of releases/2.3/server.js → 2.4, trimmed)

+const GROUPS = { asha: ["asha", "ravi", "meena"] };     ◀ only the developer's own account has a saved group
+  if (group) {
+    const members = GROUPS[customer];
+    items = members.map(() => "paneer thali");          ◀ kabir has no saved group, so members is undefined
$ cat app/releases/2.4/migrate.sql
ALTER TABLE orders ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS group_id int;   ◀ safe: old code ignores the new column

Notice: the migration only adds a nullable column. Remember that for step 6.

Step 4 · 19:00, deploy v2.4

$ ./deploy.sh 2.4
[1/6] Test          3 tests passed
[2/6] Build         tiffingo:2.4 = tiffingo:e4e9911
[3/6] Migrate       ALTER TABLE orders ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS group_id int;

Notice: every gate was green, and still 1 in 5 checkouts fail. The tests only cover pricing.js. The health check only runs SELECT 1. Neither one tries a group order from a customer without a saved group.

Slide idea

The incident: "errors for 1 in 5 orders". Gates only catch what they check: /healthz is a readiness check (is the process up, can it reach the DB), not a test of business logic. That's why you still need fast ways back.

Step 5 · 19:11, what can we go back to?

$ docker images tiffingo
IMAGE              ID             DISK USAGE   CONTENT SIZE
tiffingo:2.3       8cd72f79716f        244MB         62.1MB
tiffingo:2.4       40b1885db61e        244MB         62.1MB
tiffingo:cdf64da   8cd72f79716f        244MB         62.1MB
tiffingo:e4e9911   40b1885db61e        244MB         62.1MB

Notice: 2.3 and cdf64da have the same image ID: one image, two names. The old image is still there to go back to.

Step 6 · Redeploy the previous image

$ ./rollback.sh 2.3
Rolling back to v2.3 from image tiffingo:2.3   (live now: v2.4)
         nothing to test, build or migrate: the image already exists
[1/3] Start         $ docker run -d --name tiffingo-2-3-1793711737 --network tiffingo tiffingo:2.3
[2/3] Check health  {"status":"ok","version":"2.3"}
[3/3] Switch        old container tiffingo-2-4-1793711696 stopped
Back on v2.3 in 6.2 s.
$ curl -s localhost:8080/healthz
{"status":"ok","version":"2.3"}
# terminal 2: all green again, (v2.3)
$ docker exec t5-db psql -U postgres -c 'select version from schema_migrations'
 2.3
 2.4          # the 2.4 migration is still applied; group_id is still in the table

Notice: three stages instead of six, a few seconds instead of a full pipeline. rollback.sh never touches the database. It worked only because v2.3 doesn't mind the extra group_id column.

Slide idea

"Three ways back": revert + redeploy (minutes) > redeploy the previous image (~1 minute hosted, seconds here: nothing is rebuilt) > flag off (seconds).

Demo 1, Part B (slide 15): the rename that rollback can't undo

Step 7 · Deploy v2.4-rename: same code, but it writes order_items, and the migration renames the column.

$ cat app/releases/2.4-rename/migrate.sql
ALTER TABLE orders ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS group_id int;   ◀ safe: old code ignores the new column
ALTER TABLE orders RENAME COLUMN items TO order_items;      ◀ breaks code that still uses the old name
$ ./deploy.sh 2.4-rename
[3/6] Migrate       ... RENAME COLUMN items TO order_items
[4/6] Deploy        start v2.4-rename beside the old version (old one still serving)
[5/6] Check health  {"status":"ok","version":"2.4-rename"}
[6/6] Switch        old container tiffingo-2-3-1793711737 stopped
Deployed v2.4-rename in 12.7 s.
# terminal 2: the migration ran, but v2.3 is still the live container for ~4 s
18:46:08  ok     order #224  Rs 240  single  (v2.3)
18:46:08  ERROR  column "items" of relation "orders" does not exist  (v2.3)
18:46:09  ERROR  column "items" of relation "orders" does not exist  (v2.3)
...         # 8 failed requests in a row
18:46:12  ERROR  column "items" of relation "orders" does not exist  (v2.3)
18:46:12  ok     order #225  Rs 240  single  (v2.4-rename)
18:46:14  ERROR  Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'map')  (v2.4-rename)   # the old group-order bug is still there

Notice: the outage starts before the new code is live. Step 3 (migrate) runs while step 4's old code is still serving.

Slide idea

Migrate before deploy, and "during step 4, old and new code run side by side". So every migration must be compatible with the old code. A RENAME or DROP is not.

Step 8 · Roll back to v2.3 again ("it worked last time")

$ ./rollback.sh 2.3
Back on v2.3 in 8.1 s.
$ curl -s localhost:8080/healthz
{"status":"ok","version":"2.3"}          # healthy...
# terminal 2: ...and 100% red
18:46:47  ERROR  column "items" of relation "orders" does not exist  (v2.3)
18:46:48  ERROR  column "items" of relation "orders" does not exist  (v2.3)
$ docker exec t5-db psql -U postgres -c '\d orders'
   Column    |           Type           | Nullable
-------------+--------------------------+----------
 id          | integer                  | not null
 customer    | text                     | not null
 order_items | text                     | not null      # v2.3 asks for items
 total       | integer                  | not null
 created_at  | timestamp with time zone | not null
 group_id    | integer                  |

Notice: /healthz says ok because SELECT 1 doesn't use the orders table. The code went back. The column didn't.

Slide idea

"What going back cannot undo: a dropped or renamed column." Code is reversible. Data is not. It also answers the slide-6 poll: after a rollback, checkout fails on v2.3 too.

Step 9 · The only way out is forward

$ docker exec t5-db psql -U postgres -c 'ALTER TABLE orders RENAME COLUMN order_items TO items;'
ALTER TABLE
# terminal 2 recovers on the next request
18:47:06  ok     order #292  Rs 240  single  (v2.3)

Notice: the fix is a new change to the database (fix forward), not a rollback. Done safely, it would have been expand/contract. First ADD COLUMN order_items, then write both columns, backfill, read the new one. DROP COLUMN items comes last, and that is the irreversible step.

Trap

This manual ALTER is not recorded in schema_migrations (it still lists 2.4-rename as applied). In a real team, write it as the next migration file so every database gets it.

Demo 1, Part C (slide 18): the same bug behind a flag

Step 10 · Deploy v2.5: the only code change is an if around the group-order code.

+async function flagOn(db, name) {
+  const { rows } = await db.query("SELECT enabled FROM flags WHERE name = $1", [name]);   ◀ read on every request
+  return rows.length > 0 && rows[0].enabled;
+}
-  if (group) {
+  const groupOrder = group && await flagOn(db, "group_order");
+  if (groupOrder) {
     items = members.map(() => "paneer thali");   ◀ the same bug, still here, now behind the flag
$ ./deploy.sh 2.5
[3/6] Migrate
         CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS flags (name text PRIMARY KEY, enabled boolean NOT NULL);   ◀ new table
         INSERT INTO flags VALUES ('group_order', false) ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING;               ◀ the flag starts OFF
Deployed v2.5 in 10.3 s.
# terminal 2: no errors at all; kabir's group orders are served as single orders
18:47:27  ok     order #347  Rs 240  single  (v2.5)

Step 11 · Switch the flag on

$ ./flag.sh on
         $ UPDATE flags SET enabled = true WHERE name = 'group_order';
group_order is now ON. No deploy. The next request sees it.
18:47:40  ERROR  Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'map')  (v2.5)
18:47:42  ERROR  Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'map')  (v2.5)
$ curl -s 'localhost:8080/checkout?customer=asha&group=1'
ok     order #383  Rs 540  group  (v2.5)

Step 12 · The kill switch

$ ./flag.sh off
group_order is now OFF. No deploy. The next request sees it.
18:47:43  ok     order #384  Rs 240  single  (v2.5)     # errors stop on the very next request
$ curl -s localhost:8080/healthz
{"status":"ok","version":"2.5"}
$ docker ps --format '{{.Names}} {{.Image}}'
tiffingo-2-5-1793711839 tiffingo:2.5      # the SAME container before and after both flips

Notice: no image, no container, no deploy changed. Only one database row did. Everything else in v2.5 kept running.

Slide idea

"A flag is an if-statement whose condition lives outside the code." Kill switch = the fastest way back, seconds. "Read on every request: changes land at once, but every request hits the database." The slide-17 poll answer: C, switch the flag off.

Bonus: what a failing gate looks like

The class demo never fails a gate. Here is a real run (in a scratch copy) with one price broken in common/pricing.js ("paneer thali": 18 instead of 180), deploying 2.4 on top of a live 2.3:

$ ./deploy.sh 2.4
Deploying TiffinGo v2.4   (live now: v2.3)
[1/6] Test          unit tests in a fresh container
# tests 3
# pass 1
# fail 2
  ✗ tests failed
Pipeline stopped. Production is unchanged: still v2.3.
$ echo $?
1
$ docker images tiffingo --format '{{.Repository}}:{{.Tag}}'
tiffingo:2.3
tiffingo:cdf64da          # no tiffingo:2.4: the build never ran, the migration never ran
Slide idea

"Each step's exit code says pass or fail. One failure stops the job." A failed gate means the later stages never run: no build, no migration, no switch.

Demo 2 (slide 19): percentage rollout with sticky buckets

Each step runs docker run --rm -v "$PWD":/demo -w /demo node:22-alpine node rollout.js <cmd>. If you have Node, node rollout.js <cmd> gives the same output.

sha256("group_order:" + user) .readUInt32BE(0) % 100 < rollout_percent
  1. hash the flag name and the user id together (same input → same hash, always)
  2. first 4 bytes as a number, 0 to 4,294,967,295
  3. remainder → bucket 0..99
  4. ON if the bucket is below the percentage read from flags.json (on every call)

Steps 1–3 · Where the flag lives, the function, five users

$ cat flags.json
{
  "group_order": { "rollout_percent": 10 }
}
$ node rollout.js explain asha ravi meena kabir zoya
flags.json says group_order.rollout_percent = 10, so ON means bucket < 10
user      sha256("group_order:" + user)   first 4 bytes as a number   % 100   ON?
asha      1c386e8f...                        473,460,367             67   off (67 >= 10)
ravi      9ad54706...                      2,597,668,614             14   off (14 >= 10)
meena     5d1c93b1...                      1,562,153,905              5   ON  (5 < 10)
kabir     443daf98...                      1,144,893,336             36   off (36 >= 10)
zoya      f6b7a02e...                      4,139,229,230             30   off (30 >= 10)

Steps 4–5 · Is it fair?

$ node rollout.js spread
Smallest bucket: 72 users (-28% from average). Largest: 133 (+33%). Average: 100.
Buckets 0 to 9: 1,005 users = 10.05% of 10,000.
$ node rollout.js spread 1000000
Smallest bucket: 9,705 users (-3% from average). Largest: 10,238 (+2%). Average: 10,000.
Buckets 0 to 9: 100,478 users = 10.05% of 1,000,000.

Notice: single buckets wobble, but ten together come out close to 10%, and closer with more users. It is about 10%, never exactly.

Step 6 · Why not Math.random()? (three runs; the random row changes every run)

$ node rollout.js random asha
Math.random() < 0.10:               off off off off off off ON  off off off
bucket("group_order", "asha") < 10: off off off off off off off off off off
Math.random() < 0.10:               off off ON  off ON  off ON  off off off
bucket("group_order", "asha") < 10: off off off off off off off off off off

Notice: with random() asha's button appears and disappears. The hash gives the same answer every time.

Steps 7–8 · Roll out 10 → 50 → 100, then kill switch to 0

$ node rollout.js rollout
rollout_percent = 10: 1,005 of 10,000 users ON (10.1%)
$ echo '{ "group_order": { "rollout_percent": 50 } }' > flags.json
$ node rollout.js rollout
rollout_percent = 50: 4,957 of 10,000 users ON (49.6%)
Compared with 10%:   stayed ON: 1,005   newly ON: 3,952   turned off: 0
$ echo '{ "group_order": { "rollout_percent": 100 } }' > flags.json
$ node rollout.js rollout
rollout_percent = 100: 10,000 of 10,000 users ON (100.0%)
Compared with 50%:   stayed ON: 4,957   newly ON: 5,043   turned off: 0
$ echo '{ "group_order": { "rollout_percent": 0 } }' > flags.json
$ node rollout.js rollout
rollout_percent = 0: 0 of 10,000 users ON (0.0%)
Compared with 100%:  stayed ON: 0   newly ON: 0   turned off: 10,000

Notice: going up, turned off is always 0. Buckets don't move, only the line does. 0% is the kill switch: one edit, no deploy.

Step 9 · Why the flag name is inside the hash

$ node rollout.js overlap
hash(flag name + user id):  group_order 1005, new_menu 1016, in both: 99
hash(user id) only:         group_order 978, new_menu 978, in both: 978
Without the flag name, the same 1,000 people test every new feature.
Slide idea

"Percentage rollout uses hash(user_id) % 100 < 10, not random() < 0.1." Deterministic means sticky. A higher percentage only adds users. Salting with the flag name gives each flag an independent 10% (overlap ≈ 10% × 10% = 1%).

Demo moment → slide idea

You sawIt proves
tiffingo:2.3 = tiffingo:cdf64da, same IMAGE IDbuild once, tag with the commit id, promote the same image
Pipeline stopped. Production is unchanged: still v2.3.gated pipeline: exit code ≠ 0 stops everything after it
[3/6] Migrate runs before [4/6] Deploy; v2.3 fails during the rename deploymigrate before deploy; migrations must work with the old code
v2.4 passes tests + /healthz, 1 in 5 checkouts fail/healthz = SELECT 1 (readiness), tests cover pricing only: gates catch only what they check
rollback.sh: 3 stages, "nothing to test, build or migrate"redeploy the previous image, fast; it never touches the DB

flag.sh off, same container, errors stop next requestkill switch, seconds, no deploy
rollout 10 → 50 → 100: turned off 0sticky buckets, gradual rollout
overlap 99 vs 978salt the hash with the flag name
Trap

traffic.sh is a load generator, not a traffic splitter. The proxy always points at exactly one container (blue-green switch). The demos have no canary or weighted split; that is only on the slides.

Predict v2.3 is live, kabir sends group=1

Before step 4, curl -s 'localhost:8080/checkout?customer=kabir&group=1'. What comes back?

Show answer
ok     order #5  Rs 240  single  (v2.3)

v2.3's checkout ignores group completely: every order is a single "paneer thali, lassi" = Rs 240. The crash only exists from v2.4 on.

Predict Deploy 2.4 when a test fails

v2.3 is live. Someone sets "paneer thali": 18 in pricing.js and runs ./deploy.sh 2.4. What do /healthz and docker images tiffingo show afterwards? Is the group_id column there?

Show answer
Pipeline stopped. Production is unchanged: still v2.3.
{"status":"ok","version":"2.3"}
tiffingo:2.3   tiffingo:cdf64da      (no 2.4 image)

Stage 1 fails (2 of 3 tests), so build, migrate, deploy and switch never run. No image, no group_id column, no new schema_migrations row.

Predict Rollback after the additive migration

v2.4 is live (its migration added group_id). You run ./rollback.sh 2.3. (a) Does checkout work? (b) Is group_id still in orders? (c) What does schema_migrations list?

Show answer

(a) Yes, all green: v2.3's INSERT names its columns and ignores the nullable group_id. (b) Yes. (c) 2.3 and 2.4. rollback.sh only starts an old image and switches Caddy. It never runs SQL.

Predict Healthy but broken

After ./deploy.sh 2.4-rename then ./rollback.sh 2.3: what does /healthz return, what fraction of checkouts fail, and what is the fastest fix that does not deploy anything?

Show answer
{"status":"ok","version":"2.3"}
ERROR  column "items" of relation "orders" does not exist  (v2.3)    ← 100% of checkouts
$ docker exec t5-db psql -U postgres -c 'ALTER TABLE orders RENAME COLUMN order_items TO items;'

/healthz only runs SELECT 1, so it can't see the missing column. Every checkout (not just 1 in 5) fails, because every INSERT uses items. Fix forward by renaming the column back.

Predict Flag ON, who fails?

v2.5 is live and you ran ./flag.sh on. What do these return: (1) customer=asha&group=1, (2) customer=kabir&group=1, (3) customer=kabir&group=0? After ./flag.sh off, how many containers were restarted?

Show answer

(1) ok … Rs 540 group (v2.5): asha has a saved group of 3 thalis. (2) ERROR Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'map') (v2.5). (3) ok … Rs 240 single: the flag is only read when group=1 (group && await flagOn(...)). Zero containers restarted: docker ps shows the same tiffingo-2-5-… container.

Predict Rollout from 10% to 15%

Buckets for group_order: asha 67, ravi 14, meena 5, kabir 36, zoya 30. flags.json goes from 10 to 15. Who is ON before and after? Then a new flag new_menu is set to 10%: is meena guaranteed to be ON for it?

Show answer

At 10%: only meena (5). At 15%: meena and ravi (14 < 15). Nobody turns off when the percentage goes up. For new_menu, no guarantee: the hash input is "new_menu:meena", so her bucket is different. That's why the overlap of two 10% flags is about 1%, not 10%.

Cheat sheet

  • ./preflight.sh smoke pull images, pre-build, check 8080, test once
  • ./setup.sh network tiffingo + t5-db + t5-proxy on :8080
  • ./deploy.sh 2.4 Test → Build (2 tags) → Migrate → Deploy beside old → Health → Switch
  • ./rollback.sh 2.3 start old image → health → switch. No test, build or migrate. DB untouched.
  • ./flag.sh on|off = UPDATE flags SET enabled = … WHERE name = 'group_order';
  • curl -s localhost:8080/healthz version + SELECT 1 only
  • docker images tiffingo same IMAGE ID for :2.3 and :cdf64da
  • docker exec t5-db psql -U postgres -c '\d orders' see the real columns
  • docker exec t5-db psql -U postgres -c 'ALTER TABLE orders RENAME COLUMN order_items TO items;' fix forward
  • Safe while old code runs: ADD COLUMN (nullable), CREATE TABLE. Unsafe: RENAME, DROP.
  • Expand/contract: add new → write both → backfill → read new → stop using old → DROP old (irreversible)
  • Three ways back: revert + pipeline (minutes) · redeploy previous image (~1 min) · flag off (seconds)
  • bucket = sha256(flag + ":" + user)[0..4] % 100, ON if bucket < percent
  • Raise the percent → nobody turns off. 0% = kill switch. Flag name in the hash → independent groups.
  • ./reset-all.sh remove everything the demos created
Question bank

Theory question bank (quiz 2 syllabus)

About 85 new multiple-choice questions in the quiz style (a scenario, then "choose all that apply" or one answer), each with the reason every option is right or wrong.

How to use this

Answer before you open. Write down your letters first, then click Show answer. If you open it first, you only learn to recognise the answer. You don't learn to produce it.

For "choose all that apply", look at each option on its own and decide true or false. Several options can be right, and the wrong ones are common misconceptions.

The explanation says why each wrong option is wrong. Read it even when you got the question right.

Levels: Drill = recall, Quiz-style = quiz level, Twist = harder than the quiz, or mixes two topics.

What the quiz covers

Quiz 2A tested git internals and rebase (T2L1), testing and CI (T2L2), containers (T3L1), database migrations (T3L2) and architecture (T4L1). This bank gives those topics the most questions. The last question set before the rapid-fire facts is a short review of the earlier lectures (T0/T1), because ideas like the four locations, ports and secrets come up again inside the later topics.

Git internals: objects, hashes, diff and merge (T2L1)

Drill What a blob knows

A repo has src/app.py and backup/app.py, and the two files have exactly the same bytes. You then rename src/app.py to src/main.py and change nothing else. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. The rename creates a new blob, because the name is part of the blob.
  2. A blob stores only the file's bytes. It has no file name or path.
  3. The two identical files are stored as one blob.
  4. The file name lives in the tree object that points to the blob.
Show answer

B, C, D. A blob's id is a hash of its content alone, so identical bytes give the same id, and Git never stores the same bytes twice. Names live one level up, in a tree (100644 blob 3f9a1c2 app.py). After the rename, the tree changes and so the commit is new, but the blob is reused. A is wrong because the name is not inside the blob.

Drill What is inside a commit object?

git cat-file -p HEAD prints a commit object. Which of these does a commit object store? Choose all that apply.

  1. The hash of its parent commit(s)
  2. Author, committer, timestamps and the message
  3. The name of the branch it was made on
  4. The hash of one tree (the snapshot of the whole project)
  5. A diff of the lines that changed since the parent
Show answer

A, B, D. A commit is tree + parent + author/committer + message. It is a full snapshot, not a diff, so E is wrong: git diff works out the changes each time you ask. Branch names are not stored in commits (C). A branch is a separate file that points to a commit.

Quiz-style Two branches, one commit, then a commit

main and feature both point to commit c41a2e0. You are on feature and you make one commit, 9d03b7f. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. Git copied all project files into a new folder for feature when the branch was created.
  2. The parent of 9d03b7f is c41a2e0.
  3. .git/refs/heads/feature is overwritten with the hash 9d03b7f….
  4. main also moves, because the two branches shared a commit.
  5. .git/refs/heads/main still holds c41a2e0….
Show answer

B, C, E. A branch is a small text file holding one commit hash. Committing rewrites only the file of the branch HEAD points to. Branches are separate pointers, not containers, so pointing at the same commit doesn't tie them together (D). Making a branch writes one pointer file and copies no files (A). That is why branching is instant.

Quiz-style Amend only the message

You just made commit 5e1f0aa "Add lgoin form". You run git commit --amend -m "Add login form" and change no files. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. 5e1f0aa is still listed in git reflog.
  2. 5e1f0aa is deleted from .git/objects at once.
  3. The commit gets a new ID, because the message is part of the hashed content.
  4. The new commit points to the same tree as 5e1f0aa.
Show answer

A, C, D. The ID is a hash of the tree, parent, metadata and message, so a new message means a new commit. No file changed, so the tree hash is identical. The old commit is now unreachable from any branch, but it is still in the object database and in the reflog. It only disappears when git gc prunes it after the grace period, so B is wrong.

Twist Fix one old message: which IDs change? 
d4e5f60 (HEAD -> main) Add tests
c3d4e5f Add search route
b2c3d4e Add databse layer
a1b2c3d Initial commit

You use an interactive rebase to change only the message of b2c3d4e to "Add database layer". Which commits on main get a new ID?

  1. None, because no file content changed
  2. All four, because rebase rewrites the whole branch
  3. That commit and every commit after it ("Add search route" and "Add tests")
  4. Only the commit whose message changed
Show answer

C. Each commit stores its parent's hash. A new message gives "Add database layer" a new ID. Then "Add search route" has a new parent hash, so it gets a new ID too, and the change carries on to the tip. That is why rewriting history changes every later ID. "Initial commit" is not touched, so B is wrong. A is wrong because the message is hashed as well as the files.

Quiz-style One byte flipped on disk

A disk error flips one byte inside a blob file under .git/objects. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. Git can detect it, because the content no longer hashes to the object's name.
  2. The content still exists but the address is wrong, so the object can no longer be trusted.
  3. Git can't notice, because objects are looked up by file name.
  4. Content addressing is the same idea used for CDN cache keys and Merkle trees.
Show answer

A, B, D. An object's name is the hash of its content (id = SHA1(content)), so a change of one byte breaks the match and git fsck reports it. Git looks everything up by hash, not by file name (C). Because the name is derived from the content, you can check data without trusting whoever gave it to you.

Quiz-style Predict the diff

The committed steps.txt has 4 lines: a, b, c, d. You delete line b. What does git diff show (ignoring the header)?

  1. Nothing. Git stores diffs only for commits, not for the working tree.
  2. -a
-b
-c
-d
+a
+c
+d
  3.  a
-b
 c
 d
  4.  a
-b
+c
-c
 d
Show answer

C. Myers finds the shortest edit script: one deletion, with the matching lines kept as context (diagonal moves cost 0). B is a valid edit script, but it is 7 edits, not the shortest. D makes no sense. A is wrong: git diff computes the change between the working tree and the staging area on demand.

Twist Two shortest scripts: which one does Myers pick?

Old file: x, y. New file: y, x. Two edit scripts of length 2 exist: (1) delete x, keep y, insert x; (2) insert y, keep x, delete y. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. A replace-all script (delete both, insert both, d = 4) is equally good because the result is the same.
  2. Myers picks one at random each run, so the output can change.
  3. Both scripts are shortest (d = 2).
  4. Myers prefers deleting over inserting, so git shows -x, then y, then +x.
Show answer

C, D. When two scripts are equally short, Myers takes the deletion first. That gives the same output every run, so B is wrong. A gives the same file but is not the shortest edit script, and that is the only thing Myers looks for.

Drill Why git diff is fast on huge, almost identical files

Two versions of a 200,000-line file differ in 3 lines. Why does git diff answer at once?

  1. Git compares only the file sizes.
  2. Myers runs in O((m+n)·d) time, and d (the number of edits) is tiny here.
  3. Myers compares every line with every other line, O(m·n), and computers are fast.
  4. Git stored the 3-line diff when you committed.
Show answer

B. Myers's cost grows with the size of the edit script d. It follows the free diagonals (matching lines) and never walks one twice. Its memory is O(m+n). Git stores snapshots, not diffs (D). O(m·n log m) is roughly the older Hunt–McIlroy LCS approach (C). Sizes alone can't say which lines changed (A).

Quiz-style Why a three-way merge needs the base

You merge feature into main. Which statements about the three-way merge are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. A conflict means Git has crashed and the merge must be redone from scratch.
  2. Without the base, Git couldn't tell whether a line was added on one side or deleted on the other.
  3. If main edits line 10 and feature edits line 40, both changes are kept with no conflict.
  4. Git computes diff(base, main) and diff(base, feature) with the same diff engine.
  5. Git compares the two tips directly and makes a conflict wherever they differ.
Show answer

B, C, D. Two Myers edit scripts are made against the common ancestor. If they touch different regions, both apply cleanly. If both sides touched the same region, Git has two valid edits and no rule to choose, so it stops and asks you. That refusal is correct behaviour, not a crash (A). Comparing only the two tips (E) would make every difference look like a conflict.

Quiz-style Merge, rebase, squash: three claims about history

Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. A rebase keeps the original commit IDs and only moves the branch pointer.
  2. A merge keeps both timelines exactly as they happened, joined by a merge commit with two parents.
  3. A squash is a rebase whose commits are collapsed into one.
  4. A squash keeps each original commit on main, so git log still shows them one by one.
  5. A rebase replays your commits on a new base, which gives them new hashes and a straight line.
Show answer

B, C, E. Merge treats history as an accurate ledger. Rebase and squash treat it as a readable story. A replayed commit has a new parent, so it must get a new ID (A is wrong). A squash leaves one commit on main, and the individual commits are not part of main's history (D).

Twist Predict the graph after a rebase 
$ git log --graph --oneline --all
* 9c1d2e3 (HEAD -> feature) Add search box
* 7b2c3d4 Add search route
| * 4e5f6a7 (main) Fix footer typo
|/
* 1a0b2c3 Initial commit

You run git rebase main and there are no conflicts. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. The new copy of "Add search route" has 4e5f6a7 as its parent.
  2. git log --oneline feature now lists: Add search box, Add search route, Fix footer typo, Initial commit.
  3. main still points to 4e5f6a7.
  4. Running git switch main, then git merge feature, is now a fast-forward.
  5. 9c1d2e3 is still reachable from feature.
Show answer

A, B, C, D. Rebase copies the two feature commits on top of main's tip, so feature now contains the footer fix and is a straight line ahead of main. Main itself does not move. The old commits get new IDs, so 9c1d2e3 is no longer on feature (E is wrong). You can only find it through the reflog until gc. Since main is now an ancestor of feature, merging just moves main forward.

Quiz-style Delete an unmerged branch, then gc

Branch exp has two commits that are on no other branch. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. git branch -d exp refuses with "not fully merged".
  2. You can recover them with git branch exp <hash>, using a hash from git reflog.
  3. After git branch -D exp, the two commits are unreachable but still in the object database.
  4. git gc right after the delete removes them at once.
Show answer

A, B, C. Lowercase -d protects unmerged work, and capital -D forces the delete. Deleting a branch removes only the pointer file. The commits are unreachable but intact. They are also still referenced by the reflog, and gc only prunes them after its grace period (D is wrong). Pointing a new branch at the tip brings back both commits, because each commit links to its parent.

Remotes, undo and recovery (T0L1 + T2L1)

Drill Clean status, but teammates can't see it 
$ git status
On branch main
Your branch is ahead of 'origin/main' by 2 commits.
  (use "git push" to publish your local commits)

nothing to commit, working tree clean

Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. The working directory and the staging area disagree.
  2. The local repository and the remote disagree.
  3. git push sends the 2 commits and moves origin's main.
  4. git status just asked GitHub how many commits it has.
Show answer

B, C. "Working tree clean" means the first three locations agree. "Ahead by 2" compares your main with origin/main, which is your last downloaded copy of the remote. git status never contacts the server (D is wrong), so the count can be out of date until you git fetch. A is wrong because the tree is clean.

Quiz-style What fetch changes

You are on main with one local commit that isn't pushed and an unstaged edit to api.py. A teammate pushed 3 commits. You run git fetch origin. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. Your local commit was uploaded too.
  2. origin/main now includes the 3 commits.
  3. git status may now say your branch and 'origin/main' have diverged.
  4. Your main is unchanged and api.py keeps your edit.
Show answer

B, C, D. Fetch only downloads, and it updates only the remote-tracking pointer. You have 1 commit they don't have, and they have 3 you don't, so the branches have diverged (1 and 3). Uploading is git push (A).

Twist git pull refuses on a diverged branch 
$ git pull
hint: You have divergent branches and need to specify how to reconcile them.
...
fatal: Need to specify how to reconcile divergent branches.

Your push was rejected earlier because main and origin/main diverged. Which commands integrate the teammate's work so that your next push is accepted? Choose all that apply.

  1. git fetch, then git merge origin/main, then git push
  2. git pull --rebase, then git push
  3. git pull --no-rebase (merge), then git push
  4. git push --force
Show answer

A, B, C. Recent Git versions don't guess between merge and rebase when the branches have diverged and no pull.rebase setting exists. You have to pick one. C and A make a merge commit with both commits. B replays your commit on top of theirs (it gets a new ID) and gives a straight line. A force-push overwrites origin's main and erases the teammate's commit (D).

Quiz-style Unstage, but keep the edit

You ran git add config.py secrets.txt. You want secrets.txt out of the next commit, but the file must stay on disk unchanged. Which commands do that? Choose all that apply.

  1. git reset secrets.txt
  2. git restore --staged secrets.txt
  3. git restore secrets.txt
  4. git rm secrets.txt
Show answer

A, B. Both copy the HEAD version back into the staging area and leave the working directory alone. git restore <file> (without --staged) does the opposite: it overwrites the working copy with the staged copy and leaves it staged (C). git rm deletes the file from disk and stages the deletion (D). To keep the file out of git for good, add it to .gitignore as well.

Quiz-style Predict status after reset --soft
