c3 (HEAD -> main) Add retry logic   # changed net.py
c2 Add client
c1 Initial commit

Nothing is pushed and the tree is clean. You run git reset --soft HEAD~1. What does git status show?

  1. main is at c2, the tree is clean, and the retry change is gone from disk
  2. main is still at c3, with a new commit that undoes c3
  3. main is at c2, and net.py is under "Changes not staged for commit"
  4. main is at c2, and net.py is under "Changes to be committed"
Show answer

D. --soft moves only the branch pointer and keeps the staging area and files, so the change is staged and ready to commit again. C is --mixed (the default): pointer and staging area reset, files kept. A is --hard. B describes git revert.

Quiz-style A bad commit is already on shared main

Commit 8f2a1b0 on main broke checkout. It was pushed an hour ago, and three teammates have pulled since. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. git revert 8f2a1b0, then git push, is the safe undo.
  2. The revert needs no force-push, because it only adds a commit.
  3. After the revert, 8f2a1b0 is still in history, followed by a new commit that undoes it.
  4. git reset --hard HEAD~1 then git push --force is just as safe, because it gives a cleaner history.
Show answer

A, B, C. Revert undoes the change by adding history, so everyone's clones stay consistent and the push is a fast-forward. Reset and force-push rewrite shared history (D). Teammates who already have 8f2a1b0 would bring it back on their next push, and any commit pushed after it would be lost.

Twist Two commits made on a detached HEAD

You ran git checkout v1.0 (a tag), then made commit d1 and then d2 (which has d1 as its parent). Then you ran git switch main. You want a branch hotfix that contains both commits. Which commands work? Choose all that apply.

  1. git switch -c hotfix
  2. git branch hotfix <hash of d1>
  3. git branch hotfix <hash of d2>
  4. git branch hotfix HEAD@{1}
Show answer

C, D. A branch at d2 includes d1, because d2's parent is d1. In the reflog, HEAD@{0} is the switch to main, so HEAD@{1} is where HEAD was just before: d2. A branch at d1 loses d2 (B). switch -c creates the branch at your current commit, which is main's tip (A).

Twist You rebased a branch you had already pushed

Only you work on feature/search. You pushed it, then ran git rebase main. Now git push is rejected (non-fast-forward). Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. git pull then git push is the cleanest fix.
  2. The push is rejected because the rebased commits have new IDs, so origin's tip is not in your history any more.
  3. The same move on shared main would be dangerous.
  4. git push --force-with-lease is the right fix here.
Show answer

B, C, D. Rebase made copies, so the old tip on origin is no longer an ancestor of yours. Since nobody else uses the branch, replacing it is fine, and --force-with-lease refuses if origin moved since your last fetch. Pulling (A) would merge the old copies back in, so every commit would appear twice. On a shared branch, rewriting history breaks everyone else's clones.

Drill What stash keeps

You have an unstaged edit to app.py and a brand-new untracked file notes.md. You run git stash. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. notes.md stays in the folder, because untracked files are not stashed by default.
  2. The stash is pushed to origin so teammates can see it.
  3. app.py goes back to its last committed state.
  4. git stash pop re-applies the edit and drops that stash entry.
Show answer

A, C, D. A stash is a local "desk drawer" for tracked changes. It saves both staged and unstaged changes. Untracked files need git stash -u. Stashes are never pushed (B).

Quiz-style Inside a conflict 
$ git merge feature/pricing
CONFLICT (content): Merge conflict in price.py
$ cat price.py
<<<<<<< HEAD
TAX = 0.18
=======
TAX = 0.12
>>>>>>> feature/pricing

You are on main. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. TAX = 0.18 is main's version.
  2. git merge --abort returns to the state before the merge.
  3. Git chose 0.12 because the incoming branch always wins.
  4. After editing the file, git add price.py then git commit finishes the merge.
Show answer

A, B, D. The part between <<<<<<< HEAD and ======= is your checked-out branch. The part after it is the incoming branch. Staging the resolved file marks it resolved, and the commit records the merge with two parents (git merge --continue works too). Git chose nothing (C). Both sides changed the same line, so it shows both and stops.

Quiz-style .gitignore added too late

debug.log was committed last week. Today you add *.log to .gitignore, but git status still shows modified: debug.log. Which single step stops git from tracking it but keeps the file on disk?

  1. git rm --cached debug.log, then commit
  2. Nothing: wait, and .gitignore applies from the next commit
  3. rm debug.log
  4. git restore debug.log
Show answer

A. .gitignore only affects untracked files. Once a file is tracked, git keeps tracking it. rm --cached removes it from the staging area (so the next commit stops tracking it) and leaves it on disk. Old commits still contain it, which matters if it held a secret. D only throws away the current edits. C deletes the file and stages nothing.

Quiz-style commit -a and a new file

You edited the tracked file app.py and created a new file util.py. You run git commit -am "Add helper". What happens?

  1. The commit has only util.py.
  2. Git refuses, because an untracked file exists.
  3. The commit has the app.py change. util.py is still untracked.
  4. The commit has both files.
Show answer

C. -a stages changes to files git already tracks. A new file needs an explicit git add first. Untracked files never block a commit (B).

Testing and CI (T2L2)

Drill Which of these are integration tests?

Choose all that apply.

  1. A repository function saves a row to a real Postgres test database, then reads it back
  2. POST /api/users twice with the same email, against a disposable test database, expecting 409
  3. A browser opens /signup, creates an account, and lands on /dashboard
  4. calculate_gst(1000) returns 180
Show answer

A, B. Integration tests check real components working together: route, validation, ORM and database. D is a unit test (pure logic, no network or database). C is end-to-end (browser plus the whole stack). A duplicate-email check needs a real database to find the existing row, so it can't be a unit test.

Quiz-style Boundary cases for a range

Rule: "A username has 3 to 12 characters (inclusive)." Which input lengths are boundary cases? Choose all that apply.

  1. 7
  2. 13
  3. 2
  4. 3
  5. 12
Show answer

B, C, D, E. Each edge needs one test just outside it and one exactly on it: 2/3 at the bottom and 12/13 at the top. 7 is a normal (valid-class) case. It's worth having, but it is not on a boundary. Most real defects sit at these edges, such as > written where >= was meant.

Quiz-style 100% coverage, no assertion (pytest) 
def discount(total, is_member):
    if is_member and total > 100:
        return total * 0.9
    return total

def test_discount():
    discount(200, True)
    discount(50, False)

Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. If someone changed 0.9 to 0.5, this test would still pass.
  2. pytest marks this test as failed, because it contains no assert.
  3. Line coverage of discount is 100%.
  4. Adding assert discount(200, True) == 180 would catch the 0.5 change.
Show answer

A, C, D. Both paths of the if run, so every line executes. Coverage measures execution, not whether anything was checked. pytest passes a test that finishes without an exception, so B is wrong. That is exactly why this test proves nothing. An assertion on the result turns it into a behavioural claim.

Twist A mutant that survives 
# original
def can_vote(age): return age >= 18
# mutant made by the tool
def can_vote(age): return age > 18

def test_child(): assert can_vote(10) is False
def test_adult(): assert can_vote(30) is True

Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. A surviving mutant proves the original code has a bug.
  2. The mutant survives: both tests still pass against it.
  3. Adding assert can_vote(18) is True kills the mutant.
  4. Line coverage is 100%, so the suite is strong.
Show answer

B, C. 10 and 30 give the same answers under >= and >. Only the boundary value 18 tells them apart. A surviving mutant means the tests can't distinguish the change. It says nothing about a bug in the original code (A). Full coverage with a surviving mutant is exactly the blind spot coverage has (D).

Quiz-style Name the test double

(1) A class with save() and find() that keeps users in a Python dict. (2) A function that always returns the exchange rate 83.0. (3) An object the test checks with email.send.assert_called_once_with("a@x.com"). Which line names them correctly?

  1. (1) stub, (2) mock, (3) fake
  2. (1) fake, (2) stub, (3) mock
  3. (1) mock, (2) fake, (3) stub
  4. All three are mocks. The words mean the same thing.
Show answer

B. A fake is a working but simplified implementation (an in-memory database). A stub returns a fixed, canned value. A mock records how it was called, so the test can assert on that call. Use a double only at a slow, unsafe, costly or external boundary, never in place of the behaviour the test claims to prove.

Quiz-style Over-mocked signup test 
def test_register():
    repo = Mock()
    register(repo, {"email": "a@x.com", "password": "short"})
    repo.save.assert_called()

register is supposed to reject passwords under 8 characters. The test passes. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. The test passing shows register saves a user with a 5-character password, which is the bug.
  2. Replacing the Mock with a real production database would make it a better unit test.
  3. A behaviour-focused test would assert that the short password is rejected and that no user is stored.
  4. The test proves only that save was called, not that registration behaves correctly.
Show answer

A, C, D. The test asserts that an interaction happened, so it actually locks in the buggy behaviour. A good test states the rule: invalid input, then rejected, then nothing persisted (an in-memory fake makes the "nothing stored" part checkable). Automated tests must never use production data (B). The integration level uses a disposable test database.

Quiz-style Passes alone, fails in the suite

test_create_user inserts a@x.com into a shared test database and never cleans up. test_user_count asserts the table has 0 rows. Run alone, each passes. pytest on the whole file fails test_user_count. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. Good tests pass whether they run first or last.
  2. Each test should set up and clean up its own data (for example a fresh, disposable database per test).
  3. The tests are not independent: one leaves state that the other trips over.
  4. pytest runs tests in a random order by default, which caused this.
Show answer

A, B, C. Test order must not matter, and each test must clean up its own state. pytest runs tests in file order by default, not randomly (D). The order is what exposed the leftover row, but it did not cause it.

Twist Red only on weekends 
def test_shop_open():
    assert is_open(datetime.now()) is True

CI is green Monday to Friday and red on Saturday and Sunday. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. The test depends on the clock, which nothing controls, so it is not deterministic.
  2. Re-running the CI job until it is green is an acceptable fix.
  3. The fix is to pass fixed dates, e.g. is_open(datetime(2026, 10, 26, 10, 0)), and add a weekend case too.
  4. A flaky test weakens CI, because people learn to ignore a red status.
Show answer

A, C, D. Tests must give the same result every run. Clock, network and random values are only OK when the test controls them. Re-running hides the problem and keeps the test unreliable (B).

Quiz-style The two-commit fix

A seeded defect: is_valid_length("abcdefgh") returns False (8 characters should be valid). The milestone wants it fixed in two commits. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. A test written after the fix, which has only ever passed, might not detect the bug at all.
  2. Seeing the test fail first shows it really reproduces the bug.
  3. Commit 1 adds a test that fails against the current code. Commit 2 fixes the code and the test passes.
  4. CI should be green on both commits, otherwise the workflow is wrong.
Show answer

A, B, C. Reproduce, then red, then fix, then green. The failing test is evidence, in code, that you caught the defect. After that it guards against the bug coming back (a regression). Commit 1 is expected to be red (D).

Quiz-style Predict a workflow run 
on:
  pull_request:
    branches: [main]
jobs:
  test:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: actions/setup-python@v5
        with: { python-version: "3.12" }
      - run: pip install -r requirements.txt
      - run: ruff check .
      - run: pytest

A pull request into main has a lint error, so ruff check . exits with code 1. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. A direct push to main also triggers this workflow.
  2. The test job fails and the pull request shows a red check.
  3. The pytest step is skipped.
  4. pytest still runs, because every step is independent.
Show answer

B, C. Any step that exits non-zero fails the job, and later steps are skipped unless they say if: always(). Steps run in order on one runner, so D is wrong. The only trigger is pull_request, so a push to main does not run it (A). The course workflow lists both pull_request and push.

Quiz-style Works on my machine 
tests/test_fetch.py:1: in <module>
    import requests
E   ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'requests'
Error: Process completed with exit code 1.

pytest passes on your laptop. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. The CI runner starts clean and has only what the repository declares.
  2. The fix is to add the dependency to the declared requirements (and lockfile), push, and let CI run again.
  3. requests is installed on your laptop but missing from requirements.txt.
  4. Re-run the job: CI runners sometimes forget packages.
Show answer

A, B, C. This is the kind of mistake CI exists to catch: an undeclared package that was installed on your laptop long ago. Read the first meaningful error, find the command that failed, fix the declaration, push. A re-run (D) fails the same way, because the clean runner still lacks it.

Twist A green check with a failing test 
      - run: pytest
        continue-on-error: true

The CI job is a required status check on main. A pull request breaks a unit test. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. Branch protection reads the test results directly and still blocks the merge.
  2. The required check is green, so branch protection allows the merge.
  3. Removing continue-on-error makes the failing test block the merge again.
  4. The pytest step fails, but the job still finishes as successful.
Show answer

B, C, D. continue-on-error tells Actions to ignore that step's failure, so the job passes. Branch protection sees only the check's status, not the tests inside it (A). This setting quietly turns the gate off.

Quiz-style A database for integration tests in CI

The job declares services: test-db: image: postgres:16 with a health check, then runs the migrations and the integration tests. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. The schema should be applied (migrations run) before the integration tests.
  2. The service database exists only for this job and is thrown away when the job ends.
  3. Data seeded in one workflow run is still there in the next run, so seed it once.
  4. Its URL should come from a test-only environment variable, never one that looks like production.
Show answer

A, B, D. The integration-test lifecycle is: create a disposable environment, apply the schema, seed only the data needed, run the tests, discard. Every run starts fresh (C). That freshness is what makes the run reproducible.

Quiz-style An upside-down pyramid

A team tests every validation edge case with browser E2E tests. The suite takes 40 minutes and fails at random. Which advice matches the lecture? Choose all that apply.

  1. Delete the unit tests, because E2E tests give the broadest confidence.
  2. Move edge cases into unit tests: they are fast, isolated, and point at one function.
  3. Keep E2E tests for a few business-critical journeys (signup and login, checkout).
  4. The pyramid is a budget heuristic: many unit tests, some integration tests, few E2E tests.
Show answer

B, C, D. E2E tests are the slowest, most brittle and hardest to debug: a failure points at a whole journey. Each level gives different evidence at a different cost, so a balanced set beats maximising one level (A).

Containers and Docker (T3L1)

Drill Image, container, volume, VM

Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. A volume's lifecycle is independent of any container.
  2. A container is a running instance of an image, with its own thin writable layer.
  3. Each container boots its own kernel, like a VM.
  4. An image is an immutable, tagged set of filesystem layers plus metadata, including a default command.
  5. Three apps in three VMs on one host means three guest kernels.
Show answer

A, B, D, E. A container is one or more ordinary Linux processes, isolated by kernel features and sharing the host's kernel. That shared kernel is the security boundary. Only VMs have a guest kernel each (C is wrong).

Quiz-style Two containers, same port inside

Images a and b both run a server on port 3000. You run docker run -d -p 8080:3000 a, then docker run -d -p 8080:3000 b. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. Both servers can listen on 3000, because each container has its own network namespace.
  2. You must rebuild b to listen on another container port.
  3. -p 8081:3000 for b works, and the app needs no change.
  4. The second run fails because host port 8080 is already allocated.
Show answer

A, C, D. Inside, every container has its own network stack and its own localhost, so the same port is fine. Outside, a host port can be published only once. The mapping lives in the -p flag, so the app's own port never has to change (B).

Quiz-style PID 1 and what the container sees

40 processes run on the host. You run docker run -d --name w nginx. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. cgroups are what hide the host's 40 processes from it.
  2. Inside the container, its main process is PID 1.
  3. docker top w on the host shows the same process with an ordinary host PID.
  4. The mount namespace gives the container its own view of the filesystem tree.
Show answer

B, C, D. One process has two numbers: PID 1 in its PID namespace, and some other PID on the host. Namespaces control what a process can see (PID, mount, network, hostname, user). cgroups control how much it can use (A is wrong).

Quiz-style Over the memory limit

docker run -d --memory=256m --cpus=1 --name rep report:2.0. A bug makes the report generator load a 2 GB file into memory. What is the most likely result?

  1. The process is throttled and keeps running slowly, like with a CPU limit.
  2. The kernel may kill the process (an out-of-memory kill), and the container stops.
  3. Other containers on the host are killed to make room.
  4. Docker grows the limit to 2 GB automatically.
Show answer

B. cgroups count and cap resources. Going over the CPU limit means throttling (slower, still running). Going over the memory limit means the kernel may kill the process (A is wrong). A limit is a ceiling, not a target (D). The limit keeps this container from hurting its neighbours (C). Neighbours still share the kernel, disk and network.

Quiz-style Which steps rerun? (Python) 
FROM python:3.12-slim
WORKDIR /app
COPY requirements.txt .
RUN pip install -r requirements.txt
COPY . .
CMD ["python", "app.py"]

Since the last build on this machine, you added a package to requirements.txt and edited app.py. What rebuilds?

  1. Everything from FROM
  2. Only RUN pip install
  3. Only COPY . . and after
  4. COPY requirements.txt . and every step after it
Show answer

D. A changed input invalidates that step and every step after it. The first changed input is requirements.txt, so the install reruns, and COPY . . reruns too. FROM and WORKDIR come from the cache. C would be right if only app.py had changed.

Twist Editing the README reinstalls everything 
FROM node:24-alpine
WORKDIR /app
COPY . .
RUN npm ci --omit=dev
CMD ["node", "server.js"]

There is no .dockerignore. You fix a typo in README.md and rebuild. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. COPY . . misses the cache, because README.md is part of its input.
  2. A .dockerignore with node_modules, .git and .env keeps those out of the build context.
  3. Copying package.json package-lock.json first, then npm ci, then COPY . ., would let this rebuild reuse the install.
  4. npm ci runs again.
  5. Docker decides by file modification time, so running touch README.md with no edit would also rerun the steps.
Show answer

A, B, C, D. Every file in the context feeds COPY . ., so any edit invalidates it and everything after it. Docker's cache for COPY compares file contents (checksums), not timestamps (E is wrong). Copy the dependency manifests first, so source-only edits skip the install.

Quiz-style EXPOSE but no -p

The Dockerfile has EXPOSE 5000. You run docker run -d --name api api:1. docker ps shows PORTS 5000/tcp. curl localhost:5000 fails. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. 5000/tcp with no host address in docker ps means the port is published.
  2. EXPOSE only documents the port. Nothing was published.
  3. Running again with -p 5000:5000 fixes it.
  4. docker run -P (capital P) would publish every EXPOSEd port to a random host port.
Show answer

B, C, D. A published port looks like 0.0.0.0:5000->5000/tcp. A bare 5000/tcp means exposed, not published (A). -p creates the forwarding rule from the host into the container, and EXPOSE does not.

Quiz-style What went wrong: Flask in a container 
$ docker run -d -p 8000:5000 --name web flaskapp:1
$ docker logs web
 * Running on http://127.0.0.1:5000
$ curl localhost:8000
curl: (56) Recv failure: Connection reset by peer

The app calls app.run() with no arguments. What is the cause?

  1. The server listens on the container's own loopback (127.0.0.1), so traffic from the published port never reaches it. Use app.run(host="0.0.0.0").
  2. Port publishing does not work with -d.
  3. The host port and the container port must be the same number.
  4. The Dockerfile is missing EXPOSE 8000.
Show answer

A. Flask's default host is 127.0.0.1. Inside a container that is the container's own localhost, while forwarded traffic arrives on the container's network address. Mapping 8000 to 5000 is fine (C). EXPOSE is documentation only (D). -d doesn't matter (B). You must rebuild the image and run a new container, because a restart reuses the old image.

Twist Published to 127.0.0.1 only

docker run -d -p 127.0.0.1:18080:3000 web:1. The server inside listens on 0.0.0.0:3000. Your laptop's LAN address is 192.168.1.20. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. curl http://127.0.0.1:3000 on your laptop also works.
  2. Without the 127.0.0.1: prefix, port 18080 would open on every network interface.
  3. curl http://127.0.0.1:18080 on your laptop works.
  4. A classmate on the same Wi-Fi can't reach http://192.168.1.20:18080.
Show answer

B, C, D. The address before the host port says where on the host the port opens, and 127.0.0.1 means this machine only. Container port 3000 was never published on the host (A).

Quiz-style stop/start versus rm/run, with no volume

The counter app writes /data/count.txt and no volume is mounted. You send 2 requests (count=2). Then: docker stop c, docker start c, one request. Then docker rm -f c, the same docker run again, one request. What two counts come back?

  1. 1, then 1
  2. 3, then 1
  3. 3, then 4
  4. 3, then an error because /data is gone
Show answer

B. Stop and start reuse the same container and writable layer, so the count continues to 3. rm deletes the container with its writable layer. A new run from the image starts with an empty layer, and the app creates /data again and counts 1. Only a volume (or a bind mount) would give 4.

Twist What went wrong: the volume didn't save the count 
$ docker volume create t3data
$ docker run -d --name c -p 8080:3000 -v t3data:/tmp counter:1.0
$ curl localhost:8080      # count=1
$ curl localhost:8080      # count=2
$ docker rm -f c
$ docker run -d --name c -p 8080:3000 -v t3data:/tmp counter:1.0
$ curl localhost:8080      # count=1  ?!

The app writes /data/count.txt. Why did the count reset?

  1. You must run docker volume create again before each run.
  2. Named volumes are deleted with docker rm -f.
  3. Volumes only keep data after docker stop, not after rm.
  4. The volume is mounted at /tmp, but the app writes to /data, which sits in the deleted writable layer.
Show answer

D. Only the path given after : lives in the volume. Mount it with -v t3data:/data. A named volume survives docker rm -f (B, C), and it is removed only by docker volume rm. A isn't needed: one create is enough, and -v name:/path even creates it if it is missing.

Quiz-style Bind mount versus named volume

You run the counter with --mount type=bind,src="$(pwd)"/data,dst=/data and send 3 requests. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. A bind mount is useful when the host path itself matters, e.g. in development.
  2. After docker rm -f and a new run with the same mount, the next request gives count=4.
  3. ./data/count.txt on your laptop now contains 3.
  4. docker volume ls lists this mount as a named volume.
Show answer

A, B, C. A bind mount maps a specific host folder into the container, so the file lives on your disk and survives the container. It is not Docker-managed storage, so it does not show in docker volume ls (D). A named volume is managed by Docker and is what you use for persistent application data.

Quiz-style Rebuilt, restarted, still old

web runs from app:1. You edit server.js, run docker build -t app:1 ., then docker restart web. The old behaviour is still there. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. The build failed silently, because the tag already existed.
  2. docker restart always pulls the newest image with that tag.
  3. The fix is docker rm -f web, then docker run again with the same options.
  4. A container stays tied to the image it was created from. A restart doesn't switch to the new build.
Show answer

C, D. The tag app:1 now names the new image, but web was created from the old one and keeps it. Restart reuses the same container (B). Re-tagging is normal and doesn't fail (A). Any data in the old container's writable layer is lost on rm unless it lives in a volume.

Twist Multi-stage build 
FROM node:24-alpine AS build
WORKDIR /app
COPY package.json package-lock.json tsconfig.json ./
RUN npm ci
COPY src ./src
RUN npx tsc
FROM node:24-alpine
WORKDIR /app
COPY package.json package-lock.json ./
RUN npm ci --omit=dev
COPY --from=build /app/dist ./dist
CMD ["node", "dist/server.js"]

Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. The final image also contains every layer of the build stage.
  2. The final image has no TypeScript compiler and no src/.
  3. COPY --from=build carries only the compiled output across.
  4. Fewer tools in the production image means less attack surface.
Show answer

B, C, D. The image is built from the last stage only. Earlier stages are just a place to compile. Dev dependencies, compilers and source add size and risk with no benefit when the app runs (A is wrong).

Drill What does :latest mean?

In March you run docker build -t shop:latest .. In May you build newer code with docker build -t shop:2.0 .. Today you run docker run shop. Which image runs?

  1. The May build, because latest always means the newest image.
  2. Docker stops and asks which tag you want.
  3. The March build. docker run shop means shop:latest, and that tag still points at the March image.
  4. It fails, because you gave no tag.
Show answer

C. A tag is just a movable name for one image. With no tag, Docker uses the tag called latest. Docker does not keep latest pointing at your newest build (A). It moves only when something builds or pulls with that exact tag. No tag is not an error (B, D). Use explicit version tags in anything you deploy.

Drill One container deletes a file from the image

The image cfg:1 contains /app/config/default.json. Containers c1 and c2 both run from it. Inside c1 you run rm /app/config/default.json. Which statement is true?

  1. c2 loses the file too, because both containers share the same image layer.
  2. The delete is recorded only in c1's own writable layer. c2, and any new container from cfg:1, still see the file. docker diff c1 lists it as deleted.
  3. The image cfg:1 is changed, so new containers won't have the file.
  4. The rm fails, because image layers are read-only.
Show answer

B. This is copy-on-write. The image layers are read-only and shared, so no container can change what another one sees (A, C). The change, even a delete, goes into the container's private writable layer, so from inside c1 the rm works (D). docker diff c1 shows D /app/config/default.json.

Drill docker logs is empty

A container has run for an hour. The app writes all its log lines to /var/log/app.log inside the container. docker logs api prints nothing. Why?

  1. Logs show only for containers started with -it.
  2. Logs appear only after the container stops.
  3. docker logs needs a volume mounted at /var/log.
  4. docker logs shows only what the main process writes to stdout and stderr.
Show answer

D. Docker captures the two output streams of the main process, and nothing else. A file inside the container is invisible to docker logs, and it sits in the writable layer, so rm deletes it. Make the app print to stdout (e.g. console.log). -it, stopping, and volumes don't change what is captured (A, B, C).

Quiz-style What the build can see 
monorepo/
  shared/utils.js
  api/
    Dockerfile
    .dockerignore      # contains: .env and node_modules
    .env
    server.js

Inside api/ you run docker build -t api:1 .. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. .dockerignore only affects COPY . .. A COPY that names the file directly still works.
  2. COPY ../shared/utils.js ./ fails: the build can only see files inside the build context, the . folder.
  3. COPY .env ./ fails with "not found", because .dockerignore removed .env from the context.
  4. COPY . . puts server.js, and also the Dockerfile and .dockerignore, into the image, but not .env.
  5. Without the .dockerignore, COPY . . would also copy .env and your local node_modules into the image.
Show answer

B, C, D, E. The last argument of docker build is the build context, and COPY can only read from it. Paths outside it come back as "not found" (B). .dockerignore removes files from the context itself, so no COPY can see them, whichever way you name them (C, so A is wrong). Anything not ignored is copied by COPY . ., including the Dockerfile.

Quiz-style Bumping an ENV line 
FROM node:24-alpine
WORKDIR /app
COPY package.json package-lock.json ./
ENV APP_VERSION=1.4
RUN npm ci --omit=dev
COPY server.js ./
CMD ["node", "server.js"]

You change only APP_VERSION=1.4 to 1.5 and rebuild on the same machine. What runs again?

  1. Nothing. ENV is only metadata, like CMD.
  2. RUN npm ci and COPY server.js: every step after the ENV line.
  3. Only COPY server.js.
  4. Everything from FROM.
Show answer

B. ENV changes the environment of every later step, so it is part of their input. A changed input invalidates the steps after it (A is wrong). FROM, WORKDIR and the first COPY come before it and stay cached (D). Move the ENV below npm ci, or better, pass it at run time with -e. That way a version bump doesn't reinstall dependencies.

Quiz-style ENTRYPOINT plus CMD 
FROM alpine:3.24
ENTRYPOINT ["echo", "hello"]
CMD ["world"]

You build it as greet and run three commands: (1) docker run greet, (2) docker run greet class, (3) docker run greet echo bye. What do they print?

  1. (1) hello world (2) an error, "class" not found (3) bye
  2. (1) hello world (2) hello world class (3) hello world echo bye
  3. (1) hello world (2) hello class (3) hello echo bye
  4. (1) world (2) class (3) bye
Show answer

C. ENTRYPOINT is the fixed program. CMD is only its default arguments. Words after the image name replace CMD (not add to it, B) and are passed to the ENTRYPOINT. Option A is what happens with no ENTRYPOINT and CMD ["echo", "hello", "world"]: then your words replace the whole command. To change an ENTRYPOINT you need --entrypoint.

Quiz-style Reading exit codes 
$ docker ps -a --format '{{.Names}}  {{.Status}}'
job     Exited (0) 2 minutes ago
api     Exited (1) 5 minutes ago
report  Exited (137) 1 minute ago
web     Exited (143) 3 minutes ago

Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. job finished normally. Exit code 0 means success.
  2. api's own code exited with an error. docker logs api is the first place to look.
  3. 137 always means the container ran out of memory.
  4. 137 = 128 + 9: report was killed with SIGKILL. That happens after an out-of-memory kill, or when docker stop runs out of patience.
  5. 143 = 128 + 15: web ended because of SIGTERM, for example from docker stop.
Show answer

A, B, D, E. The status shows the main process's exit code. A code above 128 means "killed by signal (code − 128)". 137 has two common causes (C is wrong). docker inspect -f '{{.State.OOMKilled}}' report prints true only for the memory case.

Quiz-style Which containers keep restarting? 
$ docker run -d --name a --restart on-failure     alpine:3.24 sh -c 'sleep 1; exit 1'
$ docker run -d --name b --restart on-failure     alpine:3.24 sh -c 'sleep 1; exit 0'
$ docker run -d --name c --restart unless-stopped alpine:3.24 sh -c 'sleep 1; exit 0'
$ docker run -d --name d                          alpine:3.24 sh -c 'sleep 1; exit 1'

A minute later, which containers is Docker still restarting over and over?

  1. a only
  2. a, c and d
  3. All four
  4. a and c
Show answer

D. on-failure restarts only after a non-zero exit, so a restarts and b (exit 0) stays stopped. unless-stopped restarts whatever the exit code, so c restarts too. The default policy is no, so d stays down. A manual docker stop does not trigger a restart under any of these policies.

Quiz-style What went wrong: "bad address" 
$ docker run -d --name users users:1          # listens on 0.0.0.0:3000
$ docker run --rm alpine:3.24 wget -qO- http://users:3000/
wget: bad address 'users:3000'

Neither command chose a network. What is the cause and the fix?

  1. On the default bridge network, containers can't find each other by name. Run docker network create shop and start both with --network shop. Then users resolves.
  2. users must be started with -p 3000:3000 before other containers can reach it.
  3. Container names only resolve with a .local suffix.
  4. Containers can never talk to each other directly. All traffic must go through the host.
Show answer

A. "bad address" means the name lookup failed. The server was never asked. On the default bridge the same request by IP address works, so nothing is blocked (D is wrong). Only user-defined networks give containers DNS by name. -p opens a port on the host, and containers don't need it to reach each other (B). C is made up.

Quiz-style What went wrong: the API can't find Postgres 
$ docker network create shop
$ docker run -d --name db --network shop -e POSTGRES_PASSWORD=dev -p 15432:5432 postgres:16-alpine
$ docker run -d --name api --network shop \
    -e DATABASE_URL=postgres://postgres:dev@localhost:15432/postgres api:1
$ docker logs api
Error: connect ECONNREFUSED 127.0.0.1:15432

Which DATABASE_URL host and port fix it?

  1. db:15432
  2. localhost:5432
  3. db:5432
  4. localhost:15432, plus EXPOSE 15432 in api's Dockerfile
Show answer

C. Inside api, localhost is the api container itself, and nothing listens there (B, D). On a shared user-defined network, api reaches db by name, on the port Postgres listens on inside its container: 5432. 15432 exists only on the host, from -p, so db:15432 is refused (A). EXPOSE never opens anything.

Quiz-style One image, many environments

The Dockerfile has ENV MODE=dev. prod.env contains MODE=prod and DB_PASSWORD=…. You run docker run -d --name app --env-file prod.env app:1. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. docker inspect app does not show DB_PASSWORD.
  2. The app sees MODE=prod. A run-time value overrides the image's ENV default.
  3. The same image can run in dev, staging and prod with a different env file each. No rebuild needed.
  4. If you edit prod.env and run docker restart app, the app sees the new values.
  5. The values in prod.env are not written into any image layer.
Show answer

B, C, E. Configuration is an input, not a constant: one build, many environments. But -e and --env-file values are stored in the container's settings, and docker inspect prints them (A is wrong), so anyone with Docker access on that host can read them. The file is read once, when the container is created. A restart keeps the old values (D), so recreate the container with rm + run.

Quiz-style What went wrong: the file is there but "not found" 
FROM alpine:3.24
COPY report /usr/local/bin/report      # compiled on your Ubuntu laptop
CMD ["report"]
$ docker run report:1
exec /usr/local/bin/report: no such file or directory
$ docker run --rm report:1 ls -l /usr/local/bin/report
-rwxr-xr-x    1 root  root  16048 ...  /usr/local/bin/report

The file exists and is executable. What is the most likely cause?

  1. The file isn't executable.
  2. COPY placed the file somewhere other than /usr/local/bin.
  3. Alpine images can't run any compiled program.
  4. The binary was linked against glibc on Ubuntu. Alpine uses musl libc, so the loader the binary asks for doesn't exist in the image.
Show answer

D. A small base image is not the same user space as your laptop. The confusing "no such file" refers to the missing glibc loader, not to your file. A missing execute bit gives "permission denied" instead (A), and ls shows the path is right (B). Alpine runs binaries built for musl fine (C). Build inside the image (e.g. in a build stage) or use a glibc-based base such as a -slim image.

Twist Rebuilt in June, still March inside 
FROM alpine:3.24
RUN apk add --no-cache curl
RUN date > /built-at.txt
COPY app.sh /app.sh

You built this on 1 March. In May a security fix for curl was published. On 1 June you edit only app.sh and run docker build on the same machine. What is in the new image?

  1. The March curl and a March date. Only COPY app.sh runs again.
  2. The fixed curl, because apk add always contacts the network.
  3. The fixed curl and a June date, because any edit invalidates the whole build.
  4. The fixed curl, but a March date.
Show answer

A. A RUN step is cached by its instruction text and its inputs, not by what the command would return today. Nothing above COPY app.sh changed, so both RUN steps come from the cache and never run (B, C, D are wrong). docker build --no-cache --pull forces a fresh base and fresh installs. A clean CI machine without this cache would install the new curl, so the "same Dockerfile" can give two different images. Pin versions if that matters.

Twist What went wrong: docker stop takes 10 seconds 
FROM node:24-alpine
WORKDIR /app
COPY server.js ./
CMD ["node", "server.js"]      # plain HTTP server, no signal handlers
$ time docker stop web
web
real    0m10.4s
$ docker ps -a --filter name=web --format '{{.Status}}'
Exited (137) 5 seconds ago

Which explanation is correct?

  1. docker stop always waits 10 seconds before stopping anything.
  2. node is PID 1. The kernel doesn't apply the default "terminate" action of SIGTERM to PID 1, and the app has no handler, so SIGTERM is ignored. After 10 seconds Docker sends SIGKILL, which gives 137.
  3. 137 means the server ran out of memory while shutting down.
  4. Exec-form CMD blocks signals. The shell form CMD node server.js would stop at once.
Show answer

B. docker stop sends SIGTERM, waits (10 s by default), then sends SIGKILL. Add process.on("SIGTERM", …) to close the server and exit: then stop takes about a second and exits 0 (A is wrong). docker run --init also helps, because a tiny init becomes PID 1 and passes the signal on. 137 here is a kill from Docker, not memory (C). In node:24-alpine, the shell form still ends with node as PID 1, so it behaves the same (D).

Twist Deleted in a later step, still in the image 
FROM node:24-alpine
ARG NPM_TOKEN
ENV API_KEY=sk-live-123
WORKDIR /app
COPY package.json package-lock.json .env big-dataset.bin ./
RUN echo "//registry.npmjs.org/:_authToken=$NPM_TOKEN" > .npmrc && npm ci && rm .npmrc
RUN rm .env big-dataset.bin
CMD ["node", "server.js"]

You build with --build-arg NPM_TOKEN=npm_SECRET42 and push to a public registry. big-dataset.bin is 50 MB. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. docker history --no-trunc shows sk-live-123.
  2. ls -a /app in a container shows no .env, so the secret is safe.
  3. docker history --no-trunc shows npm_SECRET42.
  4. Anyone who pulls the image can recover .env from the COPY layer.
  5. The image is still about 50 MB bigger than without the dataset.
Show answer

A, C, D, E. Layers only add. RUN rm writes a new layer that hides the files, but the COPY layer below still holds them, bytes and size included. History records each ENV value, and each ARG value used by a RUN. A clean ls proves nothing (B). Keep .env out with .dockerignore, give secrets at run time with -e/--env-file, and rotate anything already pushed.

Twist Volume, bind mount, new image

The image cfg:1 contains /app/config/default.json (content: v1). The volume cfgvol doesn't exist yet. ./empty is an empty folder.

$ docker run --rm -v cfgvol:/app/config cfg:1 ls /app/config            # (1)
$ docker run --rm -v "$(pwd)"/empty:/app/config cfg:1 ls /app/config   # (2)
# rebuild as cfg:2: default.json now says v2, and there is a new file new.json
$ docker run --rm -v cfgvol:/app/config cfg:2 ls /app/config            # (3)

What do the three commands list?

  1. (1) default.json (2) nothing (3) default.json only, still v1
  2. (1) default.json (2) default.json (3) default.json new.json
  3. (1) nothing (2) nothing (3) default.json new.json
  4. (1) default.json (2) nothing (3) default.json new.json
Show answer

A. An empty named volume is filled with the image's files at that path the first time it is mounted (so not C). A bind mount does the opposite: the host folder covers the image's folder, so the empty folder hides default.json (B is wrong). In (3) the volume is no longer empty, so Docker leaves it alone. It keeps the old v1 file and never gets new.json (D). The volume, not the image, is the source of truth.

Twist One missing ./ and a :ro

You want the container to write into ./data on your laptop, but you type docker run -d --name w -v data:/data writer:1. Separately, you run another container with -v cfgvol:/app/config:ro. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. ./data on the laptop stays empty. data without a path is a volume name, so Docker silently created a named volume.
  2. docker volume ls now lists a volume called data.
  3. -v "$(pwd)"/data:/data, an absolute host path, makes it a bind mount.
  4. With :ro, writing to /app/config fails with "Read-only file system", while the rest of the container stays writable.
  5. Docker refuses -v data:/data, because no volume called data was created first.
Show answer

A, B, C, D. In -v X:/path, X is a host path only if it looks like one (starts with / or ./). A plain word is a volume name, and a missing volume is created for you with no warning (E is wrong). So the data went into Docker-managed storage, not your folder. :ro makes only that mount read-only.

Twist Same Dockerfile, six weeks apart 
FROM node:24-alpine
WORKDIR /app
COPY package.json ./
RUN npm install --omit=dev
COPY server.js ./
CMD ["node", "server.js"]

You build this today. A teammate's CI builds the same commit in six weeks on a clean runner. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. node:24-alpine is a tag that can move. The registry may point it at a newer image, so the two builds may start from different bases.
  2. FROM node:24-alpine@sha256:… (a digest) pins one exact base image.
  3. With no lockfile copied, npm install resolves the ^ ranges again and may install newer versions.
  4. Your laptop's build cache makes sure the teammate gets the same layers.
  5. Copying package-lock.json too and using npm ci installs exactly the locked tree. npm ci refuses to run without a lockfile.
Show answer

A, B, C, E. Same declared inputs give the same image, but a moving tag and a version range are inputs that change over time. A digest names image content, so it can't move. A lockfile pins the exact dependency tree. The build cache lives only on the machine that built it, and a clean CI runner starts empty (D).

Database migrations (T3L2)

Drill Rename with auto-sync on

A TypeORM app has synchronize: true. You rename the entity field bio to about and restart production, where 300,000 users have a bio. What happens?

  1. The column is renamed in place and the data is kept.
  2. The app refuses to start until you confirm the rename.
  3. Nothing changes until you run a migration.
  4. The bio column is dropped and a new, empty about column is added.
Show answer

D. Auto-sync compares two snapshots: the code says about, the database has bio, so it makes them match by dropping one column and adding the other. It has no way of knowing you meant the same column. Auto-sync (and prisma db push) is fine for local prototyping. Production needs versioned, reviewed migrations (prisma migrate deploy, flyway migrate).

Quiz-style What the runner applies 
migrations/                 history table
01_create_users.sql          01  2026-09-02
02_add_billing.sql           02  2026-09-09
03_add_full_name.sql
04_add_index.sql

You run the migration runner. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. It adds one history row for each migration that succeeds.
  2. A second run straight after does nothing ("schema is up to date").
  3. It runs all four files again, to be safe.
  4. It runs 03, then 04, in that order.
Show answer

A, B, D. The runner reads the history table, lists the files, and runs only the ones with no history row, in version order. Each one is followed by its own history row. Applied migrations never run again (C). Saving a file changes nothing until a runner executes it.

Quiz-style You fixed a typo in an applied migration 
ERROR: Validate failed: Migrations have failed validation
Migration checksum mismatch for migration version 02
-> Applied to database : 1993450877
-> Resolved locally    : 1305205389

Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. flyway repair would apply the edited SQL of 02 to the database.
  2. Someone edited 02 after it was applied, so its checksum no longer matches the history.
  3. The right fix is to put 02 back as it was and make the change in a new migration (03, or a timestamp).
  4. The deploy stopped before running any migration.
Show answer

B, C, D. The history is append-only and checksum-verified, so an applied migration must never change. Fix things with the next migration. repair only rewrites the stored checksum (and removes failed rows). It runs no SQL, so the edit would never reach this database (A), and databases that ran the old 02 would quietly differ.

Quiz-style What went wrong: a hotfix by hand

During an incident, someone ran CREATE INDEX idx_orders_user ON orders (user_id); by hand in psql on production. A week later, migration 07_index_orders.sql (the same statement) is deployed and the runner fails with relation "idx_orders_user" already exists. Why did the runner run 07?

  1. psql changes are undone when the session closes, so the index was gone.
  2. The checksum of 07 changed.
  3. SQL run by hand changes the schema but writes no history row, so 07 still looked pending.
  4. The runner always re-runs the newest migration.
Show answer

C. The runner knows only what is in its history table. The index really exists (A is wrong, because psql autocommits), but there is no row for 07. Options: drop the hand-made index and let the migration create it, or record 07 as applied without running it (Flyway: -skipExecutingMigrations=true -target=07). D and B describe nothing that happened.

Twist Runner killed before COMMIT 
BEGIN;
ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN full_name text;      -- ok
INSERT INTO migration_history (version) VALUES ('03');  -- ok
-- process killed here; COMMIT never sent

Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. The next run skips 03, because its history row was inserted.
  2. The next run executes 03 again from the start, and it succeeds.
  3. On a database where DDL commits implicitly (e.g. MySQL), the column could exist without a history row, and the next run would fail.
  4. On PostgreSQL, the ALTER and the history row are both rolled back, because DDL is transactional there.
Show answer

B, C, D. Uncommitted work vanishes when the session dies: the INSERT was never committed, so it doesn't count (A). How DDL behaves in transactions depends on the database, which is why the lecture warns about it. MySQL commits around each DDL statement, so you can end up with a column and no row.

Twist COMMIT after an error 
BEGIN;
ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN plan text;
UPDATE users SET plan = tier;      -- ERROR: column "tier" does not exist
COMMIT;

In psql on PostgreSQL, what is the state afterwards?

  1. No plan column: the failed transaction is rolled back, even though you typed COMMIT.
  2. The transaction stays open until you type ROLLBACK.
  3. The plan column exists, filled with NULL.
  4. The plan column exists and is filled from tier.
Show answer

A. After an error, the transaction is aborted and ignores further commands. A COMMIT on an aborted transaction ends it with a rollback (psql prints ROLLBACK). C is what you get without BEGIN: each statement autocommits, so the ALTER stays even though the UPDATE failed. The transaction boundary decides what commits together.

Quiz-style lock_timeout and the queue 
A: BEGIN; SELECT count(*) FROM orders;        -- finished, transaction left open
B: SET lock_timeout = '2s';
   ALTER TABLE orders ADD COLUMN note text;
C: SELECT * FROM orders WHERE id = 7;         -- sent after B
D: SELECT * FROM customers WHERE id = 1;

PostgreSQL 16. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. B waits for ACCESS EXCLUSIVE, because A still holds ACCESS SHARE on orders.
  2. C waits behind B while B is queued, even though A and C only read.
  3. After about 2 seconds B fails with "canceling statement due to lock timeout", and C then returns its row.
  4. D also waits, because the whole database is locked.
  5. lock_timeout also limits how long the ALTER may run once it has the lock.
Show answer

A, B, C. A table lock is held until the transaction ends, not just until the query finishes. C's ACCESS SHARE would be compatible with A, but it queues behind B's earlier, conflicting request. When B gives up, C can share with A. Only sessions using orders are affected (D). lock_timeout limits only the wait to get the lock (E).

Twist Which ADD COLUMN rewrites the table?

PostgreSQL 16, 40 million rows in events. Which statements rewrite every row? Choose all that apply.

  1. ALTER TABLE events ADD COLUMN seen_at timestamptz DEFAULT clock_timestamp();
  2. ALTER TABLE events ADD COLUMN note text;
  3. ALTER TABLE events ADD COLUMN ref uuid DEFAULT gen_random_uuid();
  4. ALTER TABLE events ADD COLUMN plan text DEFAULT 'free';
Show answer

A, C. Since PostgreSQL 11, a constant (non-volatile) default is stored in the catalogue, so B and D take milliseconds. A volatile default must give each row its own value, so every row is rewritten, which means a long ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock. Even the fast ones still need that lock briefly, so they can queue behind a long reader.

Quiz-style Down migration after a week of writes 
-- 12.up.sql
ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN phone text;
-- 12.down.sql
ALTER TABLE users DROP COLUMN phone;

12 ran in production a week ago, and 50,000 users have entered a phone number. You run 12.down.sql, then later 12.up.sql again. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. After the down, the column and every phone number are gone.
  2. A ROLLBACK could have undone the down, after it committed.
  3. Getting the numbers back needs a backup (restore).
  4. After the up runs again, phone is NULL for every row.
Show answer

A, C, D. A reverse migration is new SQL that runs after the earlier change committed. It can't bring back values that nothing saved. ROLLBACK only discards uncommitted changes (B). Code is reversible. Data often isn't.

Quiz-style Adding a NOT NULL column to a full table

orders on PostgreSQL has 3 rows. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. ALTER TABLE orders ADD COLUMN currency text NOT NULL; fails, because the existing rows would hold NULL.
  2. ALTER TABLE orders ADD COLUMN currency text NOT NULL DEFAULT 'INR'; succeeds, and the existing rows read 'INR'.
  3. The first statement would succeed on an empty table.
  4. The safe, no-downtime way for a live app is: add the column as nullable, have new writes supply it, backfill in batches, then enforce NOT NULL.
Show answer

A, B, C, D. All four are true. Postgres says column "currency" ... contains null values for A. A constant default fills old rows (fast on PG 11+). With no rows there is nothing to violate. D is the lecture's required-column pattern. It is the safe way when the right value differs per row, or when old code is still writing rows without the column.

Twist Order the required-column steps

You must make orders.currency required on a large live table with no downtime. Which order is right?

  1. Add nullable column, then deploy code that writes currency, then backfill in batches, then ADD CONSTRAINT ... CHECK (currency IS NOT NULL) NOT VALID, then VALIDATE CONSTRAINT, then SET NOT NULL, then drop the check
  2. Add nullable column, then backfill, then deploy code that writes currency, then SET NOT NULL
  3. SET NOT NULL first, then backfill, then deploy code
  4. Add the CHECK constraint (validated at once), then add the column, then backfill
Show answer

A. New writes must supply the value before the backfill, or old code keeps creating NULLs after it (B). NOT VALID checks new rows only. VALIDATE checks old rows while reads and writes continue. On PG 12+, the validated check lets SET NOT NULL skip the full scan, taking ACCESS EXCLUSIVE only briefly. C fails while NULLs exist. D refers to a column that doesn't exist yet.

Quiz-style Expand/contract: where it becomes irreversible

Renaming user_name to full_name during rolling deploys: 1 expand (add full_name, write both), 2 backfill, 3 transition (read full_name, still write both), 4 contract (drop user_name). Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. The backfill is a job, not a migration, so it gets no history row.
  2. Up to and including step 3, redeploying the previous app version is safe, because user_name is still current.
  3. Step 4 is where the change stops being a simple redeploy-to-undo: old code can no longer use user_name.
  4. A single RENAME COLUMN is fine, because the old instances restart within a minute.
Show answer

A, B, C. Because both columns are written until the end, every earlier step can be rolled back by redeploying the old code. After the drop, old code breaks, and a down migration would only re-add an empty column. A direct rename (D) breaks every old instance still running during the rollout.

Quiz-style Two branches, the same migration number

Asha's branch adds 05_add_coupons.sql. Ravi's branch adds 05_add_search_index.sql. Both pull requests are approved and merged. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. Timestamp versions (e.g. 202610271015_add_search_index.sql) avoid this without coordination.
  2. The migration tool refuses (Flyway: "Found more than one migration with version 05").
  3. The runner runs them in alphabetical order, so nothing goes wrong.
  4. Git merges both without a conflict, because the file names differ.
Show answer

A, B, D. The collision shows up in the tool, not in git. A version must be unique, so two 05s are an error, not something to sort (C). Timestamps are unique without any coordination and sort by when they were written. Fix it on your branch and never renumber a migration that is already applied on main.

Twist The wrong tab at 14:05

A nightly pg_dump runs at 02:00. At 14:05 someone runs DROP TABLE orders; on the production primary. A streaming replica is attached. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. WAL can't be replayed onto a logical dump (pg_dump).
  2. Restoring only the 02:00 dump loses about 12 hours of writes.
  3. RPO is the maximum acceptable data loss, and RTO is the maximum acceptable recovery time.
  4. Point-in-time recovery to 14:04:59 needs a physical base backup plus WAL archived continuously since that backup.
  5. The replica saves you, because it still has the table.
Show answer

A, B, C, D. Replication is not a backup: the replica copied the DROP within moments (E). WAL replay works only on a physical base backup, and only if WAL archiving was configured. You only know your real RTO once you have done a restore and timed it.

Drill What the history table does not keep

Flyway writes one row to flyway_schema_history for each migration it runs. Which of these is not stored in that row?

  1. installed_rank: the order in which migrations actually ran
  2. checksum: a hash of the script's contents
  3. success: whether the migration finished (1) or failed (0)
  4. The full SQL text of the script
Show answer

D. A row holds installed_rank, version, description, type, script (the file name), checksum, installed_by, installed_on, execution_time and success. The SQL itself lives only in the file. So if you delete an applied file, the history can't rebuild it. A, B and C are all real columns: A records the order things ran in, B detects edits, and C marks failures.

Drill Which order does Flyway run them? 
$ ls -l --time-style=+%Y migrations/
-rw-r--r--  1 asha  ...  2020  V10__c.sql
-rw-r--r--  1 asha  ...  2030  V1.9__e.sql
-rw-r--r--  1 asha  ...  2026  V2__b.sql
-rw-r--r--  1 asha  ...  2026  V1__a.sql
-rw-r--r--  1 asha  ...  2026  V1.10__d.sql

All five are pending on an empty database. In which order does flyway migrate run them?

  1. 1, 1.10, 1.9, 10, 2 (sorted as text)
  2. 10, 1, 2, 1.10, 1.9 (oldest file first)
  3. 1, 1.9, 1.10, 2, 10
  4. 1, 2, 10, 1.9, 1.10 (whole numbers first)
Show answer

C. Flyway compares versions as numbers, one dot-separated part at a time: 1.9 < 1.10 (9 < 10) < 2 < 10. Text sorting (A) would put 10 before 2. The file's date (B) plays no part: order comes only from the version in the name. Decimal versions are not handled differently from whole ones (D).

Drill info, validate, repair, migrate: what changes what?

Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. flyway repair runs the SQL of failed migrations again.
  2. flyway info and flyway validate only read. They never change the database.
  3. flyway validate passes when a new migration is still pending.
  4. flyway repair changes only the history table. It removes Failed rows, updates stored checksums and descriptions to match the local files, and marks applied migrations whose files are gone as deleted.
  5. flyway migrate validates first, then runs the pending SQL and adds one history row per migration.
Show answer

B, D, E. repair runs no migration SQL (A). It only edits history rows, so you must clean up any half-done changes yourself. A standalone flyway validate fails on a pending migration ("Detected resolved migration not applied to database: 2") (C). The validation inside migrate allows pending files, because running them is migrate's job.

Quiz-style Predict info: you edited a repeatable view 
$ flyway info
| Category   | Version | Description   | State   |
| Versioned  | 1       | create users  | Success |
| Versioned  | 2       | create orders | Success |
| Repeatable |         | active users  | Success |

You change the view in R__active_users.sql so it also shows city, and you add V3__add_city.sql. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. On migrate, R__active_users.sql runs before V3, because R files come first.
  2. Before migrate, flyway info shows the old repeatable row as Outdated and a second repeatable row as Pending.
  3. After migrate, the old repeatable row is deleted from the history table.
  4. If you later only touch the R file (new modified time, same content), the next migrate runs it again.
  5. After migrate, the old row shows Superseded and the new one Success. The history has two rows for R__active_users.sql, both with an empty version.
Show answer

B, E. Flyway runs all pending versioned migrations first and repeatables after them (A), which is why the view can already use city. History is append-only, so the old row stays and is shown as Superseded (C). A repeatable reruns only when its checksum changes, and the checksum is computed from the content, not the file date (D).

Quiz-style What belongs in a repeatable migration?

Your team uses V<n>__name.sql and R__name.sql files. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. CREATE OR REPLACE VIEW order_totals AS SELECT ...; is a good fit for an R file.
  2. ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN city text; is a good fit for an R file.
  3. In one flyway migrate, new or changed repeatables run after all pending versioned migrations.
  4. An R file must work whether the database has the old version of its object or none, because it runs again after every edit.
  5. Once an R file has been applied, you must never edit it. Each change needs a new R file.
Show answer

A, C, D. A repeatable has no version. It reruns every time its checksum changes, so it must be idempotent (CREATE OR REPLACE, or DROP VIEW IF EXISTS then CREATE). B fails on its second run, because the column already exists. Schema steps like that belong in a versioned file. E is the rule for versioned files. For a repeatable, editing the file is how you change it.

Quiz-style Predict info: a hotfix with a lower version 
history: V1 Success, V3 Success
you pull: V2__hotfix_status.sql, V4__add_notes.sql
$ flyway info     # state of V2 = ?
$ flyway migrate  # result = ?

No outOfOrder setting is configured. What happens?

  1. V2 shows Ignored. migrate fails validation and runs nothing, not even V4.
  2. V2 shows Pending. migrate runs V2, then V4.
  3. V2 shows Ignored. migrate skips it and runs only V4.
  4. V2 shows Out of Order. migrate runs V2, then V4.
Show answer

A. V2 is lower than the applied V3, so info calls it Ignored. migrate stops with "Detected resolved migration not applied to database: 2 ... To allow executing this migration, set -outOfOrder=true". B and C describe things Flyway refuses to do silently. Out of Order (D) is the state V2 gets only after you run flyway migrate -outOfOrder=true.

Twist Adopting Flyway on a hand-built database

shop.db was built by hand. It already has the tables that V1__a.sql and V2__b.sql create, plus real data, but no history table. V3__c.sql is new. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. A plain flyway migrate refuses: "Found non-empty schema(s) ... but no schema history table. Use baseline() or set baselineOnMigrate to true".
  2. flyway baseline with no options marks the database as version 2, because Flyway sees that both tables exist.
  3. The BASELINE row stores a checksum of the existing schema.
  4. After flyway baseline -baselineVersion=2, info shows V1 Below Baseline, V2 Ignored (Baseline), a Baseline row, and V3 Pending.
  5. flyway migrate -baselineOnMigrate=true with no baselineVersion baselines at 1, then runs V2, which fails because table b exists.
Show answer

A, D, E. Flyway never inspects your tables. The default baselineVersion is 1 (B), so you must state the real version yourself. That is the trap in E. The BASELINE row is only a marker ("everything up to here was already there"). Its checksum is empty (C). After baselining at 2, only V3 runs.

Quiz-style What went wrong: the clean-up PR

A teammate's "tidy up" PR deleted V2__add_billing.sql because "it ran everywhere ages ago". V1 and V3–V9 are still there. The next production deploy fails:

ERROR: Validate failed: Migrations have failed validation
Detected applied migration not resolved locally: 2.
If you removed this migration intentionally, run repair to mark the migration as deleted.

(flyway info shows V2 as Missing.) What is the right fix?

  1. Run flyway repair. It marks V2 as deleted, and that fully solves the problem.
  2. Run V2's SQL by hand on production.
  3. Renumber V3–V9 to V2–V8 to close the gap.
  4. Restore V2__add_billing.sql from git, unchanged.
Show answer

D. The folder must still be able to build the whole schema from zero. A new laptop or the CI database needs V2 to get the billing tables. repair (A) would make validate pass on production (V2 then shows Deleted), but every fresh database would be missing V2's tables. B changes nothing about the missing file. C changes the versions of applied migrations, which breaks every database that already ran them.

Twist Predict: the reverted branch on staging

Staging ran V2__add_flag.sql (adds users.flag) from a feature branch. The branch was then reverted, so main has only V1. Later, a teammate adds V2__add_coupons.sql on main. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. Once V2__add_coupons.sql arrives, Flyway runs it on staging, because its file name is different.
  2. Before the new file arrives, info on staging shows V2 as Future, and migrate succeeds with a warning that the schema version (2) is newer than the latest available migration (1).
  3. After V2__add_coupons.sql arrives, migrate on staging fails with a checksum mismatch for version 2, and coupons is never created.
  4. The lesson: a version that ran on any database is used up. The coupons migration needs a new version, for example a timestamp.
  5. users.flag is still on staging. Reverting code does not undo the schema.
Show answer

B, C, D, E. Flyway matches history rows to files by version, not by file name (A). So the new file is compared against the old row and fails validation. A missing file above the latest local version is Future, which is only a warning. A gap in the middle is Missing, which is an error. Remove the stray column on purpose (a new migration, or by rebuilding staging).

Quiz-style What went wrong: a tidier file name

V3__c.sql ran in production. To make names clearer, someone runs git mv V3__c.sql V3__add_coupons.sql without changing the contents. What does the next flyway migrate do?

  1. Fails with a checksum mismatch for version 3.
  2. Fails validation with "Migration description mismatch for migration version 3" (applied: c, local: add coupons).
  3. Nothing. Flyway matches by version only, and the version is unchanged.
  4. Runs V3 again, because the script name is new.
Show answer

B. The description comes from the file name and is stored in the history, so changing it counts as changing an applied migration. The contents are the same, so the checksum still matches (A). Flyway checks the description as well as the version (C), and it never reruns an applied version (D). Fix: rename the file back. flyway repair would instead update the stored description.

Quiz-style A migration fails halfway on Postgres 
-- V11__coupons.sql
CREATE TABLE coupons (id int PRIMARY KEY);
INSERT INTO promo_codes VALUES (1);   -- ERROR: relation "promo_codes" does not exist

Production (PostgreSQL) is at V10. V11 and V12 are pending. flyway migrate stops on the error. What is the state afterwards? (Default settings: each migration runs in its own transaction.)

  1. V11 has a Failed history row, and the coupons table exists.
  2. No coupons table and no history row for V11 (info shows it Pending). V12 did not run. V1–V10 are still applied.
  3. Flyway rolls back the whole migrate run, so the schema returns to empty.
  4. V11 is skipped, and V12 runs.
Show answer

B. On a database with transactional DDL, Flyway rolls back the failed migration's transaction ("Changes successfully rolled back") and writes no row. The fix is simply a corrected V11. A is what happens on a database without transactional DDL (e.g. MySQL), or with executeInTransaction=false. Earlier migrations were committed in their own transactions (C). Flyway never jumps past a failed migration (D).

Twist What went wrong: CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY 
-- 14_index_orders_email.sql      (orders: 80 million rows, PostgreSQL)
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY idx_orders_email ON orders (email);

ERROR:  CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY cannot run inside a transaction block

The team's runner wraps every migration in BEGIN ... COMMIT. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. Dropping CONCURRENTLY is a safe fix. A plain CREATE INDEX lets INSERTs and UPDATEs on orders continue while it builds.
  2. CONCURRENTLY builds the index faster than a plain CREATE INDEX.
  3. The error happens because the runner opened a transaction, and Postgres doesn't allow this statement inside one.
  4. Fix: run this migration outside a transaction, alone in its own file (Flyway: a .conf script config with executeInTransaction=false).
  5. If a migration that runs outside a transaction fails partway, the earlier statements stay done, and Flyway records a Failed row. You clean up by hand (here: drop the leftover INVALID index), then repair, then migrate.
Show answer

C, D, E. A plain CREATE INDEX holds a lock that blocks all writes to the table until the build finishes (A). On 80 million rows, that could be minutes of failed checkouts. CONCURRENTLY allows writes but is slower (B), because it scans the table twice and waits for open transactions. Without a transaction nothing is rolled back, so keep such a migration to one statement and make it safe to rerun (IF NOT EXISTS).

Quiz-style "Start every deploy from a clean state"

A teammate suggests adding flyway clean before flyway migrate in the production deploy script, "so every deploy starts from a known state". What does flyway clean do?

  1. It drops every object (tables, views, data, the history table) in the configured schemas. Flyway disables it by default, so the deploy would fail with "Unable to execute clean as it has been disabled". If enabled, it would wipe production.
  2. It removes only Failed rows from the history, like repair.
  3. It deletes only the history table, so every migration runs again on the existing tables.
  4. It rolls back the most recent migration.
Show answer

A. clean empties the schema completely. It is useful only for throwaway databases (a CI or local test DB, with cleanDisabled=false set there on purpose). B is repair's job. clean removes much more than the history (C). Flyway OSS has no rollback command (D): you roll forward with a new migration or restore a backup.

Quiz-style What went wrong: the drop shipped too early

Release 42 stops using users.nickname and also contains V20__drop_nickname.sql. The pipeline runs flyway migrate, then replaces pods one at a time over 10 minutes. During the rollout, the logs fill with column "nickname" does not exist. What went wrong?

  1. The new pods cached an old query that still uses nickname.
  2. The migration should have run after the rollout, in the same release. That would be fully safe.
  3. The old pods (release 41) still read nickname. The drop should ship in a later release, once no running code, and no version you might roll back to, uses the column.
  4. Flyway dropped the column before validating.
Show answer

C. During a rolling deploy, old and new code run against the same schema, so every schema change must work with both. This is the contract step of expand/contract, and it goes last, in its own release. B removes the errors during the rollout, but rolling back to release 41 would then break. A and D don't match the error: the new code no longer asks for nickname, and validation always runs before any SQL.

Quiz-style Backfilling 30 million rows

On a live PostgreSQL app, you must copy user_name into the new full_name column for 30 million users. New code already writes both columns. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. A batch job can't be stopped safely halfway, so one big UPDATE is better.
  2. A single UPDATE users SET full_name = user_name; holds a row lock on every row until it commits, so app updates to users wait for it.
  3. Batches by id range (e.g. 10,000 rows each), each in its own short transaction, keep the locks and each transaction small.
  4. Adding WHERE full_name IS NULL to each batch makes the job safe to restart after a crash.
Show answer

B, C, D. One huge transaction also creates a huge burst of WAL and replica lag. If it fails at row 29 million, all the work is rolled back. Committed batches are exactly what makes stopping and resuming safe (A). With the IS NULL filter, a rerun skips rows that are already done.

Twist What went wrong: "it worked on my machine"

Priya's local database uses TypeORM synchronize: true. She adds a discount field to the Order entity, and her local table gets the column automatically. She writes migration V31 for another change and forgets about discount. All tests pass on her laptop. On staging, the migrations run and then the app fails with column "discount" does not exist. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. Flyway's checksum validation should have caught the missing column.
  2. Her local schema came from auto-sync, not from the migrations, so it had drifted from what the migrations build.
  3. A CI job that runs every migration on a fresh empty database, then runs the tests against it, would have failed before merge.
  4. Fix: set synchronize: true on staging and production, so the schema always follows the code.
  5. Fix forward: add a new migration V32 that adds discount. Don't edit V31 if it has already run anywhere.
Show answer

B, C, E. A checksum compares files with history rows (A). It knows nothing about your entities. A test database built only from the migrations is the honest check, and reviewers should read the SQL in the PR as well. Auto-sync in production (D) turns renames into drop-and-add and loses data.

Twist Predict: -target to skip a broken migration 
history: V1–V4 Success
pending: V5__broken.sql   (fails: references a missing table)
         V6__add_index.sql
         V7__add_notes.sql
$ flyway migrate -target=6

An engineer hopes this skips V5 and applies only V6. PostgreSQL, default settings. What actually happens?

  1. Only V6 runs. V5 is skipped.
  2. V5 runs first and fails. Its transaction is rolled back, so V6 never runs and the schema stays at V4.
  3. V5, V6 and V7 all run, because -target only affects flyway info.
  4. Flyway refuses, because -target also needs -outOfOrder.
Show answer

B. -target is an upper limit ("migrate up to and including 6"), not a way to pick migrations (A). Pending files still run in version order, so V5 runs first. When a migration fails, migrate stops. With a working V5, -target=6 would apply V5 and V6 and leave V7 Pending (C). flyway info -target=6 labels V7 Above Target. outOfOrder (D) is about versions lower than ones already applied. To get past V5, fix it, since it has never run anywhere.

Twist Which edits break validation?

V1__create_users.sql has been applied on every database. Taken one at a time, which changes to the file make the next flyway migrate fail validation? Choose all that apply.

  1. Adding a line -- creates the users table at the top
  2. A teammate's editor saves it with Windows (CRLF) line endings, with no other change
  3. Adding one space at the end of a line
  4. touch migrations/V1__create_users.sql (only the modified time changes)
  5. Renaming it to V1__create_user_table.sql
Show answer

A, C, E. Flyway's checksum covers every character, comments and spaces included (A, C). It reads the file line by line, so a change only in line endings gives the same checksum (B). A file's date is never used (D). A rename changes the description, which gives a "description mismatch" error (E). If a harmless comment must stay, flyway repair stores the new checksum. That is the only kind of edit where repair is a fair fix.

Architecture and the stack decision (T4L1)

Quiz-style A call that timed out

Checkout calls the Inventory service over HTTP: reserve(item=42, qty=1). After 3 seconds it times out. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. Checkout can't tell whether the reservation ran and the reply was lost, or never ran at all.
  2. A timeout means the request was not processed, so retrying is always safe.
  3. You can't step through both services with one local debugger the way you can with a function call.
  4. Retrying is safe only if running the call twice has the same effect as running it once.
Show answer

A, C, D. A network call is a function call that can fail halfway, silently, on someone else's clock. That is partial failure. A timeout tells you nothing about what happened on the other side (B), which is why idempotency matters.

Quiz-style Which operations are safe to retry?

Which of these give the same end state when run twice? Choose all that apply.

  1. INSERT INTO payments (order_id, amount) VALUES (5, 499); with no unique key
  2. DELETE FROM carts WHERE id = 31;
  3. UPDATE wallets SET balance = balance - 100 WHERE user_id = 9;
  4. INSERT ... (idempotency_key, ...) where idempotency_key is UNIQUE and a duplicate is treated as "already done"
  5. UPDATE orders SET status = 'shipped' WHERE id = 5;
Show answer

B, D, E. Setting a value, or deleting a specific row, ends in the same state however many times it runs. A relative update (C) takes the money twice, and a plain insert (A) records two payments. An idempotency key sent with every retry lets the server act once per key.

Drill The eight fallacies

Which are among the eight fallacies of distributed computing? Choose all that apply.

  1. Latency is zero
  2. CPU is infinite
  3. Transport cost is zero
  4. The network is reliable
  5. The database is always consistent
  6. There is one administrator
Show answer

A, C, D, F. The full eight are: the network is reliable, latency is zero, bandwidth is infinite, the network is secure, topology doesn't change, there is one administrator, transport cost is zero, the network is homogeneous. E and B are made up. They sound plausible, but they are not about the network.

Quiz-style Which stack for an early product?

Five students are building a university hostel-booking portal. Time to market matters, the data is relational, and traffic is modest. Which choice matches the lecture?

  1. Microservices now, so the team doesn't have to rewrite later
