  2. Six microservices on Kubernetes, each with its own database, behind an API gateway
  3. A (modular) monolith on one framework, with a managed relational database and PaaS hosting
  4. Serverless functions joined by a cloud workflow orchestrator
Show answer

C. For a small team at an early stage, a monolith removes network overhead and orchestration cost, and it is the fastest path to a safe first release. B and A pay the distributed-systems tax before any measured pain exists ("Monolith First"). D adds orchestration and per-call cost that a simple app doesn't need.

Twist Why Prime Video saved 90%

Which statements explain the Prime Video monitoring case? Choose all that apply.

  1. Moving to one process (on EC2/ECS) passed the data in memory instead.
  2. Heavy video frames were passed through S3 between every step, which made an I/O bottleneck.
  3. The lesson is that microservices are always wrong.
  4. The serverless pipeline was billed per state transition in the orchestration workflow.
Show answer

A, B, D. Sending large data across a network between services or functions is expensive and slow. One memory space removes that cost. The lesson is about fit, not religion (C): "Building evolvable software systems is a strategy, not a religion."

Quiz-style When to extract Dispatch

The campus food app is a modular monolith with Ordering, Dispatch and Vendor Ops. Which observations would justify extracting Dispatch into its own service? Choose all that apply.

  1. Measured: rider matching needs very different hardware and scales separately from everything else.
  2. Engineering has grown past ~50 people, and teams keep blocking each other's deploys.
  3. "Dispatch" is a distinct word in the code.
  4. Dispatch already lives in its own folder.
Show answer

A, B. Split when the pain is measured, not guessed, such as team size or very different scaling needs. A name (C) or a folder (D) doesn't create a need to distribute. An enforced module boundary is the seam that makes a later extraction possible without a rewrite.

Quiz-style One word, three meanings

"Order" means cart and payment status in Ordering, pickup and rider ETA in Dispatch, and a kitchen ticket in Vendor Ops. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. Each is a bounded context: inside it, "Order" has one precise meaning.
  2. One shared, mutable Order object used everywhere is the cleanest design.
  3. Each module can keep its own order-like record.
  4. The contexts should integrate through public APIs and events such as OrderPlaced.
Show answer

A, C, D. All three definitions are correct inside their own boundary. Sharing one giant object (B) couples every team to every other team's rules. A context map shows how the contexts relate, and arrows go only through APIs and events, never through a shared table.

Quiz-style Is it a module?

Which observations make something a strong module candidate? Choose all that apply.

  1. You can state its responsibility and public contract in one or two sentences.
  2. Two features happen to share a database table today.
  3. It has its own business rules, changes for its own reasons, and keeps its data private.
  4. "Coupon" is a unique noun in the codebase.
Show answer

A, C. The five tests are responsibility, language, rules, data and contract. A unique keyword is only a clue (D): "Coupon" may just be a pricing rule inside Ordering. Table design must not decide module boundaries (B).

Twist CI says "dependency violation" 
$ bundle exec packwerk check
✗ billing/app/services/invoice_builder.rb
Dependency violation: billing → user
`User.find(id).email` — not in user's public API
⚠ 1 violation — build failed

Which responses are right? Choose all that apply.

  1. Turn the check off: everything runs in one process anyway.
  2. Get the email through the User module's public API (or an event), not its internals.
  3. This check is what stops the monolith from relapsing into a big ball of mud.
  4. Move Billing into its own microservice to fix the violation.
Show answer

B, C. In a modular monolith, modules talk only through explicit public APIs, and the boundary is enforced mechanically in CI, not by good intentions. Disabling the check (A) lets the boundary wear away. Distribution (D) is a separate decision that needs measured pain, and it doesn't fix the coupling.

Quiz-style Monolith, modular monolith, microservices

Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. A modular monolith's team autonomy comes from separate deployments.
  2. A modular monolith is still a single deployable.
  3. Microservices pay network overhead on every call that crosses a boundary.
  4. A modular monolith keeps ACID transactions easy, because it has one database and one process.
  5. Conway's Law: a system's structure tends to copy the communication structure of the organisation that builds it.
Show answer

B, C, D, E. A modular monolith's autonomy comes from enforced boundaries, not separate deploys (A). Microservices apply Conway's Law in reverse: you shape services so that teams can move fast. A mature plain monolith's biggest risk is one team's change silently breaking another team's feature.

Twist Serverless meets Postgres on launch day

Each serverless invocation opens its own Postgres connection. A launch brings 800 concurrent invocations, and the server's connection limit is 100. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. The CAP theorem is the constraint that matters here.
  2. Adding more serverless instances fixes it.
  3. New invocations start failing with "too many connections" errors, even though the CPU is idle.
  4. A connection pooler in front of the database lets many invocations share a few real connections.
Show answer

C, D. One connection per invocation against a hard server limit is why serverless compute and relational databases work badly together. The database, not the application, fails first. With a single-primary relational database, CAP is not yet the relevant constraint (A). More instances means more connections, which makes it worse (B).

Drill A bill line, not a latency graph

Your API runs in a Mumbai region. Every night it copies 2 TB of user uploads to a backup service in a Frankfurt region. Nothing is slow and nothing fails. But finance asks why the cloud bill has a large "inter-region data transfer" line. Which of the eight fallacies did the design assume?

  1. Latency is zero
  2. Bandwidth is infinite
  3. Transport cost is zero
  4. The network is homogeneous
Show answer

C. "Transport cost is zero" is about money: every gigabyte that crosses a region has a bill attached. Latency (A) is about time, and nothing here is slow. Bandwidth (B) is about the pipe filling up and dropping packets, which didn't happen. Homogeneous (D) is about different hardware, kernels and MTUs.

Quiz-style Three fallacies in one incident

The order page runs in a Virginia data centre. It calls a pricing service in Tokyo once per cart item, in a loop. Each reply is a 3 MB JSON document. Last week the ops team moved the pricing service to a new rack, and the IP address hard-coded in the config stopped working. Which fallacies did the design assume? Choose all that apply.

  1. The network is secure
  2. Latency is zero
  3. Bandwidth is infinite
  4. There is one administrator
  5. Topology doesn't change
Show answer

B, C, E. One call per item from Virginia to Tokyo assumes zero latency, but a WAN call is limited by the speed of light in fiber. 3 MB replies assume infinite bandwidth. A hard-coded IP that broke when a rack moved assumes the topology never changes. Nothing in the story is about attackers (A) or about competing teams owning parts of the network (D).

Twist What went wrong: the retry still double-charged 
def charge(order_id, amount):
    for attempt in range(3):
        key = str(uuid4())
        try:
            return payments.charge(order_id, amount,
                                   idempotency_key=key, timeout=3)
        except Timeout:
            continue

The Payments service saves every idempotency key and ignores a key it has already seen. Even so, a customer was charged twice after one timeout. What is the fix?

  1. Raise the timeout to 30 seconds, so the first call never times out.
  2. Retry only once instead of three times.
  3. Catch every exception, not only Timeout.
  4. Create the key once per payment, before the loop, and send the same key on every retry.
Show answer

D. The key is made inside the loop, so every attempt looks like a brand-new payment to the server and the duplicate check never fires. The key must name the payment, not the attempt. A longer timeout (A) makes timeouts rarer, but a lost reply can still happen. One retry (B) can still double-charge. Catching more exceptions (C) does nothing for duplicate detection.

Quiz-style Conway's Law at a startup

A startup has three teams: Web, Mobile and Payments. They work in different cities and talk mostly through tickets. Which statements match the lecture? Choose all that apply.

  1. Amazon keeps its teams small enough to be fed by two pizzas, which gives each team accountability and agility.
  2. Conway's Law says an organisation's structure ends up copying the design of its system.
  3. The system these teams build will tend to have three parts, joined where the teams talk to each other.
  4. Microservices became popular mainly because they handle more traffic. Team structure has little to do with it.
  5. A "two-pizza team" is a team that owns exactly two services.
Show answer

A, C. Conway (1967): a system's design copies the organisation's communication structure, which is why four teams build a four-pass compiler. B gets the direction backwards. The slides say microservices were popularised "not for scalability alone" (D): they apply Conway's Law in reverse to maximise team velocity. E is made up.

Quiz-style Six services later

A team split its monolith into six services, each with its own database. One checkout now touches Cart, Billing, Inventory and Shipping. Which new costs does the lecture predict? Choose all that apply.

  1. One slow checkout leaves logs spread across many services, so finding the cause gets harder.
  2. Placing the order and reserving stock can still run in one ACID transaction, because each service has a database.
  3. When Inventory is down, Cart, Billing and Shipping can turn slow while they wait for it.
  4. Running, deploying and connecting six services adds a large orchestration overhead.
  5. Calls between the services cost nothing extra, because they all run in the same data centre.
Show answer

A, C, D. The distributed-systems tax: one request, a dozen logs; cascading partial failures (one service down, three others slow); staggering orchestration overhead. B is wrong: with separate databases a distributed transaction is "nearly impossible". E is wrong: microservices pay network overhead on every boundary call, and each call can still fail halfway.

Drill Six services, one big tangle

User, Auth, Billing, Inventory, Shipping and Notification each have their own database. But every service calls every other service directly, and a change to User needs a release of Billing and Shipping at the same time. What does the lecture call this?

  1. A modular monolith
  2. A distributed, tightly coupled monolith
  3. Microservices done right, because each service owns its database
  4. A traditional monolith
Show answer

B. Pull the mask off these "microservices" and you find a distributed, tightly coupled monolith. You pay the network tax and still keep the coupling. Separate databases (C) are not enough when every service calls every other and deploys are not independent. A modular monolith (A) and a traditional monolith (D) are both one process with no network calls inside.

Twist A photo pipeline that repeats Prime Video's mistake

A student's app checks uploaded 4K photos. A cloud workflow service (like AWS Step Functions) runs three functions: Resize → S3 → Detect faces → S3 → Notify. The workflow is billed per state transition, and each step reads and writes the full photo through S3. Cost and delay grow fast as uploads grow. What would the lecture suggest?

  1. Run the three steps in one process on a VM or container (EC2/ECS) and pass the photo in memory.
  2. Split Detect faces into two smaller functions, so each one finishes faster.
  3. Keep the design, but raise the functions' concurrency limit.
  4. Put an API gateway in front of the workflow.
Show answer

A. This is the Prime Video shape: per-transition billing plus heavy data relayed through S3 at every hop. Moving to one process with in-memory transfer cut their cost by 90%. Splitting further (B) adds more transitions and more S3 trips. C and D leave both cost drivers in place.

Quiz-style Which calls respect the boundaries?

The campus food app is a modular monolith with Ordering, Dispatch and Vendor Ops. It is one deployable, and all modules use one Postgres server. Which pieces of code respect the module boundaries? Choose all that apply.

  1. In Dispatch: SELECT prep_minutes FROM vendor_ops_kitchen_tickets WHERE order_id = $1, "because it's the same database, so it's fast"
  2. In Vendor Ops: set payment_status = 'refunded' on Ordering's order row when the kitchen runs out of an item
  3. In Dispatch: a handler that runs when OrderReadyForPickup is published
  4. In Ordering: import Dispatch's internal RiderAssignment model to print the rider's name on the receipt
  5. In Dispatch: call vendor_ops.getKitchenStatus(order_id)
Show answer

C, E. Modules talk only through explicit public APIs and events, such as getKitchenStatus() and OrderReadyForPickup. A reads another module's private table; sharing one database doesn't make that OK. Vendor Ops must not change the customer's payment status (B). Ordering must not read Dispatch's rider assignment directly (D).

Quiz-style Dispatch wants to change the kitchen queue

Riders often arrive early. The Dispatch team wants an order to jump to the front of the kitchen queue when its rider is two minutes away. Vendor Ops owns the kitchen queue. What is the right design in a modular monolith?

  1. Dispatch updates the kitchen_queue table directly, because both modules share one database.
  2. Vendor Ops adds a public command (for example, a priority request) to its contract, and Vendor Ops decides how the queue changes.
  3. Merge Dispatch and Vendor Ops into one module, so there is no boundary to cross.
  4. Move Vendor Ops into its own microservice first, then let Dispatch call it.
Show answer

B. Dispatch "must not edit the vendor's kitchen queue". Vendor Ops owns the queue and its rules, so other modules may only use what its public contract offers. A breaks the data test: a module's data stays private. C removes a real boundary (different language, different rules) just to dodge a contract. D adds network cost without measured pain, and a module call does the same job.

Drill Name the DDD idea

A diagram shows Ordering publishing OrderPlaced to Dispatch, and Vendor Ops publishing OrderReadyForPickup to Dispatch. In Domain-Driven Design, what is this diagram?

  1. The ubiquitous language
  2. A bounded context
  3. An entity-relationship diagram of the orders table
  4. A context map
Show answer

D. A context map shows how bounded contexts relate and integrate. The ubiquitous language (A) is the shared vocabulary. A bounded context (B) is one of the boxes: a boundary inside which a word has one precise meaning. C is about tables, but DDD says "model the business, not the database".

Quiz-style Vendors say "ticket", the code says "OrderRow"

Kitchen staff talk about "tickets", "prep time" and "queue position". In the Vendor Ops code, the same things are called OrderRow, t_delta and idx. New engineers keep misreading the bug reports vendors send. Which DDD idea is missing?

  1. Ubiquitous language: one vocabulary, shared by engineers and domain experts, used in conversation and in code
  2. A context map, because the arrows between modules are missing
  3. Conway's Law, because the vendors are not on the engineering team
  4. Monolith First, because Vendor Ops should be split out
Show answer

A. The ubiquitous language has to reach the code, not just the meetings. A context map (B) is about how contexts connect, but this problem is inside one context. Conway's Law (C) is about how teams shape a system. Monolith First (D) is about when to split, not about naming.

Quiz-style Draw the context map

Which arrows belong on the campus food app's context map? Choose all that apply.

  1. Ordering publishes OrderPlaced, and Dispatch reacts to it
  2. Dispatch reads Ordering's carts table to find the drop point
  3. Vendor Ops publishes OrderReadyForPickup, and Dispatch reacts to it
  4. Ordering and Dispatch both edit one shared, mutable Order object
  5. Dispatch publishes DeliveryCompleted when a delivery ends
Show answer

A, C, E. The lecture's map has OrderPlaced (Ordering → Dispatch) and OrderReadyForPickup (Vendor Ops → Dispatch), and the contracts table says Dispatch publishes DeliveryCompleted. Arrows go only through public APIs and events, never through a shared table (B). Each module may keep its own order-like record, but never one giant shared mutable Order object (D).

Twist Coupon grows up

At first, "Coupon" just took 10% off at checkout, and it lived inside Ordering. Now marketing wants campaign eligibility rules, merchant-funded promotions, abuse limits, budget caps and reporting. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. Coupon became a module on the day someone first wrote the word "Coupon" in the code.
  2. Every bounded context must become exactly one microservice.
  3. Coupon is now a real module candidate, because it owns its own policy and rules.
  4. Before making it a module, check responsibility, language, rules, data and contract.
  5. If it becomes a module, it still ships inside the one deployable. Being a module does not make it a service.
Show answer

C, D, E. "A module is created around an owned responsibility and its rules, not because we discovered a new noun" (A is wrong). Coupon earns a module once it owns independent policy such as eligibility, budget caps and abuse limits. One bounded context is usually one module candidate, but that is not a law, and a context should not become a microservice just because you named it (B).

Drill Where the workshop starts

In the lecture's workshop for finding the food app's modules, what is the first step?

  1. List the database tables and group them into modules.
  2. List the nouns in the requirements: Order, Rider, Menu, Coupon.
  3. List actions and events, not nouns: menu updated, order placed, rider assigned, delivery completed.
  4. Draw one service box per team and connect them.
Show answer

C. After listing events, you ask who owns each decision, cluster the events, run the five boundary tests, draw arrows only through public APIs or events, and last ask what measured pain would justify a service. Tables must not decide module boundaries (A). Nouns are only clues (B). D starts with deployment, which is the last and optional question.

Quiz-style What Shopify's numbers show

A classmate says: "Real scale needs microservices." Which statements about Shopify, from the lecture, are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. Shopify split into thousands of microservices before its first big Black Friday.
  2. Shopify runs a modular monolith, and it served 173 billion requests over a Black Friday weekend.
  3. Shopify's peak was 284 thousand requests per minute.
  4. Shopify enforces module boundaries with Packwerk, which fails the build on a dependency violation.
  5. Shopify's boundaries hold because developers agree not to cross them.
Show answer

B, D. Shopify is a modular monolith at planetary scale: 173B requests over the weekend, 284 million requests per minute at peak (C says thousand), and 12 TB of data every minute. It is not microservices (A). Boundaries hold because a tool checks them in CI. Without mechanical enforcement (E), a modular monolith slides back into a Big Ball of Mud.

Quiz-style 12 engineers, one Postgres

Predict before you check: 12 engineers. One Postgres database. Growing steadily, not explosively. What do you build?

  1. Microservices from day one, so scaling later is free
  2. A modular monolith with enforced module boundaries
  3. One undivided monolith with no internal boundaries
  4. Serverless functions, one per feature
Show answer

B. 12 people is far below Fowler's ~50-engineer threshold, and nothing needs very different hardware, so there is no measured pain to split (A). But 12 people in one undivided codebase (C) is how a monolith becomes a Big Ball of Mud, where one team's change silently breaks another's. D adds network and orchestration cost for no measured reason. Enforced seams give autonomy now and a clean cut later.

Twist What only the microservices version needs

The same campus SaaS is built twice: once as a modular monolith and once as six microservices. Which parts does the microservices version need that the modular monolith version does not? Choose all that apply.

  1. A relational database
  2. An orchestrator such as Kubernetes (EKS) plus an API gateway
  3. A message broker such as Kafka for events between services
  4. A queue for background jobs (for example Redis with BullMQ)
  5. Distributed tracing such as OpenTelemetry
  6. A CI pipeline that runs the tests and the boundary checks
Show answer

B, C, E. The modular monolith keeps one runtime, one Postgres, enforced boundaries, a queue for async jobs (D) and one deploy pipeline. Both versions need a relational database (A), though the microservices one needs six, and both need CI (F). Microservices add an orchestrator and gateway, a broker and tracing, so they need much more platform skill before they are safe to run.

Twist The boundary check that never blocks 
# .github/workflows/ci.yml (steps)
      - name: Module boundaries
        run: bundle exec packwerk check
        continue-on-error: true
      - name: Tests
        run: bundle exec rspec

Six months later, Billing makes 40 direct calls into User's internals. Every pull request that added one shows a green check. What went wrong?

  1. Packwerk can't detect calls from Billing into User.
  2. The tests should run before the boundary check.
  3. The app should have been split into microservices instead.
  4. A failing boundary step doesn't fail the job, so violations merge with a green check and the boundary is no longer enforced.
Show answer

D. continue-on-error: true lets the step fail while the job still passes. A check that never blocks a merge is no stronger than a code comment, and the monolith slides back into a Big Ball of Mud. The lecture's Packwerk output shows exactly "Dependency violation: billing → user" (A). Step order doesn't matter here (B). Splitting into services (C) doesn't fix the coupling.

Hosting and orchestration (T4L2)

Drill Read the trade-off row

In the lecture's trade-off table, one hosting model has: responsibility "App and images", scaling by "Replica counts", lock-in "Medium", complexity "Moderate". Which model is it?

  1. Virtual machine
  2. Container PaaS
  3. Managed containers
  4. Serverless
Show answer

C. A VM (A) is "OS and app", scaled by adding VMs by hand, with low lock-in and many chores. A container PaaS (B) is "app only", scaled by a platform setting, and the lowest to start. Serverless (D) is "function only", scales per request, and has higher lock-in.

Quiz-style Which rows of the table are right?

Which statements match the lecture's hosting trade-off table? Choose all that apply.

  1. Serverless has the lowest lock-in, because you only ship a function.
  2. A VM gives the highest control, and you scale it by adding VMs by hand.
  3. One hosting model wins on every row, if you pick the right one for your team.
  4. Serverless is usually charged per invocation plus duration. A VM is charged for provisioned capacity.
  5. A container PaaS is the lowest-complexity way to start.
Show answer

B, D, E. Serverless lock-in is "Higher" (A). The VM has the lowest lock-in. The slide's own line is "No column wins every row" (C), so you choose by who operates what.

Quiz-style PaaS plus managed Postgres: what is still yours?

hello-api runs on a container PaaS at the custom domain api.acme.dev. Postgres is managed by Supabase. Which jobs does the team still own? Choose all that apply.

  1. Patching the OS on the machines that run the container
  2. Restarting the process after it crashes
  3. Testing that a backup actually restores
  4. Pointing the api.acme.dev DNS record at the platform
  5. The database schema, access rules and credentials
Show answer

C, D, E. The PaaS handles build and deploy, runtime, routing, TLS and process restarts (B), and the provider runs the machines and OS patching (A). You still own custom-domain DNS, config and secrets, the database, health endpoints, logs, scaling limits and cost. A managed database does backups, patching and failover, but schema, access rules, credentials and restore tests stay yours. "Managed does not mean responsibility-free."

Quiz-style PaaS for the app, a VM for the database?

Your team deploys hello-api on Render, a container PaaS. The database admin wants to keep Postgres on a VM the team already runs. A teammate says: "Not allowed. If the app is on a PaaS, the database must be managed too." Who is right?

  1. The admin. Compute and database are separate decisions, but the team then owns the database chores: install, firewall rules, replicas and backups.
  2. The teammate. A PaaS app can only connect to a managed database.
  3. The admin, and the VM database needs no extra work, because the PaaS backs it up.
  4. Neither. The database should run inside the same container as the app.
Show answer

A. "hello-api can run on a PaaS and keep its database on a VM, or the reverse. Decide each on its own." A self-hosted database means you do the install, firewall rules, replica streaming and backup scripts yourself. The PaaS only backs up what it runs (C). D ties the data to one disposable container, and scaling out needs state kept outside each copy.

Twist "Managed" and the auditor's question

hello-api's Postgres moved to AWS RDS a year ago, with automated daily backups turned on. Nobody has touched the backups since. An auditor asks: "If the database were deleted today, could you get yesterday's data back?" Which answer is honest?

  1. Yes. RDS is managed, so recovery is AWS's responsibility, not ours.
  2. Yes. A backup has run every day for a year.
  3. Yes. A replica would be promoted automatically.
  4. Not proven yet. The backups exist, but restore tests are still ours. We must restore one into a scratch database and check the data.
Show answer

D. A managed database gives backups, patching and failover. Restore tests are still yours (A). A year of backups only proves the job ran (B), like the cron-dump question from T3. Failover helps when a server dies, but a replica is not a backup: it copies the delete too (C).

Quiz-style Why can't the internet reach port 8000? 
$ docker run -d --name hello-api --restart unless-stopped \
    -p 127.0.0.1:8000:8000 ghcr.io/acme/hello-api:1.0
$ sudo ufw allow 22,80,443/tcp
$ cat /etc/caddy/Caddyfile
api.acme.dev { reverse_proxy 127.0.0.1:8000 }

From a laptop, curl http://203.0.113.10:8000/healthz fails, but curl https://api.acme.dev/healthz returns 200. Which statements about this setup are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. Port 8000 is published only on the VM's loopback address, so only programs on the VM can reach it.
  2. The setup is wrong: the lecture wants -p 8000:8000, so the app can be reached directly.
  3. Caddy is the one public door. It receives HTTPS on 443 and forwards inward to 127.0.0.1:8000.
  4. The firewall rule opens only SSH (22), HTTP (80) and HTTPS (443). Port 8000 is not on the list.
  5. Port 22 is open so that browsers can reach the API over plain HTTP.
Show answer

A, C, D. The demo binds the app to localhost on purpose, "so nothing outside the machine can reach it". The reverse proxy is the only public entry (B is wrong). Port 22 is SSH, so you can still log in. Plain HTTP is port 80 (E).

Quiz-style Certificate pending 
$ sudo systemctl reload caddy
⚠ certificate pending: no DNS record yet

What should you do next?

  1. Open port 8000 in the firewall, so the certificate authority can reach the app.
  2. Add the DNS record A api.acme.dev -> 203.0.113.10. The certificate can be issued once the name points to the server.
  3. Change the Caddyfile to reverse_proxy 0.0.0.0:8000.
  4. Run docker restart hello-api.
Show answer

B. "DNS must point to the server before the certificate can be issued." Opening 8000 (A) exposes the app and does nothing for the certificate. C and D change the inside of the server, but the problem is that the name doesn't point to it yet.

Quiz-style Edited the env file, nothing changed 
$ sudo sed -i 's/LOG_LEVEL=info/LOG_LEVEL=debug/' /etc/hello-api.env
$ docker restart hello-api
$ docker exec hello-api printenv LOG_LEVEL
info

The container was started with --env-file /etc/hello-api.env. Why does it still say info?

  1. docker restart pulls the image again, and the image has LOG_LEVEL=info baked in.
  2. --env-file is read only the first time the VM boots.
  3. The sed command failed, because the file belongs to root.
  4. The environment is fixed when the container is created. Restart reuses the same container, so you must docker rm -f it and docker run again with --env-file, as the demo did.
Show answer

D. Restart is not recreate. The demo ran docker rm -f hello-api and then docker run ... --env-file: "same image, new configuration". A restart never pulls an image (A). B is made up. The command used sudo, so the edit worked (C).

Twist Where does the production DATABASE_URL go?

A teammate wants to "make deploys simpler" and suggests ways to handle hello-api's production DATABASE_URL, which contains a password. Which ideas follow the lecture? Choose all that apply.

  1. Add ENV DATABASE_URL=postgres://app:s3cret@db.internal:5432/hello to the Dockerfile, so every container has it.
  2. Keep it in /etc/hello-api.env on the server and pass --env-file to docker run.
  3. Commit prod.env to the private Git repo, then delete it in the next commit.
  4. Build a separate production image with the secret inside, tagged hello-api:1.0-prod.
  5. Run the same image tag in staging and production, each with its own env file.
Show answer

B, E. "Never bake production secrets into source code or container images." A and D put the password into image layers, so anyone who can pull the image can read it. C leaves the password in Git history even after the delete, so it must be rotated. "One image runs in every environment. Only the file on the server changes."

Drill Stopped by hand before lunch

hello-api runs with --restart unless-stopped. You run docker stop hello-api for maintenance and go to lunch. What do you find 30 minutes later?

  1. It is still stopped. This policy doesn't restart a container you stopped by hand.
  2. It restarted within seconds, because the policy restarts after any exit.
  3. It restarted once, then stopped for good.
  4. It was deleted, because unless-stopped removes stopped containers.
Show answer

A. unless-stopped restarts the container after a crash and after Docker or the host restarts, unless you stopped it yourself. It never deletes containers (D), and it doesn't treat a manual stop like a crash (B, C).

Quiz-style What does one VM recover from?

hello-api runs on one VM with --restart unless-stopped and nothing else. Which events does it recover from on its own? Choose all that apply.

  1. sudo pkill -9 -f "uvicorn main:app" kills the app process
  2. The cloud provider loses the VM for good
  3. A bad release makes the app crash on every start
  4. The VM reboots after a kernel update
  5. The TLS certificate expires because renewal failed
Show answer

A, D. The demo's list: a crashed process and a host reboot recover. A lost VM (B) has no other machine to take over. A crash on every start (C) gives a restart loop with no alert. Certificates, disks, OS patches and backups (E) are not covered. "A restart policy is a process supervisor for one host. It is not an orchestrator."

Twist Restarting, 37 times 
$ docker ps --filter name=hello-api
CONTAINER ID   IMAGE                        STATUS
4be1c0a9d2f1   ghcr.io/acme/hello-api:1.1   Restarting (1) 8 seconds ago
$ docker inspect -f '{{.RestartCount}}' hello-api
37

Version 1.1 has a bug in how it reads DATABASE_URL, so it exits right after starting. What is the restart policy doing, and what is missing?

  1. It will roll back to 1.0 after three failed starts.
  2. It moved the container to a healthy host, which is why the count is 37.
  3. It keeps restarting the same broken container in a loop. Nothing alerts a human, so someone has to notice and redeploy.
  4. It has stopped trying, and 37 is the number of users affected.
Show answer

C. The lecture lists "crash on every start (restart loop, no alert)" and "nobody was told" as things a restart policy does not fix. It restarts the same image, so it can't roll back (A); getting back fast is a T5 topic. It supervises one host and never moves anything (B). The status says "Restarting", so it is still trying, and the count is restarts, not users (D).

Drill Name the orchestration job

VM A dies and takes hello-api-1, 2 and 3 with it. VM B still runs 4 and 5. Which of the four orchestration jobs answers "Who finds a machine with free capacity for the missing containers?"

  1. Failure detection
  2. Scheduling
  3. Workload placement and startup
  4. Routing and service discovery
Show answer

B. Scheduling finds capacity. Failure detection (A) notices that VM A is gone. Placement and startup (C) puts the containers on the chosen machine and starts them. Routing and service discovery (D) points traffic at the new addresses.

Quiz-style Six replicas, one node down

An orchestrator is told to keep 6 healthy replicas of hello-api across three nodes. One node, running 2 of them, goes down. Which statements describe the reconciliation loop? Choose all that apply.

  1. It pages a human for each lost container and waits for approval before acting.
  2. Desired 6 minus observed 4 gives the action: start 2 replicas on nodes with free capacity.
  3. It waits until the dead node is repaired, then restarts the 2 replicas there.
  4. It updates routing so traffic reaches the new replicas.
  5. A human is paged when the loop can't recover or users still see symptoms.
Show answer

B, D, E. "Desired state minus observed state equals actions": start the missing containers where there is capacity and update the load balancer. The loop acts on its own (A) and doesn't wait for a dead machine (C). "You declare what should be true. The orchestrator keeps making it true."

Drill The supervision ladder

Which statements about the lecture's ladder (systemd, Docker restart policy, Kubernetes or Swarm, managed platform) are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. systemd can restart hello-api on another VM if this one dies.
  2. Docker's restart policy finds free capacity on other hosts.
  3. Kubernetes and Swarm manage many hosts toward a declared desired state.
  4. On a managed platform, the provider runs the scheduling and the control plane for you.
  5. A load balancer replaces dead instances once it stops sending them traffic.
Show answer

C, D. systemd restarts one process on its own machine (A). "Docker's restart policy handles one host. It does not find capacity or reroute traffic. That takes an orchestrator" (B). A load balancer only routes. The slide asks "who replaces the three missing containers?" because the LB doesn't (E).

Drill Alive, but not ready

The database goes down. On one hello-api instance, the liveness check /healthz returns 200 and the readiness check /readyz returns 503. What should happen to that instance?

  1. It is restarted, because one of its checks failed.
  2. It is restarted and also removed from the load balancer.
  3. Nothing. Only liveness changes anything.
  4. The load balancer stops sending it traffic. It is not restarted.
Show answer

D. Readiness fails: "stop routing to me. No restart." Liveness fails: "restart me". "Load balancers use readiness to choose targets. Orchestrators use liveness to restart." Here liveness passes, so there is no restart (A, B), and readiness does change routing (C).

Twist Predict: a liveness check that needs the database 
livenessProbe:
  httpGet: { path: /hello, port: 8000 }

Every hello-api replica uses this probe. /hello runs SELECT count(*) FROM visits. Postgres has a two-minute outage. What happens?

  1. Every replica fails liveness and is restarted, though the processes were fine. The restarts don't fix the database.
  2. The load balancer stops routing to the replicas, and nothing is restarted.
  3. The orchestrator restarts Postgres, because the liveness check found it down.
  4. Nothing, because /hello still returns 200 while the database is down.
Show answer

A. Liveness means "restart me", and /hello fails on every replica at once when the database is down. That is why the lecture puts liveness on /healthz, which doesn't touch the database, and checks dependencies with readiness. B describes a readiness failure. A probe acts on its own container, not on Postgres (C). /hello needs the database, so it fails (D).

Quiz-style Ninety seconds to boot

A new version of hello-api loads a big cache for about 90 seconds at startup. Its liveness check starts failing after 30 seconds, so it gets restarted again and again. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. Liveness asks "Am I alive?" When it fails, the instance is restarted.
  2. A startup check asks "Can I serve traffic?" When it fails, the instance is restarted at once.
  3. Load balancers use liveness to choose which instances get traffic.
  4. A startup check fits this case: the other checks wait until booting has finished.
  5. Readiness asks "Can I serve traffic?" When it fails, traffic stops going to the instance.
Show answer

A, D, E. Startup asks "Have I finished booting?", and while it fails the other checks wait, which is exactly the slow-start, big-cache case. B mixes up startup with readiness. Load balancers use readiness, and orchestrators use liveness to restart (C).

Quiz-style Bigger box or more boxes?

hello-api is slow at peak on one VM with 1 vCPU and 2 GB. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. Moving to a bigger VM lets hello-api survive losing a machine.
  2. Moving to 8 vCPU and 32 GB is simple, but it has a ceiling and is still one point of failure.
  3. Running three copies needs no load balancer if each copy has its own IP address.
  4. Running three copies needs a load balancer in front and state kept outside each copy.
  5. Vertical scaling usually needs a restart.
  6. Horizontal scaling can survive losing one instance.
Show answer

B, D, E, F. Vertical (a bigger machine) is simple, but it has a ceiling, needs a restart, and is still one machine (A is wrong). Horizontal (more copies) survives losing one, but it needs a load balancer in front (C is wrong) and stateless app servers.

Twist What went wrong: logged out at random 
SESSIONS = {}                      # token -> user_id, in process memory

@app.post("/login")
def login(...):  ...; SESSIONS[token] = user.id

@app.get("/me")
def me(token: str):  return {"user": SESSIONS.get(token)}

This worked on one instance. After scaling to three replicas behind a load balancer, users are randomly told they are not logged in. Why, and what is the fix?

  1. The load balancer is broken. Replace it.
  2. Each replica has its own SESSIONS in memory, so a request sent to another replica finds no session. Keep the session state outside the process, for example in Postgres.
  3. Three replicas go over the pool limit of max_size=5.
  4. Python dicts are not thread-safe. Add a lock.
Show answer

B. "hello-api scales out because its persistent state lives in Postgres, not in process memory." Scaling out needs stateless app servers. The load balancer is doing its job by spreading requests (A). The pool limit is per instance and has nothing to do with logins (C). A lock can't share memory between three separate processes (D).

Twist The 14:02 deploy and the pool of five 
ALERT   p95 latency of /hello above 1 s
METRIC  /hello slow since the 14:02 deploy
TRACE   client 2 ms · load balancer 3 ms · hello-api 14 ms · Postgres 2,300 ms   (total 2,319 ms)
LOG     waiting for a free connection (pool max_size=5)

Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. The trace answers "where did this request spend its time?": 2,300 of the 2,319 ms were in the database step.
  2. The app code is the bottleneck, so a bigger VM for hello-api is the fix.
  3. The log shows requests waiting for one of the instance's 5 pool connections.
  4. The restart policy should have caught this, because the process was unhealthy.
  5. The alert tells a human when to react, and the metric points to the 14:02 deploy as the place to start looking.
Show answer

A, C, E. Each signal answers one question: logs say what happened, metrics say how much and since when, traces say where the time went, and alerts say when a human must act. hello-api itself took only 14 ms (B). The process never crashed, so a restart policy has nothing to do (D), and a restart would not fix a pool that is too small.

Quiz-style Two engineers, 40 requests a minute

Two engineers run hello-api and one Postgres database. Traffic is about 40 requests a minute. Which setup fits best?

  1. Kubernetes on three nodes that the team operates
  2. Docker Swarm on two VMs that the team maintains
  3. A PaaS container and a managed database
  4. Six microservices on managed Kubernetes
Show answer

C. An orchestrator earns its cost when you must detect failures, schedule, place and reroute across many hosts. One small app at 40 requests a minute doesn't need that, so A and B buy the operating cost without the benefit. D adds the distributed-systems tax too. A PaaS is the lowest to start: it handles restarts, routing and TLS, and a managed database handles backups, patching and failover.

Continuous safe delivery and the T5 demos (T5)

Drill Detect, decide, act at TiffinGo 
19:00  TiffinGo v2.4 deploys "group order"
19:02  Checkout returns errors for 1 in 5 orders
19:09  Alert: errors above 2% for 5 minutes
19:11  Deploy tiffingo:2.3
19:12  Recovered

Time to recover = detect + decide + act. Which split is right, and what does the slide say makes most outages long?

  1. Detect 2 min, decide 7 min, act 1 min. Most outages are long because people take too long to decide.
  2. Detect 9 min, decide 2 min, act 1 min. Most outages are long because deploys are slow.
  3. Detect 7 min, decide 2 min, act 1 min. "Most outages are long because nobody noticed."
  4. Detect 7 min, decide 1 min, act 2 min. Most outages are long because rollbacks are slow.
Show answer

C. Detect runs from the first error (19:02) to the alert (19:09), so 7 minutes. Decide runs from 19:09 to the redeploy at 19:11 (2 minutes), and act runs from 19:11 to 19:12 (1 minute). A swaps detect and decide. B starts the clock at the deploy (19:00), but nothing was broken until 19:02. D swaps decide and act. Detection was 7 of the 10 minutes, which is why the slide blames "nobody noticed", not slow fixing.

Quiz-style No alert, just a tweet 
14:00  v3.2 deployed
14:03  first payment errors appear in the logs
14:21  a customer's angry tweet reaches the on-call engineer (no alert was set up)
14:25  on-call decides to redeploy the previous image
14:26  recovered

Which statement is right?

  1. Time to recover is 26 minutes. The biggest lever is a faster way back, such as a feature flag.
  2. Time to recover is 23 minutes, and 18 of them are detection. An error-rate alert (like "errors above 2% for 5 minutes") is the biggest lever.
  3. Time to recover is 23 minutes. Deciding (4 minutes) is the biggest part, so a written runbook would help most.
  4. Time to recover is 1 minute, because only the act step counts.
Show answer

B. Detect 14:03 → 14:21 = 18, decide 14:21 → 14:25 = 4, act 14:25 → 14:26 = 1, total 23. A starts at the deploy (14:00), not at the first error. Also, the act step is already 1 minute, so a flag could save less than a minute. C: 4 minutes of deciding is much smaller than 18 minutes of not noticing. D leaves out detect and decide, which are part of the formula.

Quiz-style Rename, then redeploy 2.3

v2.4's migration ran ALTER TABLE orders RENAME COLUMN items TO order_items; and v2.4 also has a checkout bug. You redeploy the previous image tiffingo:2.3. Its code runs INSERT INTO orders (customer, items, total) VALUES (…) for every checkout. What happens to checkout?

  1. It recovers, because tiffingo:2.3 is the exact image that worked at 18:59.
  2. It recovers, because starting the old image runs its old migration again and brings items back.
  3. Only group orders fail. Single orders work again.
  4. Every checkout fails with column "items" … does not exist, because redeploying code never changes the schema.
Show answer

D. This is the slide-6 poll. The image holds code, not the schema. The database still has order_items, and v2.3 asks for items. A is true for the code but ignores the database it talks to. B is wrong because starting a container runs no migrations, and in the demo rollback.sh never touches the database. C is wrong because every insert names items, so single orders fail too, not only group orders.

Twist Healthy, and 100% red

In the demo, right after ./deploy.sh 2.4-rename, the professor runs:

$ ./rollback.sh 2.3
...
Back on v2.3 in 2.1 s.
$ curl -s http://localhost:8080/healthz
{"status":"ok","version":"2.3"}

# Terminal 2 (traffic.sh): every line is red
ERROR  column "items" of relation "orders" does not exist  (v2.3)

Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. The health check failed, so rollback.sh kept v2.4-rename live. That is why the errors continue.
  2. /healthz passes because it only runs SELECT 1. It checks that the database can be reached, not that checkout works.
  3. Running ./deploy.sh 2.3 instead would fix it, because the pipeline would run 2.3's migrate.sql again and recreate items.
  4. docker exec t5-db psql -U postgres -c '\d orders' would list order_items and no items.
  5. rollback.sh started the old image, checked health and switched traffic. It ran no down migration and did not change schema_migrations.
Show answer

B, D, E. The code went back but the column didn't: "Code is reversible, data is not." A is wrong because /healthz reports 2.3 and the errors end in (v2.3), so 2.3 is live. C is wrong twice. schema_migrations already has a row for 2.3, so the pipeline prints "already applied". And 2.3's file is CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS, which does nothing to an existing table. The way out is to fix forward: RENAME COLUMN order_items TO items.

Drill What the runner is

A push to main starts a GitHub Actions job with the steps checkout, install, test and deploy. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. The job runs on a fresh computer (the runner). It starts empty and is deleted when the job ends.
  2. Files from last night's run, such as an installed node_modules, are still on the runner today unless you delete them.
  3. If the test step exits with code 1, the deploy step never runs.
  4. The deploy step should get the production database password from the repository's settings, for example ${{ secrets.DB_PASSWORD }}.
  5. Because the repository is private, it is fine to write the password into the workflow file.
Show answer

A, C, D. The slide: the runner "starts empty, runs the steps, and is thrown away". Each step's exit code says pass or fail, and one failure stops the job. B is wrong because nothing carries over between runs. Anything you want to keep (like a cache) must be set up on purpose. E is wrong because secrets come from the repository's settings, never from the code. A private repo is still copied to every clone and stays in git history.

Quiz-style Stopped at stage 1 
$ ./deploy.sh 2.6
Deploying TiffinGo v2.6   (live now: 2.5)
[1/6] Test          unit tests in a fresh container
  ✗ tests failed
Pipeline stopped. Production is unchanged: still v2.5.

What is the state after this run?

  1. No tiffingo:2.6 image was built, the 2.6 migration did not run, and v2.5 still serves every request.
  2. The image tiffingo:2.6 was built but not deployed.
  3. The 2.6 migration ran, but the new code was not started.
  4. v2.6 is serving traffic, but it is marked unhealthy.
Show answer

A. Test is stage 1. node --test exits non-zero, so deploy.sh stops before build (2), migrate (3), deploy (4), health (5) and switch (6). That is the gate: "one failure stops the job". B and C describe stages that come after the failed one, so they never ran. D is wrong because traffic is switched only at stage 6.

Twist "Production is unchanged" … or is it? 
$ ./deploy.sh 2.6
[1/6] Test          3 tests passed
[2/6] Build         tiffingo:2.6 = tiffingo:5be20c4
[3/6] Migrate       releases/2.6/migrate.sql
         ALTER TABLE orders DROP COLUMN total;
[4/6] Deploy        start v2.6 beside the old version (old one still serving)
[5/6] Check health
  ✗ new version is not healthy
Pipeline stopped. Production is unchanged: still v2.5.

v2.5's checkout runs INSERT INTO orders (customer, items, total) …. What is true now?

  1. Nothing changed. The pipeline undid the migration when the health check failed.
  2. v2.6 is serving traffic, because the switch happens before the health check.
  3. The v2.6 container was removed and v2.5 still serves, but total is already gone, so v2.5's checkouts now fail.
  4. v2.5 keeps working, because the migration ran inside the failed pipeline and so doesn't count.
Show answer

C. The pipeline migrates (step 3) before it starts the new code (step 4) and checks health (step 5). When health fails, deploy.sh only runs docker rm -f on the new container. It has no down migration, so "Production is unchanged" is true only for the code. A is wrong for that reason. B is wrong because the switch is stage 6, after health. D is wrong because the DROP committed in the database, whatever happened to the pipeline later. This is why every step-3 migration must work with the old code.

Quiz-style Two builds of one commit 
Mon  docker build -t shop:staging .     # commit 3f9a2c1, staging tests pass
Thu  docker build -t shop:prod .        # the same commit 3f9a2c1

The Dockerfile starts FROM node:22 and runs npm install. On Thursday production fails, and staging is still fine. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. This can't happen: the same commit always gives the same image.
  2. Thursday's build can differ. node:22 may now point to a newer image, and npm install may pick newer dependency versions.
  3. The slide's practice: build once, tag the image with the commit id (shop:3f9a2c1), and run that same image in staging and production.
  4. The fix is to rebuild production with --no-cache so it matches staging.
  5. With one image tagged by commit id, you know exactly which code is running, and the previous image is still there to redeploy.
Show answer

B, C, E. "Rebuilding for each environment can produce a different image." So what staging tested is not what production runs. A is wrong for exactly that reason: a commit fixes your files, not the base image or the registry. D makes it worse, because --no-cache gives a fresh build with today's base image and dependencies, which is even further from Monday's. Promote the one tested image instead of "repacking" it.

Drill The order of the steps

Which order does the slide (and deploy.sh) use?

  1. Build → Test → Deploy → Migrate → Check health
  2. Test → Build → Deploy → Migrate → Check health
  3. Test → Migrate → Build → Deploy → Check health
  4. Test → Build → Migrate → Deploy → Check health
Show answer

D. Test, build the image, migrate the database, deploy the new code, check health. The new code needs the new schema when it starts, so the migration comes first. A tests after building, so a failing test would come after you had already built the image. B starts new code before the schema it needs exists. C changes the database before you know the image even builds. Remember too that old code is still serving during steps 1 to 4.

Twist Safe to run while v2.3 is serving?

v2.3 runs INSERT INTO orders (customer, items, total) VALUES (…) and reads items and total. The orders table has 40,000 rows. The pipeline runs the next release's migration at step 3, while v2.3 is still serving. Which lines are safe to run then? Choose all that apply.

  1. ALTER TABLE orders ADD COLUMN group_id int;
  2. ALTER TABLE orders RENAME COLUMN items TO order_items;
  3. ALTER TABLE orders ADD COLUMN order_items text;
  4. ALTER TABLE orders ADD COLUMN tip int NOT NULL;
  5. ALTER TABLE orders DROP COLUMN total;
  6. CREATE TABLE flags (name text PRIMARY KEY, enabled boolean NOT NULL);
Show answer

A, C, F. Adding a nullable column or a new table is additive, so old code just ignores it (the expand step). B breaks v2.3 straight away, exactly as in the demo's 2.4-rename deploy. E breaks v2.3's insert and reads. D fails on a table that has rows (the existing rows would be NULL). Even on an empty table, v2.3's inserts don't name tip, so they would break the NOT NULL rule.

Twist The contract shipped with a flag

v3.1's migration ran ALTER TABLE customers DROP COLUMN phone;. v3.0's code still reads phone. v3.1 also added an "invoice PDF" feature behind the flag invoice_pdf. An hour later, the invoice feature turns out to be broken. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. Redeploying the v3.0 image is the safest way back, because it comes from before both changes.
  2. Reverting the v3.1 commit and running the pipeline brings phone back, because the migration file is then gone.
  3. Switching invoice_pdf off still works. Nothing is deployed, and the v3.1 code, which matches the new schema, keeps running.
  4. A down migration that re-adds phone brings back the phone numbers.
  5. After the DROP, the old values can only come back from a backup. Going back in code can't do it.
Show answer

C, E. This is the outline's question: which way back is unavailable once the schema has changed? The answer is the previous image, and a revert, because both bring back code that reads phone. A: v3.0 would start, then fail on every query that reads phone. B: deleting a migration file undoes nothing. The runner only runs pending files, and the column stays dropped. D: a down migration recreates the column, but every value is NULL.

Drill Why the redeploy is fast

./rollback.sh 2.3 prints "nothing to test, build or migrate: the image already exists". It takes about 2 seconds locally, and the slide says about a minute on a hosted platform. Why is it faster than reverting the commit and deploying?

  1. tiffingo:2.3 already exists, so only start, health check and switch run. A revert makes a new commit that goes through test, build, migrate and deploy again.
  2. Rollback skips the health check, which is the slowest stage.
  3. Docker kept the old v2.3 container running and warm, so nothing has to start.
  4. Rollback restores a database snapshot along with the image, which saves the migrate step.
Show answer

A. "Redeploy the previous image: about a minute, nothing is rebuilt." A revert means "the pipeline runs again", which takes minutes. B is wrong because rollback.sh does run a health check (stage 2 of 3). C is wrong because the demo's switch removes the old container (docker rm -f). The rollback starts a new container from the old image. D is wrong because rollback never touches the database, which is the whole point of the rename part of the demo.

Quiz-style Split the bill charges the wrong friend

v2.5 ships five changes. One of them, "split the bill", is behind the flag split_bill. Users report that it charges the wrong friend, and 600 wrong charges have been made so far. Which is the best fix, and what does it leave behind?

  1. Redeploy v2.4. It removes the button and refunds the 600 charges.
  2. Switch split_bill off. New wrong charges stop within seconds and the other four changes keep running. The 600 charges still need a separate fix, such as refunds.
  3. Revert the commit and deploy. It is the fastest, because no image is involved.
  4. Switch split_bill off. It stops new charges and reverses the 600, because the flag sends everything back to the old code path.
Show answer

B. The slide-16 poll answer is "switch the flag off": seconds, nothing deployed, and nothing else in v2.5 is thrown away. A throws away four good changes and refunds nothing: code going back doesn't touch data. C is the slowest way back, because the pipeline runs again, including building a new image. D is wrong because a flag changes what happens next. The charges already made are data, and "data is not reversible".

Quiz-style What the redeploy left behind

v2.6 ran for 25 minutes at dinner time. Its bug did three things. It sent "Your group order is on the way!" by SMS to 3,000 customers whose orders were never placed. It overwrote delivery_note with NULL on 900 rows. And it deleted 40 saved carts. Its migration only added a nullable column. You redeploy tiffingo:2.5, and /healthz reports 2.5. What is still wrong? Choose all that apply.

  1. The 3,000 SMS messages have been sent.
  2. v2.5 crashes because of the new nullable column.
  3. v2.6 keeps serving some requests after the switch.
  4. The 900 delivery notes are still NULL.
  5. The 40 carts are still gone.
Show answer

A, D, E. "Code is reversible. Data is not. Sent messages are forever." Overwritten and deleted rows come back only from a backup, and an SMS can't be unsent. B is wrong because old code ignores an extra nullable column. That is why the demo's first rollback from 2.4 (which only added group_id) worked. C is wrong because the switch sends all traffic to the redeployed version and removes the old container.

Quiz-style Green gates, red checkouts 
$ ./deploy.sh 2.4
[1/6] Test          3 tests passed
[2/6] Build         tiffingo:2.4 = tiffingo:ab12cd3
[3/6] Migrate       ALTER TABLE orders ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS group_id int;
[4/6] Deploy        ...
[5/6] Check health  {"status":"ok","version":"2.4"}
[6/6] Switch        send traffic to v2.4

# Terminal 2, every 5th line:
ERROR  Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'map')  (v2.4)

Why didn't the pipeline's gates stop this release?

  1. The tests ran on the developer's laptop instead of in a fresh container.
  2. The image was rebuilt for production, so it differs from the tested one.
  3. The tests only cover pricing, and /healthz only runs SELECT 1. Nothing tried a group order for a customer with no saved group (kabir).
  4. The migration failed silently, so group_id is missing.
Show answer

C. Gates only catch what they check. pricing.test.js tests totals, and the health check only proves the process can reach the database. A is wrong because stage 1 runs node --test in a throwaway node:22-alpine container. B is wrong because the image was built once, with two tags. D is wrong because the error is a JavaScript TypeError (members is undefined), not an SQL error. This is why you still need detection, a fast rollback and flags.

Quiz-style random() versus a hash 
// version 1
if (Math.random() < 0.10) showSplitBill();

// version 2   (bucket = sha256("split_bill:" + userId), first 4 bytes, % 100)
if (bucket("split_bill", userId) < 10) showSplitBill();

Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. Version 2 turns the feature on for exactly 10% of users, never 10.05%.
  2. With version 1, asha may see the button on one page load and not on the next.
  3. With version 2, changing 10 to 50 keeps everyone who already had the button.
  4. With version 1, about 10% of requests show the button, but not to a fixed group of users.
  5. Version 2 is preferred mainly because hashing is faster than Math.random().
Show answer

B, C, D. The slide's rule is hash(user_id) % 100 < 10, not random() < 0.1. The hash gives each user a fixed bucket, so the feature is sticky, and raising the line only adds buckets. Random flickers: "the button appears and disappears". A is wrong because buckets only come out about even. The demo gets 1,005 of 10,000 users, which is 10.05%. E is wrong because the reason is a stable answer per user, not speed.

Quiz-style Who is ON at 30%? 
$ node rollout.js explain asha ravi meena kabir zoya      # flags.json: rollout_percent = 30
user    bucket (sha256("group_order:" + user) ... % 100)
asha    67
ravi    14
meena    5
kabir   36
zoya    30

isOn returns bucket(flag, user) < rollout_percent. Who has the feature?

  1. meena only
  2. meena and ravi
  3. meena, ravi and zoya
  4. meena, ravi, zoya and kabir
Show answer

B. ON means bucket 0 to 29, which is 30 buckets, or 30%. meena (5) and ravi (14) are below 30. zoya's bucket is exactly 30, and 30 < 30 is false, so C is the boundary trap. A is the answer at 10%, as in the demo. D counts kabir (36), who only comes in at 37% or more. (These buckets are the real values the demo prints.)

Twist Turning the dial down

The same five users (buckets: asha 67, ravi 14, meena 5, kabir 36, zoya 30). At noon group_order went from 10% to 50%. After some complaints you lower it to 20%. Compared with 50%, what happens?

  1. meena and ravi keep the feature, kabir and zoya lose it, and asha never had it.
  2. Nobody loses it, because buckets never move.
  3. A new random 20% of users is chosen, so a different group has it now.
  4. Everyone loses it until a new release is deployed with the new number.
Show answer

A. "Buckets don't move, only the line does." At 50%, buckets 0 to 49 are ON (meena, ravi, zoya, kabir). At 20%, only 0 to 19 are ON (meena, ravi). B mixes this up with raising the line: going up never turns anyone off, but going down turns off exactly the buckets between the two lines. C is wrong because the hash is fixed, so the same people stay in, with no randomness. D is wrong because the percentage lives in flags.json, outside the code, so no deploy is needed.

Twist Two flags, the same users

A team's helper computes bucket = sha256(userId) % 100, with no flag name in the hash. They run new_menu at 10% and group_order at 10% for 10,000 users. What does the demo's overlap step show for this method?

  1. About 99 users are in both, roughly 10% × 10%, as chance would give.
  2. No user is in both, because each flag picks its own buckets.
  3. About 2,000 users have at least one feature, so the two groups don't touch.
  4. The same 978 users are in both. Every 10% experiment lands on the same people.
Show answer

D. Without the flag name, a user gets the same bucket for every flag. So buckets 0 to 9 are the same 978 people each time: "the same 1,000 people test every new feature". A is what the demo shows when the flag name is in the hash ("group_order:" + user): 1,005 and 1,016 users, 99 in both. B and C are wrong because the two flags compute the same number per user, so their groups are identical, not separate.

Quiz-style A kill switch behind a 30-second cache

Each of three app servers caches its flag values for 30 seconds. At 19:20:00 you switch split_bill off in the flags table. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. Every request still reads the flags table, so the change lands at once.
  2. No server can see the change before 19:20:30.
  3. Some requests may still use the old value until about 19:20:30.
  4. Raising the cache to 5 minutes cuts database reads further, but then the switch can take up to 5 minutes to land.
  5. The demo's v2.5 runs SELECT enabled FROM flags on every group-order request, so flag.sh off landed on the next request, at the cost of a database query each time.
Show answer

C, D, E. Slide 20: reading on every request lands changes at once but hits the database every time. Caching for 30 seconds means one read per 30 seconds, but a change takes up to 30 seconds. "Choose the delay you can live with." A describes reading on every request, not a cache. B is wrong because each server's cache runs out at its own time. A server whose cache ran out at 19:20:05 sees the change then, so 19:20:30 is the latest, not the earliest.

Quiz-style Lessons from Knight Capital

Which practices deal with the causes given on the Knight Capital slide? Choose all that apply.

  1. Write more unit tests for the new code. They would have caught it.
  2. Never give a new feature an old flag name.
  3. Cache flag values for 5 minutes so a bad change lands slowly.
  4. Before turning the flag on, check that every server runs the new code.
  5. Use a hosted flag service, because services can't reuse names.
  6. Delete retired code paths and their flags once you are done with them.
Show answer

B, D, F. The slide: new code reused an old flag name, and one of eight servers didn't get the new code. On that server, the flag switched on code retired years earlier. A misses the point, because the failing server wasn't running the new code at all. C only slows everything down, including your kill switch. E: a service still lets you pick any name, and the problem was the old code still being there, which is flag debt.

Drill Knight Capital in numbers

Which line matches the slide?

  1. About $44 million in 45 minutes. One of eight servers missed the new code.
  2. About $440 million in 45 hours. A hacker reused a flag.
  3. About $440 million in 45 minutes. The flag service went down.
  4. About $440 million in 4.5 minutes. All eight servers ran retired code.
  5. About $440 million in 45 minutes (09:30 to 10:15, 1 August 2012). New code reused an old flag name, and one of eight servers didn't get the new code.
Show answer

E. Knight Capital, 1 August 2012: about $440 million lost in 45 minutes. The cause was a reused flag name plus one of eight servers that was not updated, so the flag switched on code retired years earlier. A is ten times too small. B has the wrong time, and there was no hacker. C names a cause the slide doesn't give. D is wrong because seven servers were fine, and only one ran the old code.

Quiz-style Fourteen flags, no owners

The flags table has 14 rows. Nine have been at 100% for months, and nobody knows who owns six of them. Which actions follow the slide? Choose all that apply.

  1. Give every flag an owner and a removal date (for example remove by: 30 Nov).
  2. Keep the 100% flags forever as free kill switches. Flags cost nothing.
  3. When a new feature needs a flag, reuse the name of a deleted one to keep the table short.
  4. For each flag at 100%, delete the flag and the old code path.
  5. Delete the flag rows but leave the old code paths in place, in case someone needs them years later.
Show answer

A, D. Slide 23: "Every flag gets an owner and a removal date. Once a rollout reaches 100%, delete the flag and the old code path." B ignores flag debt: old flags and dead branches make the code harder to reason about. C is exactly the Knight Capital mistake. E keeps the dangerous part, the retired code that a flag could switch back on.

Twist What kind of rollout is deploy.sh?

In the demo, stage 6 (switch_to) rewrites the Caddyfile to reverse_proxy <new>:3000, runs caddy reload, waits 1 second, then runs docker rm -f <old>. Meanwhile traffic.sh sends every 5th request as a group order. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. It is a canary, because traffic.sh sends 1 request in 5 to the new feature.
  2. It is a blue-green style switch: all traffic moves to the new container at once.
  3. Unlike the slide's blue-green, the old version is not kept warm, so going back means starting the old image again (rollback.sh).
  4. The health check before the switch plays the readiness role: a new container that never answers /healthz gets no traffic.
  5. A canary would send, say, 5% of traffic to v2.5 and 95% to v2.4, and grow only if healthy.
Show answer

B, C, D, E. The proxy always points at exactly one container, which is "switch all at once". The old container is removed after the switch, not kept warm. "In both [blue-green and canary], readiness checks decide which servers get traffic." Here a failed health check removes the new container before any traffic reaches it. A is wrong because traffic.sh is a load generator (fake customers). Every request, group or single, goes to the one live version.

Surviving load: the database under load (T6)

Drill Count the queries 
const orders = await db.query(
  "SELECT * FROM orders WHERE user_id = $1 LIMIT 20", [userId]);
for (const o of orders.rows) {
  o.items = await db.query(
    "SELECT * FROM order_items WHERE order_id = $1", [o.id]);
}

The user has 35 orders. How many queries does one page load send to the database?

  1. 2
  2. 20
  3. 21
  4. 36
Show answer

C. This is the N+1 shape: 1 query for the list, then 1 more per row. LIMIT 20 caps the list at 20 rows, so 1 + 20 = 21. A is what a fixed version (one list query plus one batched query) would send. B forgets the list query. D uses all 35 orders and ignores the LIMIT.

Quiz-style 40 bookings, 25 ms away

A page lists a user's 40 bookings, then fetches each booking's passengers in a loop, one query per booking. In production the database is 25 ms away (per round trip), and each query itself takes almost no time. About how long does the page spend on database trips?

  1. About 25 ms
  2. About 50 ms
  3. About 40 s
  4. About 1 s
Show answer

D. 1 + 40 = 41 queries, and each one waits for the previous one, so 41 × 25 ms = 1,025 ms, about 1 second. (The slide's poll: 51 × 30 ms ≈ 1.5 s.) A counts one trip. B is what the fixed version (2 queries) would cost. C multiplies by 1 second instead of 25 ms.

Twist Fast on the laptop

A booking page runs 51 queries (the N+1 shape). You fix it to 2 queries: the bookings, then SELECT * FROM passengers WHERE booking_id = ANY($1) with all 50 ids. On your laptop a trip takes about 1 ms, and in production about 30 ms. Counting round trips only, how much time does the fix save per page in production?

  1. About 49 ms
  2. About 1,470 ms
  3. About 1,530 ms
  4. Nothing, because the database reads the same passenger rows either way.
Show answer

B. Before: 51 × 30 = 1,530 ms. After: 2 × 30 = 60 ms. The saving is 1,470 ms. A is the saving on the laptop ((51 − 2) × 1 ms), which is why nobody notices N+1 locally. C forgets that the fixed version still makes 2 trips. D misses the point: the cost of N+1 is the number of round trips times the latency, not the work per query.

Quiz-style Which changes fix the N+1?

The slide's code runs SELECT * FROM bookings WHERE user_id = $1 LIMIT 50, then SELECT * FROM passengers WHERE booking_id = $1 once per booking in a for loop with await. The database is 30 ms away. Which changes remove the N+1? Choose all that apply.

  1. Add an index on passengers.booking_id.
  2. Fetch all passengers in one query with WHERE booking_id = ANY($1), passing the 50 booking ids.
  3. Raise the connection pool from 10 to 50.
  4. Use one query that JOINs bookings and passengers.
  5. Use the ORM's eager loading, which sends one IN (…) query for all the bookings' passengers.
Show answer

B, D, E. Each of these brings the page down to 1 or 2 round trips. A makes each passenger query cheaper, but it is still 51 trips × 30 ms. C doesn't help either, because the loop awaits each query before sending the next one, so the extra connections sit idle. More connections also add load to a limited database.

Drill Pages, scans and B-trees

Which statements match the slides? Choose all that apply.

  1. A B-tree lookup takes one more step for every row added, so a table with a crore of rows needs about a crore of steps.
  2. An index stores a second full copy of every column in the table.
  3. Without an index, finding one PNR means reading every page of the table. That is a sequential scan.
  4. An index is a sorted copy of some columns, with pointers back to the rows.
  5. A B-tree is short and wide, so a lookup takes a handful of steps (3 in the slide's PNR example).
Show answer

C, D, E. A table is stored as pages on disk, and with no index Postgres reads them all. An index is like the index at the back of a book. Because the B-tree is short and wide, it takes "a handful of steps, even among crores of rows". A describes a sequential scan, not a B-tree. B is wrong because an index copies only the indexed columns (plus pointers), not every column.

Quiz-style The phone directory rule

RailBook has CREATE INDEX ON journeys (from_station, journey_date);. For which queries can Postgres use this index to go straight to the matching part of the index? Choose all that apply.

  1. WHERE from_station = 'PUNE'
  2. WHERE journey_date = '2026-11-14'
  3. WHERE train_no = '12124'
  4. WHERE from_station = 'PUNE' AND journey_date = '2026-11-14'
  5. WHERE from_station = 'PUNE' AND train_no = '12124' (it finds the PUNE entries, then checks train_no on those rows)
Show answer

A, D, E. The index is sorted by station first, then by date within each station, like a phone directory sorted by surname, then first name. Station alone is fast, and station plus date is fast (and is the one that uses the index fully, as in the slide's poll). E uses the station part, then filters. B is "date alone: no help". The dates are spread across every station, so there is no place to jump to. At best Postgres reads the whole index. C uses a column that isn't in the index at all.

Quiz-style Which column goes first?

Every query on journeys filters on journey_date. Some of them also filter on from_station. You want one composite index. Which one follows the slide's rule?

  1. (journey_date, from_station)
  2. (from_station, journey_date)
  3. (from_station) alone
  4. A separate index on every column of journeys
Show answer

A. "Put the column you always filter on first." With journey_date leading, date-only queries and date-plus-station queries can both use the index. B helps only queries that include the station, so the date-only queries get no help. C ignores the date. D pays the write cost of many indexes: every insert and update must update every index.

Quiz-style Seven indexes at 10:00

The bookings table has 7 indexes. At 10:00, Tatkal brings thousands of inserts per minute. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. More indexes make inserts faster, because Postgres finds where to put the row sooner.
  2. Each new booking row also adds an entry to all 7 indexes.
  3. The indexes take up disk space.
  4. An index on seat_no slows down SELECTs that filter only on train_id.
  5. The rule is to index the queries you run often, not every column.
Show answer

B, C, E. Slide 14: "Every insert and update also updates every index", and indexes use disk space. So index only what your frequent queries need. A has it backwards, because each index is extra work on every write. D is wrong because an unused index doesn't slow down reads that don't use it. Its cost is on writes and disk.

Quiz-style Read the EXPLAIN 
EXPLAIN ANALYZE SELECT * FROM tickets WHERE pnr = 4521873390;     -- 100,000 rows
 Seq Scan on tickets  (cost=0.00..1887.00 rows=1 width=18) (actual time=23.194..23.195 rows=0 loops=1)
   Filter: (pnr = '4521873390'::bigint)
   Rows Removed by Filter: 100000
 Planning Time: 1.022 ms
 Execution Time: 23.227 ms

CREATE INDEX idx_pnr ON tickets (pnr);

EXPLAIN ANALYZE SELECT * FROM tickets WHERE pnr = 4521873390;
 Index Scan using idx_pnr on tickets  (cost=0.29..8.31 rows=1 width=18) (actual time=0.244..0.245 rows=0 loops=1)
   Index Cond: (pnr = '4521873390'::bigint)
 Planning Time: 1.210 ms
 Execution Time: 0.336 ms

Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. Before the index, Postgres checked all 100,000 rows against the filter.
  2. The new index will also make inserts into tickets faster.
  3. After the index, the Index Cond line shows the condition was used to search the index.
  4. Most of the gain is in planning time.
  5. Execution time fell from about 23 ms to about 0.3 ms.
Show answer

A, C, E. This is what the slide-15 demo asks you to watch for: Seq Scan becomes Index Scan with an Index Cond, and the execution time drops. "Rows Removed by Filter: 100000" means every row was read and thrown away. B is wrong because every insert now also updates idx_pnr. D is wrong because planning time actually rose a little (1.0 to 1.2 ms). Execution time is what fell. (This output came from a real Postgres 16 run.)

Twist Building an index at 09:58

bookings has 40 million rows. At 09:58 someone runs a plain CREATE INDEX idx_b_user ON bookings (user_id);, which takes 6 minutes to build. What do users see between 10:00 and 10:04?

  1. Searches (SELECTs) on bookings fail, but new bookings go through.
  2. New bookings (INSERTs) wait until the build finishes, while reads keep working.
  3. Nothing. Postgres always builds indexes in the background.
  4. The build is cancelled automatically when the first INSERT arrives.
Show answer

B. Slide 16: a plain CREATE INDEX blocks writes to the table while it builds, and at Tatkal time that means stuck bookings. A is the wrong way round, because writes are blocked, not reads. C describes CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY, which you have to ask for. It doesn't block writes, but it takes longer and can't run inside a transaction. D: Postgres doesn't cancel the build. The writes queue behind it.

Drill A phone line to one operator

Why does Postgres have a hard connection limit, max_connections (default 100)?

  1. Each connection starts a separate Postgres server process with its own memory.
  2. Each connection permanently takes one CPU core.
  3. TCP only allows 100 ports per server.
  4. The limit is per app server, so each app server gets its own 100.
Show answer

A. "A connection is a phone line to one operator." Each one is a process with its own memory, so the database has a fixed number of operators. B is wrong because an idle connection uses memory, not a CPU core. C is wrong because TCP has tens of thousands of ports. D is wrong because the limit belongs to the database server and is shared by every client, which is why connections multiply when you scale out.

Twist Blue-green meets max_connections

TiffinGo runs 8 app containers, each with a pool of max: 10. Postgres has max_connections = 100. A blue-green deploy (T5) starts 8 new containers beside the 8 old ones, health-checks them, then switches traffic. Dinner traffic is high, so the pools fill. What can happen during the overlap?

  1. Nothing. The total stays at 80, because the old containers stop using the database when the new ones start.
  2. Nothing. The total stays at 80, because the pools on different containers share their connections.
  3. Up to 160 are needed. Postgres queues the extra 60 until the old containers are removed.
  4. Up to 160 are needed. Connections past the limit are refused with FATAL: sorry, too many clients already, so health checks or requests can fail in the middle of the deploy.
Show answer

D. During a deploy, old and new run side by side (T5), and every container brings its own pool (T6): 16 × 10 = 160. A is wrong because the old containers keep serving until the switch.
