B is wrong because a pool belongs to one process and is not shared (only a pooler such as PgBouncer shares connections). C is wrong because Postgres refuses connection 101. It doesn't queue it. One fix is to size for the overlap: 16 × p ≤ 100 − 10 spare gives p = 5.

Drill Count with me: serverless

At 10:00, 200 serverless functions run at once, and each one opens its own pool of 5 connections. The limit is 100. How many connections are wanted?

  1. 205, just over the limit
  2. 200, twice the limit
  3. 1,000, ten times the limit
  4. 100, because the platform shares one pool among all functions
Show answer

C. 200 × 5 = 1,000, which is the slide's "count with me" line. A adds instead of multiplying. B counts one connection per function. D is wrong because each function instance has its own pool. Sharing only happens if you put a pooler in front of the database.

Twist Break it on purpose 
# max_connections has been set to 20
docker compose exec db sh -c \
  'for i in $(seq 30); do
     psql -U postgres -c "select pg_sleep(5)" >/dev/null &
   done; wait'

Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. The failures happen because pg_sleep(5) runs longer than Postgres's default query timeout.
  2. The & makes each psql wait for the previous one to finish.
  3. About 10 of the 30 print psql: error: … FATAL: sorry, too many clients already.
  4. The extra 10 wait in a queue inside Postgres and run once a slot frees up.
  5. Without the &, the 30 would run one after another, and none would fail.
Show answer

C, E. & starts each psql in the background, so all 30 try to connect at once. Only 20 fit, so about 10 are refused. (I checked this on Postgres 16: exactly 10 failures.) Run one at a time, only one connection is ever open. A is wrong because Postgres has no default query timeout, and the error is about connecting, not about the query. B describes running without &. D is wrong because connections past the limit are refused, not queued.

Quiz-style The fixes for too many clients

The autoscaler can take RailBook from 4 to 8 app servers at 10:00, and max_connections is 100. Which of these are fixes from the slides? Choose all that apply.

  1. Raise max_connections to 5,000, because connections are cheap.
  2. Size every pool on purpose: add them all up and stay under the limit (8 × 10 = 80 ≤ 100 − 10 spare).
  3. Set a wait timeout, so a request waits for a free pooled connection instead of failing.
  4. Remove the pool, so each request opens and closes its own connection.
  5. Put a pooler (PgBouncer, Supavisor) in front of the database.
  6. Leave room for the extra servers the autoscaler may add.
Show answer

B, C, E, F. These are the four fixes on slide 22. A ignores that each connection is a process with its own memory, so connections are not cheap. D makes things worse. Every request then pays to open a connection, and a spike opens as many connections as there are requests, which hits the limit even sooner.

Twist Moving to transaction mode

A reporting service talks to Postgres through PgBouncer. On one client connection it does this:

PREPARE seat_count (int) AS SELECT count(*) FROM bookings WHERE train_id = $1;   -- transaction 1
EXECUTE seat_count(12124);                                                       -- transaction 2

The team switches PgBouncer from session mode to transaction mode. What is the risk?

  1. None. The pooler keeps the same database connection for a client until it disconnects.
  2. Each transaction may run on a different database connection, so the prepared statement may not exist when EXECUTE runs.
  3. The pooler now refuses more than 5 app connections.
  4. Nothing new. Session mode had the same problem, and the two modes differ only in speed.
Show answer

B. In transaction mode a connection is "lent per transaction", so it "can't keep anything between transactions, such as prepared statements or session locks". A describes session mode ("kept per client"), which is the mode they just left. C confuses the slide's picture (12 app connections sharing 5 database connections) with a cap on clients. D is wrong because session mode keeps one connection per client, so the prepared statement would still be there.

Quiz-style "We still have 10% left"

At 09:55 the database is 90% busy, with requests arriving at random. A manager says: "We have 10% headroom. At 99% busy, waits will only be about 10% longer." What does the slide say?

  1. The manager is right. Waiting time grows in step with how busy the server is.
  2. Waiting depends only on how fast each query runs, not on how busy the server is.
  3. At 99% the queue gets shorter, because the database is working at full speed.
  4. The manager is wrong. At 50% busy the queue is short, at 90% it is long, and at 99% it explodes.
Show answer

D. Slide 24: with one counter and random arrivals, waiting time stays nearly flat up to about 50%, rises at 90% and goes almost vertical near 99%. A assumes a straight line, but the curve is not linear. B ignores queueing: a request that waits behind others is slow even when each query is fast. C has it backwards. Keep headroom, because the last few percent cost the most.

Drill Wasn't that a transaction? 
BEGIN;
SELECT booked, capacity FROM trains WHERE id = 12124;    -- 99, 100
-- app: 99 < 100, so book
UPDATE trains SET booked = booked + 1 WHERE id = 12124;
INSERT INTO bookings (train_id, user_id) VALUES (12124, $1);
COMMIT;

10 requests arrive at once for the last seat, and more than one booking succeeds. Why?

  1. The transaction wasn't committed.
  2. Postgres doesn't support transactions under load.
  3. The transactions all read the same count before any of them wrote.
  4. There was no index on trains.id.
Show answer

C. This is the slide-30 poll. Every request reads booked = 99, decides 99 < 100, and books. A transaction is all or nothing, but "a transaction alone does not stop two requests reading the same count". (I ran this on Postgres 16 with 10 parallel clients: all 10 booked, and booked ended at 109.) A is wrong because each transaction did commit, which is how the extra bookings got saved. B is wrong because transactions work fine under load. D is wrong because an index changes speed, not correctness, and id is the primary key, which already has one.

Quiz-style Check-then-act

Which statements about the last-seat bug are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. Wrapping the check and the insert in BEGIN … COMMIT closes the gap.
  2. Between the read (check) and the write (act), another request can change the state. This is the "danger window".
  3. The unit tests passed because they send one request at a time.
  4. A transaction gives all or nothing (like a UPI transfer, where the ₹1,450 debit and credit happen together). It does not make requests take turns.
  5. The race needs two app servers. One Node process with a pool can't oversell.
Show answer

B, C, D. Check-then-act breaks when two requests interleave, and "your tests pass because they send one request at a time". A is the trap from the poll: the demo's code was already inside BEGIN/COMMIT and still oversold. E is wrong because one Node process handles many requests at once. While one request awaits its SELECT, another runs on a different pooled connection, so they can interleave just like two servers.

Quiz-style Predict: one statement, ten requests 
UPDATE trains
   SET booked = booked + 1
 WHERE id = 12124 AND booked < capacity
RETURNING booked;

Train 12124 has booked = 99 and capacity = 100. Ten requests run this at the same moment. What happens?

  1. One request gets 1 row back (booked = 100). Nine get 0 rows back, and the API answers them with HTTP 409.
  2. All ten get a row back, and booked ends at 109.
  3. Nine fail with a deadlock error and must retry.
  4. One gets a row back. The other nine get HTTP 500, because 0 rows is a database error.
Show answer

A. Fix 1 puts the check inside the write. The first UPDATE locks the row. The others wait, then re-check booked < capacity against the new value (100 < 100 is false), so they update 0 rows. (I checked this on Postgres 16: exactly one row came back, and booked = 100.) B is the unfixed check-then-act result. C is wrong because they simply wait on one row, so nothing deadlocks. D is wrong because 0 rows is a normal answer meaning "the train is full", and the slide's response is 409, not 500.

Twist FOR UPDATE: who waits? 
BEGIN;
SELECT capacity FROM trains WHERE id = $1 FOR UPDATE;
SELECT count(*) FROM bookings WHERE train_id = $1;
-- if count < capacity: INSERT INTO bookings ...
COMMIT;

Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. FOR UPDATE locks the whole bookings table until COMMIT.
  2. Other requests for the same train wait at their SELECT … FOR UPDATE until this transaction commits.
  3. Without FOR UPDATE, this code is still safe, because it runs inside BEGIN/COMMIT.
  4. A booking for a different train (12951) doesn't wait for this lock.
  5. Keep this transaction short: while it holds the row, every other booking for that train is waiting.
Show answer

B, D, E. "FOR UPDATE makes everyone else wait for this train's row until you commit." It locks the selected row, not a table (A), so other trains go ahead. Because everyone queues on that row, hold it as briefly as you can. C is check-then-act again: without the lock, two transactions can both count 99 and both insert. (On Postgres 16 with 10 parallel clients, the FOR UPDATE version booked exactly 1.)

Quiz-style Safety nets and other names

Which statements match the slides? Choose all that apply.

  1. CHECK (booked <= capacity) makes the database refuse booked = 101, even if the app code has a bug.
  2. With constraints in place you can drop Fix 1 and Fix 2. The constraint error makes a fine message for users.
  3. SERIALIZABLE isolation never produces errors. It only makes transactions wait.
  4. Optimistic locking uses a version column. Your update fails if someone changed the row first.
  5. A Redis distributed lock is the slide's fix for a seat count stored in one Postgres database.
  6. UNIQUE (train_id, seat_no) stops two bookings for seat 42 on the same train.
Show answer

A, D, F. Constraints are a safety net: "if your code has a bug, the database still refuses the bad row". B is wrong because they are a backstop, not the main logic. A full train should get a clean 409 from Fix 1 or Fix 2, not a constraint error. C is wrong because Postgres aborts one of the conflicting transactions, and you retry it. E is wrong because distributed locks are for state outside one database.

Quiz-style Little's law: how many connections are busy? (outline)

(Course outline, not on the slides.) The booking API gets 200 requests per second at a steady rate. Each request holds a database connection for 50 ms on average. On average, how many connections are in use at any moment?

  1. 4
  2. 50
  3. 200
  4. 250
  5. 10
Show answer

E. Little's law: L = λ × W, where L is the number in the system, λ the arrival rate and W the time each one spends. Here 200/s × 0.05 s = 10. A divides 200 by 50. B mixes up milliseconds with a count. C is the number of arrivals per second, not how many are in use at once. D adds the numbers instead of multiplying. Ten in use on average still means you need more connections than that for bursts, because of queueing near full use.

Twist Median unchanged, p99 tripled (outline)

(Course outline, not on the slides.) A k6 test sends a sudden burst, like Tatkal at 10:00. Compared with normal traffic, the median response time stays at 80 ms, but p99 goes from 300 ms to 900 ms. Which statements are reasonable? Choose all that apply.

  1. A sudden jump like this is a spike test. A soak test instead holds steady load for hours to find leaks or slow decline.
  2. Most requests are fine, but a few wait: for a free pooled connection, for a row lock (FOR UPDATE), or in a queue near full use.
  3. The median didn't move, so nothing is wrong. p99 is just noise.
  4. A few requests whose query can't use an index (a Seq Scan) could explain a slow tail.
  5. A ramp test raises the load step by step to find where things break.
  6. Because the median didn't change, the database must have more than 50% spare capacity, so queueing can't be the cause.
Show answer

A, B, D, E. A stable median with a growing tail means a minority of requests is waiting for something. Pool waits, lock waits and queues near saturation all hit only some requests. C is wrong because 1 user in 100 now waits about a second, and at Tatkal scale that is thousands of people. F is wrong because queueing near 100% busy shows up first in the tail, while the typical request still looks fine.

Review of earlier lectures: runtime, config, secrets and AI workflow (T0/T1)

These lectures were in quiz 1A, not 2A. Their ideas still show up inside later questions (ports, lockfiles, secrets in history), so here is a short set.

Quiz-style Prove .env was never committed

.env is listed in .gitignore. You want proof that it was never committed on any branch. Which command shows that (empty output = never)?

  1. git log --all --full-history -- .env
  2. cat .gitignore
  3. git status
  4. ls -a
Show answer

A. It searches every branch's history for commits that touched .env. .gitignore only stops untracked files being added. It says nothing about the past (B). git status describes only the present (C). If a secret was ever pushed, rotate it, because deleting the file doesn't remove it from history, forks or clones.

Quiz-style Configuration as input

Which statements match the twelve-factor rule "store config in the environment"? Choose all that apply.

  1. A committed .env.example with safe defaults documents each variable.
  2. One build can run in dev, staging and production with different DATABASE_URL values.
  3. Changing the database URL means changing a variable and restarting, not rebuilding.
  4. Production passwords belong in a committed config.js so every teammate has them.
Show answer

A, B, C. Config compiled into code is a constant that is true in exactly one place. An environment variable is read fresh each time the process starts. D is the classic leak: scanners find committed credentials within minutes.

Drill Connection refused

On a server, nothing has bound port 5432. An attacker scans that port. What do they see?

  1. A crash log with the Postgres version
  2. Connection refused: nothing is listening
  3. A Postgres login prompt
  4. A timeout, every time
Show answer

B. A port is only "open" when a process has a listening socket bound to it. With no listener, the OS refuses the connection. (A firewall that silently drops packets would make it look like a timeout instead. That is a different setup from "nothing is listening".)

Quiz-style A good CLAUDE.md rule

Which lines belong in a committed CLAUDE.md or AGENTS.md? Choose all that apply.

  1. STRIPE_KEY=sk-live-...
  2. pytest -q (the exact test command)
  3. "Please try to be careful with the database."
  4. "Never run db:push. Edits to schema.py must generate a migration."
Show answer

B, D. The file is operational ground truth: exact commands, a directory map, and hard rules. Concrete negative constraints ("never", "must") get obeyed, while vague suggestions get skimmed (C). Secrets never go in a file the agent or the repo can read (A).

Twist The deny rule that didn't protect .env

.claude/settings.json denies Read(**/.env*). The agent still printed your .env. Which statements are true? Choose all that apply.

  1. If a secret sits in a file the agent can open, assume it will be read.
  2. Real protection denies both doors, or runs Bash in an OS-level sandbox that can't see the path.
  3. A Read deny rule blocks the Read tool, not Bash("cat .env").
  4. The deny rule must be in settings.local.json to work.
Show answer

A, B, C. The same file can be reached through a different tool. Shared deny rules belong in the committed .claude/settings.json, while settings.local.json is for personal preferences (D is wrong).

Quiz-style Context rot

You are 70 turns into a chat, and the agent is ignoring a rule from turn 3. Which actions match the lecture? Choose all that apply.

  1. Validate the output (grep, tests) instead of trusting it.
  2. Switch to a model with a bigger context window, so nothing gets dropped.
  3. Let an explore subagent read the codebase and return only a summary.
  4. Start a fresh session from SPEC.md, TASKS.md and CLAUDE.md.
Show answer

A, C, D. Attention cost grows with n², and reliability drops as the context fills. The fix is a shorter, cleaner context, not a bigger one (B). A bigger window gives more room, but it doesn't move the point where quality starts to drop.

Rapid-fire facts

Read these the night before and again just before the quiz. Each one has decided a multiple-choice option before.

Git internals and history

  • Git = a DAG of content-addressed objects: blob, tree, commit, tag
  • Object id = hash of its content. Change 1 byte and you get a new object with a new id
  • A blob has no name. The tree holds names and modes
  • A commit = tree + parent(s) + author/committer + message. It is a snapshot, not a diff
  • A branch = a small file holding one hash. Committing overwrites only that file
  • Change one commit and it and every later commit get new IDs
  • Amend or rebase = new commits. The old ones stay (reflog) until gc's grace period
  • Myers = shortest edit script, O((m+n)·d) time, O(m+n) memory, git's default
  • On a tie, Myers prefers delete over insert
  • 3-way merge = diff(base,ours) + diff(base,theirs). Same region on both sides = conflict
  • Merge = accurate ledger (2-parent commit). Rebase = readable story (new hashes, straight line)
  • Squash = rebase with the commits collapsed into one
  • Rebase moves only the branch you're on. main stays put
  • git branch -d refuses unmerged work. -D forces it

Remotes, undo and recovery

  • Four locations: working dir, staging, local repo, remote
  • git status never contacts the remote. "ahead/behind" uses the last fetch
  • fetch = download + move origin/main only. pull = fetch + integrate
  • Diverged + plain git pull = "Need to specify how to reconcile". Use --no-rebase or --rebase
  • Rejected push: integrate first. Never --force on shared main
  • restore f = staged copy goes to the working dir. restore --staged f = unstage
  • reset --soft keeps staged, --mixed keeps files, --hard discards
  • Pushed bad commit: git revert (adds an undo commit)
  • Lost commit: git reflog, then git branch name <hash>
  • After switching away, HEAD@{1} = where HEAD was just before
  • Rebased your own pushed branch: push --force-with-lease
  • commit -a skips untracked files. stash skips untracked unless -u
  • .gitignore ignores only untracked files. Use git rm --cached to stop tracking

Testing and CI

  • Arrange, Act, Assert. No assertion = no behavioural check
  • pytest passes a test with no assert if it doesn't raise
  • Cases: normal, boundary (just below and exactly on), invalid/missing, error
  • Unit = pure logic. Integration = real components + DB. E2E = browser journey
  • Pyramid: many unit, some integration, few E2E. It is a budget, not a law
  • Coverage = lines/branches/functions executed, not asserted
  • Mutation testing: a surviving mutant means the tests can't tell the change apart
  • Stub = canned value. Fake = working simplified version. Mock = records calls
  • Tests must be independent, clean up after themselves, and be deterministic (no uncontrolled clock or network)
  • Bug fix = commit 1 failing test (red), commit 2 fix (green)
  • CI = validate, build, test, report status on a change. CD = deliver/deploy
  • A failing step fails the job, and later steps are skipped
  • continue-on-error: true lets the job pass anyway
  • Gate = pull_request trigger + required status check in branch protection
  • Clean runner = only declared dependencies. Read the first meaningful error

Containers and Docker

  • docker logs shows only stdout and stderr of the main process. Log files inside the container are invisible to it
  • ENTRYPOINT = fixed program. CMD = default args. Words after the image name replace CMD
  • Exit 137 = SIGKILL (OOM kill, or docker stop after 10 s). Exit 143 = SIGTERM. Check OOMKilled to tell
  • Default bridge: no DNS by container name. User-defined network: reach others by name on their container port
  • Empty named volume is pre-filled from the image once. A bind mount hides the image's folder
  • RUN rm doesn't remove a file from earlier layers. ENV and used ARG values show in docker history
  • Container = isolated process(es) on the shared host kernel. A VM has its own kernel
  • Namespaces = what you see (PID, mount, net, hostname, user)
  • cgroups = how much you use: over CPU = throttled, over memory = may be killed
  • Main process is PID 1 inside, with a different PID on the host
  • Image layers are read-only and shared (copy-on-write). Each container has its own writable layer
  • A changed input invalidates that step and all steps after it
  • Copy manifests, then install, then copy source. CMD only records metadata
  • COPY cache uses file contents, not timestamps. .dockerignore trims the context
  • npm ci installs exactly the lockfile and fails on a mismatch
  • EXPOSE documents only. -p host:container publishes
  • -p 127.0.0.1:8080:3000 = this machine only. Without the prefix = all interfaces
  • Server inside must listen on 0.0.0.0, not 127.0.0.1
  • stop/start keeps the writable layer. rm deletes it
  • A named volume outlives containers. Only the mounted path is kept
  • New image = new container (rm + run). restart reuses the old image

Database migrations

  • History row: installed_rank, version, description, type, script (file name), checksum, installed_by, installed_on, execution_time, success. No SQL text
  • Versions compare as numbers, part by part: 1.9 < 1.10 < 2 < 10. File dates never matter
  • R__ repeatable: no version, runs after the versioned ones, reruns when its checksum changes (Outdated, then the old row becomes Superseded)
  • New file below the applied version = Ignored, and migrate refuses. -outOfOrder=true runs it, and the state is Out of Order
  • Deleted applied file: in the middle = Missing (validate fails), above the newest file = Future (warning only). repair marks Missing as Deleted
  • baseline defaults to version 1: set -baselineVersion. clean drops everything and is disabled by default
  • Code, schema and data change independently. Code is reversible, data often isn't
  • Auto-sync rename = drop + add an empty column. Prototype only
  • Runner: read history, run pending files in order, insert a history row, COMMIT
  • History = append-only, ordered, checksum-verified
  • Edited applied file = checksum mismatch, deploy stops before running anything
  • flyway repair fixes checksums and runs no SQL
  • Hand-run SQL = schema changed, no history row, migration still pending
  • Postgres DDL is transactional. Killed before COMMIT = it reruns cleanly
  • Error inside BEGIN: transaction aborted. COMMIT then rolls back
  • Without BEGIN each statement autocommits
  • ADD COLUMN needs ACCESS EXCLUSIVE. It waits for open readers, and later readers queue behind it
  • Locks are held until the transaction ends. lock_timeout limits only the wait
  • PG 11+: constant default = fast. Volatile default (clock_timestamp(), gen_random_uuid()) = full rewrite
  • Down migration can't restore deleted values, only a backup can
  • Expand, backfill, transition, contract. Irreversible at contract (drop)
  • Required column: nullable, writes supply it, batched backfill, CHECK NOT VALID, VALIDATE, SET NOT NULL
  • Use timestamp versions so two branches don't collide
  • PITR = physical base backup + archived WAL. A replica is not a backup. RPO = data loss, RTO = time

Architecture

  • Network call costs: partial failure, retries plus idempotency, distributed transactions, no debugger
  • Timeout = unknown outcome. Retry only idempotent calls, and send an idempotency key
  • 8 fallacies: reliable, zero latency, infinite bandwidth, secure, fixed topology, one admin, zero transport cost, homogeneous
  • Conway's Law: systems copy org communication. Microservices apply it in reverse
  • Monolith First: split only on measured pain (~50+ engineers, very different scaling)
  • Modular monolith = one deployable, enforced boundaries, ACID easy, no network tax
  • Modules talk via public APIs/events, never another module's tables
  • Boundary enforced in CI (Packwerk), not by comments
  • DDD: ubiquitous language, bounded context, context map
  • Five module tests: responsibility, language, rules, data, contract
  • A unique noun or a shared table does not make a module
  • Prime Video: per-transition billing + S3 relay, fixed by one process with in-memory transfer
  • Serverless + Postgres: one connection per invocation exhausts the limit. Use pooling

Architecture and hosting (T4L1, T4L2)

  • A network call is a function call that can fail halfway, silently, on someone else's clock. Its tax: partial failure, retries plus idempotency (one key per payment, reused on every retry), no distributed transactions, no local debugger
  • Fallacy traps: "transport cost is zero" = money (every GB across a region is billed). "Homogeneous" = hardware, kernels, MTUs. Latency (time) and bandwidth (pipe size) are separate fallacies
  • Conway (1967): systems copy the org's communication. Microservices = Conway in reverse, for team velocity, "not for scalability alone". Two-pizza teams = small, accountable, agile
  • Microservices tax: one request, a dozen logs; distributed transactions nearly impossible; one service down, three slow; huge orchestration overhead. All services calling all = distributed, tightly coupled monolith
  • Prime Video (2023): -90% cost by moving Step Functions (billed per state transition) + S3 relays to one process on EC2/ECS. Vogels: "a strategy, not a religion"
  • Shopify = modular monolith: 173B requests over Black Friday weekend, 284M req/min peak, 12 TB/min. Packwerk in CI fails the build. Contracts: placeOrder()/OrderPlaced, getKitchenStatus()/OrderReadyForPickup, assignRider()/DeliveryCompleted
  • Hosting spectrum: VM → container PaaS → managed containers → serverless. Choose by who operates what. "No column wins every row". PaaS = lowest to start; serverless = per invocation + duration, higher lock-in
  • Compute and database are separate decisions. Managed DB gives backups, patching, failover. Still yours: schema, access rules, credentials, restore tests
  • Demo A: -p 127.0.0.1:8000:8000 (not public), Caddy on 443 forwards inward, ufw allow 22,80,443/tcp, DNS A record before the TLS certificate. New env file = docker rm -f + docker run --env-file (restart keeps the old env)
  • Restart policy = supervisor for one host, not an orchestrator. Recovers: crashed process, host reboot. Not: lost VM, crash on every start (loop, no alert), cert/disk/patches, nobody told. docker stop by hand stays stopped
  • Ladder: systemd (one process) → Docker restart policy (one host) → Kubernetes/Swarm (many hosts, desired state) → managed platform. Four jobs: failure detection, scheduling, placement/startup, routing/service discovery. Desired − observed = actions
  • Liveness (/healthz, no DB) fails → restart. Readiness fails → stop routing, no restart (LBs use it). Startup = wait while booting. Scale out needs a load balancer + stateless servers. Trace: 2,300 of 2,319 ms in DB, "waiting for a free connection" (pool max_size=5 per instance)

Safe delivery and the database under load (T5, T6)

  • Time to recover = detect + decide + act. TiffinGo: 7 + 2 + 1 min (19:02 → 19:09 → 19:11 → 19:12). "Most outages are long because nobody noticed"
  • Pipeline order: Test → Build → Migrate → Deploy → Check health. Old code still serves during the migration, so every migration must work with the old code
  • CI runner: a fresh computer, starts empty, deleted at the end. A non-zero exit code stops the job. Secrets come from the repository's settings, never from the code
  • Build the image once, tag it with the commit id (tiffingo:3f9a2c1), and run that same image in staging and production. Rebuilding can give a different image
  • Three ways back: flag off = seconds (nothing deployed), previous image = ~1 min (nothing rebuilt), revert the commit = minutes (the pipeline runs again)
  • "Code is reversible. Data is not. Sent messages are forever." Redeploying 2.3 after the items → order_items rename: checkout fails on 2.3 too. Fix forward
  • Demo: /healthz only runs SELECT 1, so it says "ok" while checkout fails. rollback.sh never touches the database. A failed gate prints "Production is unchanged", but a migration that already ran stays
  • Percentage rollout: hash(user_id) % 100 < 10, not random() < 0.1. Demo: sha256(flag + ":" + user), first 4 bytes, % 100. Raising the % never turns anyone off. Put the flag name in the hash, or every flag hits the same users (978 overlap vs 99)
  • Flags: kill switch, percentage, targeted (Pune first). Reading on every request lands changes at once. A 30 s cache means up to 30 s delay. Every flag needs an owner and a removal date. At 100%, delete the flag and the old code path
  • Knight Capital, 1 Aug 2012: a reused flag name, 1 of 8 servers didn't get the new code, ~$440M in 45 min. Blue-green = switch all at once (old kept warm). Canary = 5% first, then grow only if healthy. In both, readiness checks decide which servers get traffic
  • N+1 = 1 query for the list + 1 per row. 50 rows = 51 queries. 51 × 30 ms ≈ 1.5 s in production, about 51 ms on a laptop. Fix: a JOIN or = ANY($1), so 1 or 2 trips
  • No index = Seq Scan (reads every page). B-tree = a handful of steps. EXPLAIN after the index: Index Scan + Index Cond. Index (a, b) helps a, or a + b, but not b alone. Every index slows every insert and update. Plain CREATE INDEX blocks writes. CONCURRENTLY doesn't, but can't run in a transaction
  • Each connection = one Postgres process. max_connections default 100. Connection 101 gets FATAL: sorry, too many clients already. Rule: servers × pool ≤ limit − spare (8 × 10 = 80 ≤ 90). Serverless: 200 × 5 = 1,000. A pooler in transaction mode loses prepared statements and session locks. Busy 50% = short queue, 90% = long, 99% = explodes
  • Last seat: both read 99/100, and BEGIN/COMMIT alone doesn't help. Fixes: UPDATE … WHERE booked < capacity RETURNING (0 rows → 409), SELECT … FOR UPDATE, and CHECK/UNIQUE as a safety net. SERIALIZABLE aborts one transaction, and you retry. Optimistic locking uses a version column
Read before the quiz

Quiz-day traps & method

The small things that cost whole questions, and a routine for any terminal task, even one you've never seen.

Method for any terminal task

The checker grades the final state: which branch you're on, where each branch points, how many commits there are, what is inside the files. It does not see your commands. So read the "What was checked" list first. That list is the real task.

A 6-step routine

  1. Read "What was checked" first. Turn each line into a goal, e.g. "main has 4 commits" or "branch X points at commit Y".
  2. Look before you act: pwd, ls, git status, git branch, git log --oneline --graph --all, docker ps -a.
  3. Predict the final state in one line, e.g. "after the merge, main = base + mine + theirs + merge commit = 4".
  4. One command at a time. Read each command's output before typing the next. Errors are usually explained in that output.
  5. Verify against the checks with the same look-commands as step 2. Count commits with git log --oneline | wc -l.
  6. Respect the "Stop" line. Don't push, delete or add commits that it forbids. An extra commit fails a commit-count check.

The traps (each one has cost real marks)

TrapWhat to do instead
docker build -t app:1.0Missing the build context. Always end with a folder: docker build -t app:1.0 .
docker run app:1.0 -p 8080:3000Anything after the image name becomes the container's command. Options go before the image: docker run -d -p 8080:3000 app:1.0
-p 3000:8080 backwardsIt's HOST:CONTAINER. The app's own port goes on the right.
git merge x then stuck in vimEsc :wq Enter. Better: git merge x --no-edit, or after resolving a conflict git commit --no-edit.
Resolved a conflict but commit failsYou must git add file after editing it. That's how git learns the conflict is resolved.
Conflict markers left inDelete every <<<<<<<, ======= and >>>>>>> line. Check with grep -n "<<<<" file.
Committed on the wrong branchCheck git branch before committing. Use git switch -c name to create a branch and move onto it.
git commit -am with a new file-a only includes files git already tracks. New files need git add first.
One extra commitgit reset --soft HEAD~1 undoes the last commit but keeps its changes staged.
> reports/x.txt: No such file or directoryThe folder must exist first: mkdir -p reports
Second > wiped the first result> replaces the file, >> adds to the end. Use > for the first write, then >>.
grep A f1 | grep B f2The second grep reads f2 and ignores the pipe. Give the file only to the first grep: grep A f1 | grep B
grep with 2 files adds file: prefixesUse grep -h, or grep one file at a time in the order the task asks.
mv before reading the fileDo the reading/grepping first, then move. Or use the file's new path.
Renamed a file with plain mv in a repoUse git mv old new, or mv then git add -A, so git records the rename.
Edited an already-applied migrationFlyway's checksum check fails. Never edit applied migrations; add a new one.
Ran migration SQL by handThe history table doesn't record it, so the runner will run it again and fail. Let the runner apply migrations.
Failing-test task: committed test + fix togetherTwo commits: git add test_file → commit, then git add code_file → commit.
git add . grabbed junkRunning pytest creates __pycache__/ and .pytest_cache/. Add files by name: git add test_validate.py.
git push pushed the wrong thingBe explicit: git push origin main.

Look-commands to type before and after

$ pwd ; ls -la                              # where am I, what's here
$ git status                               # branch + staged/unstaged/untracked
$ git branch -a                            # all branches, * = current
$ git log --oneline --graph --all --decorate   # the whole history picture
$ git log --oneline | wc -l                # how many commits on this branch
$ git show main:greet.py                   # a file's content as stored at a branch
$ docker ps -a ; docker images ; docker volume ls
$ flyway info                              # which migrations are applied / pending (run inside work/)
